# The Corporate English Playbook

### 3,200 phrases, ranked by how often you'll actually use them
#### A working handbook for data and analytics professionals in global organizations

---

**What this is**

Sixteen chapters. Each one gives you 200 phrases for a single situation, ordered from the ones you'll use this week down to the ones you'll use once a year. Every chapter also gives you the underlying sentence patterns, realistic dialogues, a blunt-to-natural conversion table, and drills you can do out loud in ten minutes.

**Who it's for**

Professionals whose English grammar is already fine but whose *register* isn't — people who are technically excellent and still get talked over, misread as blunt, or asked "sorry, what's the bottom line?"

**How to use it**

Read Chapter 1. Then never read this book cover to cover again. Open the chapter that matches tomorrow's calendar, drill Tier 1, and use two phrases in a real conversation within 48 hours.

## Part I — The System


---

# Chapter 1 — How to Use This Book

> **One-line promise:** By the end of this chapter you will have a repeatable system that turns phrases you read into phrases you say without thinking.

## 1.1 Why this chapter matters

- Your English is not the problem. Your **retrieval speed** is. You know the words; they don't arrive in the 1.5 seconds a live meeting gives you.
- Most people study business English by collecting phrases. Collecting is step one of four, and it is the least valuable one.
- A phrase you have read 50 times but never said aloud is not in your vocabulary. It is in your library.
- Fluency at senior level is not about vocabulary size. It's about having a *ready-made move* for each recurring situation: disagreeing, buying time, closing a point, saying you don't know.
- This book is built as a lookup system, not a novel. You are not meant to read it front to back.

## 1.2 The four-step system

Every phrase in this book is meant to travel through four steps. Skipping a step is why previous attempts didn't stick.

### Step 1 — Collect (situation first, phrase second)

Don't collect phrases. Collect **situations**, then attach a phrase to each.

| Situation | Natural phrase |
|---|---|
| Need more investigation | "I'd like to dig a little deeper into this." |
| Disagree politely | "I'm not entirely convinced that's the best approach." |
| Make a recommendation | "My recommendation would be to..." |
| Raise a concern | "I see a couple of concerns with this approach." |
| Ask someone to explain | "Could you walk me through your thinking?" |
| Buy thinking time | "Let me think about that for a moment." |
| Don't know the answer | "I'm not sure yet. Let me look into it." |
| Agree partially | "I agree with you to an extent, but..." |
| Clarify | "Just to make sure we're aligned..." |
| Summarize | "So, if I understand correctly..." |

The situation is the index key. In a live meeting your brain searches by situation ("I need to push back"), never by phrase.

### Step 2 — Understand the pattern, not the sentence

Memorizing this as one block is fragile:

> "I see a couple of concerns with this approach."

Learn the **formula** instead:

`I see + [number] + concern(s) with + [thing]`

Then you can generate on demand:

- I see a couple of concerns with the timeline.
- I see a few concerns with the proposed architecture.
- I see one potential concern with the data quality.

One pattern gives you fifty sentences. Fifty memorized sentences give you fifty sentences. Every chapter has a Pattern Breakdown section for exactly this reason.

### Step 3 — Speak it (this is where most people quit)

Reading is input. Speaking is installation.

Take one anchor phrase:

> "I'm not entirely convinced that's the best approach."

Say it aloud ten times. Then extend it, out loud, into different second halves:

- "...I'd prefer to test it with a smaller sample first."
- "...I think we should consider the cost implications."
- "...could we look at another option?"

When the second half changes freely and the first half stays automatic, the phrase is yours.

**The 10× rep rule:** ten spoken repetitions beat a hundred silent readings. Ten minutes a day, out loud, beats two hours of reading on a Sunday.

### Step 4 — Use it under pressure

Rehearsal without pressure doesn't transfer. Create pressure deliberately.

Open a voice conversation with an AI and give it a role. Example prompt:

```
Act as my American engineering manager. We're discussing a machine-learning
project and I need to disagree with your proposal professionally.

Rules:
- Stay in character the whole time. Push back on me at least twice.
- Do NOT correct my English while we're talking.
- After the conversation ends, list every unnatural or non-native phrase I used,
  and give me 5 better corporate alternatives for each.
```

Then actually have the conversation. Fifteen minutes of this is worth more than studying a hundred phrases.

Every chapter ends with a roleplay prompt built for that chapter's situation, with a different counterpart each time.

## 1.3 How this book is organized

Each chapter follows the same seven-part structure, so you always know where to look.

| Section | What it gives you |
|---|---|
| X.1 Why this chapter matters | The specific failure mode you're fixing |
| X.2 The Frequency Ladder | 200 phrases, ranked most-used → least-used, in four tiers |
| X.3 Pattern Breakdown | 12–15 formulas that generate hundreds of sentences |
| X.4 Scenarios | Realistic dialogues with phrase numbers bracketed |
| X.5 Avoid This → Say This | 25 upgrades from blunt/non-native to natural |
| X.6 Practice Drills | Speaking reps, slot swaps, cold opens, AI roleplay |
| X.7 Master these 10 first | The highest-leverage phrases in the chapter |

**The tiers in every ladder:**

| Tier | Phrases | Frequency | What to do with them |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tier 1 | 1–60 | You'll hear or need these weekly | Drill until automatic |
| Tier 2 | 61–120 | Monthly | Recognize now, drill later |
| Tier 3 | 121–170 | Situational | Read once, look up when needed |
| Tier 4 | 171–200 | Rare but high-impact | Keep in reserve for the big moment |

Tier 1 of any chapter is 80% of the value. If you only ever drill Tier 1 across all sixteen chapters, you will have covered the language of a full corporate career.

**Register labels** in the tables tell you where a phrase is safe:

| Label | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Neutral | Safe almost anywhere |
| Formal | Senior audiences, written records, external parties |
| Casual | Peers, your own team, Slack |
| US | Reads naturally to American colleagues; may sound over-enthusiastic in Europe |
| UK/EU | Reads naturally in Europe; may sound stiff or cold in the US |
| Written | Better on the page than out loud |
| High-stakes | Reserve for moments that matter; overuse destroys the effect |

## 1.4 Which chapter to open

| If your problem is... | Go to |
|---|---|
| I go quiet in meetings | Ch. 3 Meetings, Ch. 15 Confidence |
| I sound blunt or rude without meaning to | Ch. 10 Disagreement, Ch. 6 US, Ch. 7 European |
| Senior people lose interest when I talk | Ch. 13 Executive Communication |
| I can't explain my model to non-technical people | Ch. 8 Explaining ML and AI |
| My numbers get questioned and I fumble | Ch. 9 Explaining Data |
| I agree to deadlines I can't meet | Ch. 11 Negotiation |
| Interviews go badly despite strong skills | Ch. 5 Interviews |
| The first five minutes of any call are painful | Ch. 14 Small Talk |
| I don't understand what my US colleagues actually mean | Ch. 6 US (§6.8), Ch. 16 Idioms |
| I sound like a textbook | Ch. 17 Phrasal Verbs, Ch. 16 Idioms |
| I lead people but sound like a peer | Ch. 12 Leadership |
| I present well but can't handle Q&A | Ch. 4 Presentations |
| I don't know the everyday moves at all | Ch. 2 Core Categories — start here |

## 1.5 The 30-day plan

Ten minutes a day. Out loud. Not silent reading.

| Days | Focus | Daily action |
|---|---|---|
| 1–5 | Ch. 2 Tier 1 | 12 phrases/day, 10 reps each, aloud |
| 6–8 | Ch. 3 Meetings Tier 1 | Use 2 new phrases in a real meeting each day |
| 9–11 | Ch. 15 Confidence §15.3 | Strip one hedge from your speech per day |
| 12–14 | Ch. 10 Disagreement Tier 1 | One AI roleplay, disagreeing |
| 15–17 | Ch. 9 Explaining Data | Narrate one real chart aloud daily |
| 18–20 | Ch. 13 Executive Comm. | Rewrite one update as headline + so-what + ask |
| 21–23 | Ch. 17 Phrasal Verbs Tier 1 | Replace one stiff verb per email |
| 24–26 | Ch. 14 Small Talk | Two real small-talk openers per day |
| 27–28 | Ch. 6 or Ch. 7 (whichever you work with) | Decode-table drill |
| 29–30 | Review | Re-drill every chapter's §X.7 list |

After 30 days, rotate: pick whichever chapter matches next week's calendar.

## 1.6 Five rules that decide whether this works

1. **Out loud or it doesn't count.** Silent reading builds recognition, not production.
2. **One situation at a time.** Don't drill twenty phrases for one situation; drill one phrase for twenty situations.
3. **Use it within 48 hours.** A phrase not used in a real conversation within two days is usually lost.
4. **Steal from your colleagues.** When a native speaker says something that lands well, write it down that day. This book is a starting library, not a closed one.
5. **Don't perform.** The goal is not to sound impressive. It's to sound like someone whose ideas are easy to agree with.

## 1.7 A note on what not to do

- Don't drop idioms into a serious conversation to prove fluency. Chapter 16 marks the ones that now make people wince.
- Don't switch register mid-sentence ("Hey folks, kindly find the aforementioned analysis attached herewith").
- Don't over-apologize. Chapter 15 exists because "sorry" is the single most common credibility leak for non-native senior professionals.
- Don't translate directly from your first language's politeness system. English politeness is built on *hedging*, not on formality — "Could we possibly look at..." is polite; "Kindly do the needful" is not.

# Chapter 2 — The 12 Core Categories: Your Everyday Toolkit

> **One-line promise:** By the end of this chapter you'll have 200 ready-to-speak phrases sorted into the 12 situations that make up roughly 90 percent of your working week — so you stop translating in your head and start choosing a line.

## 2.1 Why this chapter matters

- Your English isn't broken; your **inventory** is thin. You have one way to disagree, one way to buy time, one way to say "I don't know" — so under pressure you reuse it until it sounds abrupt.
- The specific failure mode: you swing between **too blunt** ("No, that is wrong", "Why did you do like this?") and **too bookish** ("I would like to humbly submit that the aforementioned approach may be revisited"). Native-level professionals live in the narrow band between those two, and that band is made of about 200 phrases.
- Non-native speakers over-invest in vocabulary and under-invest in **function**. Nobody in a leadership review notices your word choice; they notice whether you softened a disagreement, named a risk, or bought yourself four seconds before answering.
- Organized by function, phrases become retrievable. You will not think "what's the word for…" — you'll think "this is a *clarifying* moment" and pull line 3.
- What changes when you fix it: you interrupt without offending, you disagree without escalating, and you stop saying "yes" to timelines you can't hit because you had no polite way to stall.
- The categories below are ordered by how often you'll actually need them in a week at a data/analytics job — not alphabetically, and not by how impressive they sound.

## 2.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

Twelve categories, ordered most-needed to least-needed. Numbering runs 1–200 continuously. Categories 1–5 are your daily driver; by category 12 you're in phrases you may use twice a year and be very glad you had.

### Category 1 — Clarifying and probing (phrases 1–22)

*The trap: non-native speakers ask "Why?" bare, which lands like an accusation — native speakers wrap the same question in "help me understand" or "walk me through".*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | So, if I understand correctly... | Restating before you commit to a task | Neutral |
| 2 | Just so I'm clear, ... | Pinning down one specific detail | Neutral |
| 3 | Could you walk me through your thinking? | You want reasoning, not conclusion | Neutral |
| 4 | I'd like to dig a little deeper into this. | Signaling you're not done with a topic | Neutral |
| 5 | What's driving that number? | A metric moved and you want the cause | Neutral |
| 6 | Say more about that. | Someone stopped short of the point | Casual |
| 7 | Help me understand the constraint here. | Probing a "we can't" without confrontation | Neutral |
| 8 | When you say "active users," what exactly counts? | Definitions differ across teams | Neutral |
| 9 | Is that a hard deadline or a target? | Before you plan a sprint around it | Neutral |
| 10 | Where is that number coming from? | Two reports disagree | Neutral |
| 11 | What would "done" look like here? | Vague request, no acceptance criteria | Neutral |
| 12 | Can we back up a step? | Discussion jumped ahead of you | Neutral |
| 13 | What's the decision you're trying to make? | Someone asks for data with no context | Neutral |
| 14 | Who's the audience for this? | Scoping a dashboard or deck | Neutral |
| 15 | Let me play that back to you. | Confirming a long or messy request | Neutral |
| 16 | What am I missing? | You suspect a hidden constraint | Casual |
| 17 | Are we solving the right problem? | The team is deep in the wrong fix | High-stakes |
| 18 | Is that an assumption or is it measured? | Someone states a number confidently | Neutral |
| 19 | How are you defining that metric? | Before you replicate someone's figure | Neutral |
| 20 | What happens if we don't do this? | Testing whether a request is real | Neutral |
| 21 | Just to close the loop on that question... | Returning to an unanswered point | Neutral |
| 22 | Which part is the blocker — the data or the sign-off? | Diagnosing a stalled deliverable | Neutral |

### Category 2 — Agreeing and aligning (phrases 23–42)

*The trap: "Yes, yes, correct" repeated three times reads as anxious rather than agreeable — one clean agreement is stronger than three.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 23 | Just to make sure we're aligned... | Before a hand-off or a commitment | Neutral |
| 24 | That works for me. | Approving a plan or a time | Neutral |
| 25 | Agreed — let's go with that. | Closing a decision quickly | Neutral |
| 26 | I'm with you on that. | Backing a colleague in a group | Casual |
| 27 | That's a fair point. | Conceding part of an argument | Neutral |
| 28 | I agree with you to an extent, but... | Partial agreement before a caveat | Neutral |
| 29 | Makes sense. | Light acknowledgement, moving on | Casual |
| 30 | We're on the same page. | Confirming shared understanding | Neutral |
| 31 | I'd back that. | Supporting a proposal in a review | UK/EU |
| 32 | No objections from my side. | Asked to approve something minor | Neutral |
| 33 | Happy to go with the group on this. | Low-stakes decision, you don't care | Neutral |
| 34 | That's consistent with what I'm seeing in the data. | Independent confirmation adds weight | Neutral |
| 35 | You've read it the same way I have. | Validating someone's interpretation | Neutral |
| 36 | I think we're saying the same thing differently. | Defusing a fake disagreement | Neutral |
| 37 | Let's lock that in. | Ending circular discussion | US |
| 38 | Fully on board. | Enthusiastic sign-on | Casual |
| 39 | Directionally, yes. | Agreeing on shape, not specifics | Neutral |
| 40 | I'll take that as our working assumption. | Proceeding without full certainty | Neutral |
| 41 | Same conclusion, different route. | Two analyses converge | Casual |
| 42 | Then we're aligned — I'll write it up. | Converting agreement into a record | Written |

### Category 3 — Buying thinking time (phrases 43–60)

*The trap: filling the gap with "actually, basically, see" instead of a clean pause — native speakers buy time with a full sentence, not filler syllables.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 43 | Let me think about that for a moment. | Unexpected question in a meeting | Neutral |
| 44 | Good question — give me a second. | You need three seconds, not three days | Neutral |
| 45 | Let me make sure I answer that properly. | Signaling care, not evasion | Neutral |
| 46 | That's worth pausing on. | Flagging a question as substantial | Neutral |
| 47 | Two things come to mind. | Structuring an answer as you speak | Neutral |
| 48 | Let me answer that in two parts. | Complex question, split it | Neutral |
| 49 | Before I answer, can I check one thing? | You need a fact first | Neutral |
| 50 | I want to give you a real answer, not a guess. | Declining to improvise on numbers | Neutral |
| 51 | Can I come back to you on that by end of day? | Deferring with a deadline attached | Neutral |
| 52 | Let me restate the question to be sure. | Buys time and confirms scope at once | Formal |
| 53 | Off the top of my head... | Giving a rough answer with cover | Casual |
| 54 | I'll need to look at the numbers before I commit. | Pressure to promise a timeline | Neutral |
| 55 | Let's park that and come back to it. | Side topic derailing the agenda | UK/EU |
| 56 | Hold that thought — I want to finish this point. | Someone interrupts mid-explanation | Neutral |
| 57 | There's a short answer and a long answer. | Letting the listener choose depth | Neutral |
| 58 | Give me until tomorrow morning and I'll have it. | Converting stall into commitment | Neutral |
| 59 | I'd rather check than guess on that one. | Protecting your credibility | Neutral |
| 60 | Let me pull that up. | Buying seconds while you open a file | Casual |

### Category 4 — Running and surviving meetings (phrases 61–80)

*The trap: waiting for a polite gap that never comes — in US and European meetings you must claim the floor with a phrase, not wait to be invited.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 61 | Let's get started — I'll keep this to 30 minutes. | Opening a meeting you own | Neutral |
| 62 | Quick agenda check before we dive in. | Aligning on purpose in 20 seconds | Neutral |
| 63 | Can I jump in here? | Claiming the floor politely | Neutral |
| 64 | Sorry, I think you cut out — could you repeat that? | Bad audio on a call | Neutral |
| 65 | Building on what Priya said... | Adding without contradicting | Neutral |
| 66 | I want to make sure we get to the decision today. | Meeting is drifting | Neutral |
| 67 | We're going down a rabbit hole — can we refocus? | Deep tangent, low value | Casual |
| 68 | Let's take that offline. | Two people only; not for the room | US |
| 69 | I'll note that as an action item for me. | Taking visible ownership | Neutral |
| 70 | Who owns this after today? | Preventing an orphaned task | Neutral |
| 71 | We have five minutes left — let me summarize. | Landing the plane on time | Neutral |
| 72 | Can we timebox this to ten minutes? | Bounding an open-ended item | Neutral |
| 73 | I didn't get a chance to finish my point. | You were talked over | Neutral |
| 74 | Just to recap what we've decided... | Closing loop before people leave | Neutral |
| 75 | I'd like to hear from the reporting side on this. | Pulling in a silent participant | Neutral |
| 76 | Are we good to move on? | Checking consent before advancing | Casual |
| 77 | Let me share my screen. | Switching from talk to evidence | Neutral |
| 78 | I'll follow up in writing so nothing gets lost. | Verbal agreement needs a paper trail | Written |
| 79 | If there are no objections, that's our decision. | Forcing closure in a leadership review | High-stakes |
| 80 | Thanks all — I'll send notes within the hour. | Ending with a commitment | Neutral |

### Category 5 — Presenting data and results (phrases 81–100)

*The trap: building up to the finding like a detective novel — corporate audiences want the answer in sentence one and the method in sentence four.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 81 | Here's the headline. | Opening any data update | Neutral |
| 82 | The short version is... | Compressing for a busy listener | Neutral |
| 83 | Three things stand out. | Structuring findings on the fly | Neutral |
| 84 | Enrollment is up 12 percent quarter over quarter. | Stating a clean, sourced number | Neutral |
| 85 | Let me start with what changed. | Recurring report, only deltas matter | Neutral |
| 86 | The trend is clear, the cause isn't. | Honest about what you can prove | Neutral |
| 87 | That's within normal variance. | Killing a false alarm | Formal |
| 88 | Caveat: this is based on partial data through Friday. | Numbers aren't final yet | Neutral |
| 89 | Two sources disagree, and here's why. | Reconciliation question you anticipated | Neutral |
| 90 | If you only remember one number, remember this one. | Executive audience, low attention | High-stakes |
| 91 | Compared with last cycle, we're flat. | Framing against a baseline | Neutral |
| 92 | The sample is small, so read it as a signal, not proof. | Preventing over-interpretation | Neutral |
| 93 | Let me walk you through the dashboard top to bottom. | First-time audience for a view | Neutral |
| 94 | Numbers first, then interpretation. | Setting expectations for your talk | Neutral |
| 95 | What you're seeing is a data-quality artifact, not a drop. | Explaining an alarming chart | Neutral |
| 96 | The model is doing better on recall than on precision. | Reporting ML performance honestly | Neutral |
| 97 | This holds across all three programs. | Showing a finding generalizes | Neutral |
| 98 | I've excluded test records from this view. | Documenting a filter choice | Written |
| 99 | That spike is a reporting lag, not real demand. | Correcting a misread chart | Neutral |
| 100 | Bottom line: we're on track, with one risk. | Closing a status update | High-stakes |

### Category 6 — Making recommendations (phrases 101–118)

*The trap: presenting options and stopping — leadership reads "here are three choices" with no recommendation as an analyst who won't take a position.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 101 | My recommendation would be to... | Standard, safe, works everywhere | Neutral |
| 102 | If it were my call, I'd... | Advising above your authority | Neutral |
| 103 | I'd suggest we start with the smaller pilot. | Reducing risk on a big ask | Neutral |
| 104 | The cleanest option is... | Several messy paths, one tidy one | Neutral |
| 105 | There are three options; I'd take the second. | Showing range and still choosing | Neutral |
| 106 | I'd hold off until we see next month's data. | Recommending patience | Neutral |
| 107 | What I'd propose is a two-week spike, then decide. | Unknowns block the real decision | Neutral |
| 108 | Given the timeline, I'd cut scope rather than quality. | Deadline pressure forces a trade | Neutral |
| 109 | My strong preference is to fix the pipeline first. | You need to be heard clearly | High-stakes |
| 110 | I'd rather do one thing properly than three halfway. | Pushing back on scope creep | Neutral |
| 111 | Worth considering: buy instead of build. | Introducing an unpopular option | Neutral |
| 112 | The pragmatic answer is to ship the manual report now. | Perfect solution is too slow | Neutral |
| 113 | I'd frame it as a phased rollout. | Making a large change palatable | Formal |
| 114 | If we have to choose, I'd protect the leadership dashboard. | Forced prioritization | Neutral |
| 115 | Let me put a recommendation on the table. | Breaking a stalemate | Neutral |
| 116 | I'd go with option B, and here's the trade-off. | Owning the downside of your pick | Neutral |
| 117 | My advice would be to bring the vendor in early. | Recommending on process, not tech | Neutral |
| 118 | Recommend we revisit this after the audit closes. | Written status note or email | Written |

### Category 7 — Disagreeing softly (phrases 119–136)

*The trap: "I don't agree" plus silence — native speakers always attach a reason or a question, because bare disagreement forces the other person to defend rather than think.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 119 | I'm not entirely convinced that's the best approach. | Core disagreement, minimal friction | Neutral |
| 120 | I see a couple of concerns with this approach. | Opening a structured objection | Neutral |
| 121 | I'd push back gently on that. | Signaling dissent before giving it | Neutral |
| 122 | Can I offer a different read? | Reframing the same evidence | Neutral |
| 123 | I hear you, but the data suggests otherwise. | Opinion versus measurement | Neutral |
| 124 | That's one way to look at it. | Buying room to offer another | Neutral |
| 125 | I'm hesitant, and here's why. | Naming discomfort with a reason | Neutral |
| 126 | Where I'd differ is on the timeline. | Narrowing disagreement to one axis | Neutral |
| 127 | Help me understand why we'd do it that way. | Disagreeing in the form of a question | Neutral |
| 128 | I'm not sure that holds when volume triples. | Attacking scalability, not the person | Neutral |
| 129 | My worry is what it costs us later. | Raising long-term cost | Neutral |
| 130 | Respectfully, I think we're underestimating the effort. | Disagreeing with a senior person | Formal |
| 131 | I'd challenge the assumption behind that. | Foundation is wrong, not the math | High-stakes |
| 132 | That solves the symptom, not the cause. | Rejecting a quick fix | Neutral |
| 133 | Can we stress-test that for a minute? | Inviting the room to poke holes | Neutral |
| 134 | I'll go along with it, but I want my concern on record. | Outvoted, still accountable | High-stakes |
| 135 | I'd be more comfortable if we validated the source first. | Conditional agreement | Formal |
| 136 | Devil's advocate for a second: what if adoption is low? | Testing optimism without blame | Casual |

### Category 8 — Expressing uncertainty (phrases 137–152)

*The trap: treating "I don't know" as failure and inventing a number instead — one invented figure costs more credibility than ten honest "let me check" answers.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 137 | I'm not sure yet. Let me look into it. | Cleanest honest answer there is | Neutral |
| 138 | My best estimate is around three weeks. | Estimate flagged as an estimate | Neutral |
| 139 | I'd put that at maybe 60 percent confidence. | Quantifying your own doubt | Neutral |
| 140 | I don't want to guess on that one. | Refusing bait for a fast number | Neutral |
| 141 | That's outside what I can verify today. | Question beyond your data access | Formal |
| 142 | I have a hypothesis, not an answer. | Sharing early thinking safely | Neutral |
| 143 | Early signs suggest yes, but it's too soon. | Partial results, pressure to conclude | Neutral |
| 144 | I'll caveat this heavily. | Prefacing shaky numbers | Neutral |
| 145 | Two unknowns are driving the range. | Explaining why the estimate is wide | Neutral |
| 146 | Honestly, I don't know — I'll find out who does. | Ignorance plus a next step | Neutral |
| 147 | Treat this as a rough order of magnitude. | Ballpark sizing for planning | Formal |
| 148 | I'd rather give you a range than a false number. | Someone demands a single figure | Neutral |
| 149 | Ask me again after the data load finishes. | Answer genuinely depends on timing | Casual |
| 150 | There's a chance I'm wrong about this. | Pre-empting overconfidence | Neutral |
| 151 | We're guessing on the vendor side, and that's the risk. | Uncertainty owned by someone else | Neutral |
| 152 | I don't have visibility into that part yet. | Question about another team's scope | Neutral |

### Category 9 — Explaining technical concepts simply (phrases 153–166)

*The trap: reaching for more precision under pressure — non-experts hear added jargon as evasion, so the fix is a shorter sentence, not a more accurate one.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 153 | Think of it as a filter, not a calculation. | Correcting a mental model | Neutral |
| 154 | Skipping the technical detail for a second... | Signaling deliberate simplification | Neutral |
| 155 | In plain terms, the report was reading yesterday's data. | Translating a refresh failure | Neutral |
| 156 | The analogy I'd use is a queue at a counter. | Explaining throughput or backlog | Neutral |
| 157 | It's the difference between "no data" and "zero." | Explaining nulls to a business user | Neutral |
| 158 | Two systems are counting the same student twice. | Explaining a join problem | Neutral |
| 159 | Do you want the mechanism or just the impact? | Calibrating depth before you start | Neutral |
| 160 | At a high level, there are three steps. | Structuring an explanation | Neutral |
| 161 | The model is guessing, and we're grading the guess. | Introducing ML evaluation | Neutral |
| 162 | Nothing broke — the definition changed. | Numbers moved for non-technical reasons | Neutral |
| 163 | It's slow because it's rebuilding everything each night. | Explaining a full-load job | Neutral |
| 164 | Same numbers, different cut-off date. | Two reports, apparent mismatch | Neutral |
| 165 | I'll spare you the SQL and give you the outcome. | Audience doesn't want the query | Casual |
| 166 | Roughly speaking, it halves the refresh time. | Quantifying benefit without detail | Neutral |

### Category 10 — Stakeholder management (phrases 167–180)

*The trap: saying yes to every request to seem cooperative — a native-level professional accepts work by naming what it displaces.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 167 | Let me make sure the right people see this first. | Before something goes wide | Neutral |
| 168 | Whose sign-off do we need? | Clarifying the approval path | Neutral |
| 169 | I'd like to keep you in the loop on this one. | Pre-empting a surprise | Neutral |
| 170 | Before I commit my team, let me check capacity. | New request, unclear bandwidth | Neutral |
| 171 | That's a reasonable ask — it just moves something else. | Trading, not refusing | Neutral |
| 172 | Can we agree on what "urgent" means here? | Everything is labeled urgent | Neutral |
| 173 | I'll flag the risk now so it's not a surprise later. | Early warning, on record | Neutral |
| 174 | Happy to take this on if we can drop the weekly refresh. | Explicit swap for new work | Neutral |
| 175 | Let me manage expectations on the timeline. | Enthusiasm outpacing reality | Formal |
| 176 | I want to set this up so you don't have to chase me. | Building trust with a stakeholder | Neutral |
| 177 | Who else has a stake in this decision? | Finding hidden stakeholders | Neutral |
| 178 | I'll send a short update Friday, same format each week. | Establishing a reporting rhythm | Written |
| 179 | Let's socialize the draft before the committee meeting. | Testing an idea privately first | US |
| 180 | I'd rather over-communicate here than surprise you. | High-visibility project | Neutral |

### Category 11 — Executive communication (phrases 181–192)

*The trap: giving leadership the full journey — context, method, caveats, then the answer. Reverse it: answer, ask, one risk, stop.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 181 | Here's the ask. | Cutting to what you need | High-stakes |
| 182 | One slide, three numbers, one decision. | Framing a leadership review | High-stakes |
| 183 | Net-net, we're two weeks behind but the risk is contained. | Compressed status for a director | US |
| 184 | I need one decision from you today. | Meeting with a busy executive | High-stakes |
| 185 | If you do nothing, here's what happens. | Making inaction visible | High-stakes |
| 186 | The trade-off is speed versus accuracy — you pick. | Escalating a genuine choice | High-stakes |
| 187 | I'll give you the answer first, then the reasoning. | Setting an executive-friendly order | Neutral |
| 188 | This is a five-minute conversation, not a meeting. | Requesting time efficiently | Neutral |
| 189 | I'm not asking for budget — I'm asking for air cover. | You need political support | High-stakes |
| 190 | Two risks worth your attention; one needs you. | Filtering upward | High-stakes |
| 191 | Everything else is on track; this is the exception. | Exception-based reporting | High-stakes |
| 192 | I'll come back to you only if something changes. | Closing a check-in cleanly | Neutral |

### Category 12 — Difficult conversations (phrases 193–200)

*The trap: softening so heavily that the message disappears — the other person leaves the room without knowing anything was wrong.*

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 193 | Can we talk about how last week went? | Opening a feedback conversation | Neutral |
| 194 | I want to raise something directly rather than let it sit. | Naming the conversation's purpose | High-stakes |
| 195 | This is feedback, not a complaint. | Lowering defensiveness early | Neutral |
| 196 | Here's the impact it had on the team's timeline. | Making it about effect, not character | High-stakes |
| 197 | I may be reading this wrong — tell me if I am. | Leaving room to be corrected | Neutral |
| 198 | I need to be honest about where we are. | Bad news to a stakeholder | High-stakes |
| 199 | Going forward, what would you like me to do differently? | Receiving criticism gracefully | Formal |
| 200 | Let's agree on what changes, and check in in two weeks. | Closing with accountability | High-stakes |

## 2.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `Just to {verb}, {statement/question}`
- Just to make sure we're aligned, the deadline is the 14th, not the 24th.
- Just to confirm, you want headcount by program, not by cohort?
- Just to flag it early, the vendor hasn't sent the schema yet.
*Watch out:* Use "just to" + bare verb, never "just for confirm" or "just to confirming".

**Pattern 2:** `Could you walk me through {your thinking / the logic / how you got there}?`
- Could you walk me through how you calculated retention?
- Could you walk me through your thinking on excluding the pilot cohort?
- Could you walk me through the approval path for this?
*Watch out:* "Walk me through" takes a noun or a clause with "how", not "walk me through that why".

**Pattern 3:** `I'd like to {verb} {a little / a bit} {deeper / further} into {topic}`
- I'd like to dig a little deeper into the drop-off between weeks three and four.
- I'd like to look a bit further into why two dashboards disagree.
- I'd like to go a little deeper into the model's false positives.
*Watch out:* "Dig deeper into", not "dig deeper on" and not "dig more deep".

**Pattern 4:** `My recommendation would be to {verb phrase}`
- My recommendation would be to freeze the schema until the audit closes.
- My recommendation would be to pilot with one program before scaling.
- My recommendation would be to hire a contractor rather than delay the release.
*Watch out:* "Would be to" plus a bare verb. Not "my recommendation would be that we should to…".

**Pattern 5:** `I'm not entirely convinced {clause}`
- I'm not entirely convinced that's the best approach.
- I'm not entirely convinced the data supports that conclusion.
- I'm not entirely convinced we need a new tool for this.
*Watch out:* "Entirely" softens; "not convinced at all" hardens sharply. Choose deliberately.

**Pattern 6:** `I see {a couple of / one or two / some} {concerns / risks / gaps} with {X}`
- I see a couple of concerns with this approach.
- I see one or two gaps in how we're counting withdrawals.
- I see some risk in going live before the reconciliation runs.
*Watch out:* "With this approach", not "in this approach" when you mean the plan overall.

**Pattern 7:** `Let me {verb} {for a moment / for a second}`
- Let me think about that for a moment.
- Let me pull up the numbers for a second.
- Let me check that with the registry team for a moment.
*Watch out:* "For a moment" is neutral; "for a sec" is casual and fine on internal calls, not in a board review.

**Pattern 8:** `So, if I understand correctly, {restatement}`
- So, if I understand correctly, you want the dashboard rebuilt, not just refreshed.
- So, if I understand correctly, the deadline moves only if finance signs off.
- So, if I understand correctly, we're measuring applications, not admits.
*Watch out:* Use the present tense in the restatement even if they spoke in the past.

**Pattern 9:** `I agree with you {to an extent / up to a point}, but {qualification}`
- I agree with you to an extent, but the effort estimate looks optimistic.
- I agree with you up to a point, but that assumes clean source data.
- I agree with you to an extent, but I'd still want a fallback report.
*Watch out:* "To an extent", not "to some extent only" — the extra word makes it grudging.

**Pattern 10:** `{The headline / The short version / Bottom line} is {finding}`
- The headline is that applications are up but conversions are flat.
- The short version is the pipeline failed twice and self-recovered.
- Bottom line: we can hit the date if we drop the mobile view.
*Watch out:* "Bottom line" takes a colon or "is that". Avoid "bottom line is like…".

**Pattern 11:** `I'm not sure yet. Let me {verb} and {come back / confirm}`
- I'm not sure yet. Let me look into it and come back to you tomorrow.
- I'm not sure yet. Let me check the logs and confirm by noon.
- I'm not sure yet. Let me ask the vendor and revert with a date.
*Watch out:* In global corporate English, "I'll revert" reads as Indian English. Say "I'll get back to you".

**Pattern 12:** `Where I'd {differ / land differently} is on {specific axis}`
- Where I'd differ is on the timeline, not the approach.
- Where I'd land differently is on which program we pilot in.
- Where I'd differ is the definition of an active applicant.
*Watch out:* Keep the disagreement to one axis; naming three makes it sound like total rejection.

**Pattern 13:** `Think of it as {simple analogy}, not {the technical thing}`
- Think of it as a filter, not a calculation.
- Think of it as a queue, not a crowd.
- Think of it as a photo of Friday, not a live feed.
*Watch out:* One analogy per explanation. Stacking two analogies confuses more than jargon does.

**Pattern 14:** `Happy to {take this on / help}, if we can {trade-off}`
- Happy to take this on, if we can push the weekly refresh to Monday.
- Happy to help, if we can drop the cohort breakdown for now.
- Happy to own it, if we can add one analyst for two weeks.
*Watch out:* "Happy to" plus bare verb. Not "happy to taking" and not "happy for take".

**Pattern 15:** `{Caveat / Worth flagging}: {limitation of the data}`
- Caveat: this is based on partial data through Friday.
- Worth flagging: the June figures haven't been reconciled yet.
- Caveat: three programs are missing from the feed.
*Watch out:* Put the caveat before the number, not after. A caveat after the fact sounds like backpedaling.

## 2.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 2.1 — A dashboard number is being questioned in a leadership review

**Setting:** Monthly program review. The Associate Dean has spotted an enrollment figure that doesn't match her own notes.

**Associate Dean:** This says 412 enrollments. My note from last week says 389. Which one is right?

**You:** Both are, actually — same numbers, different cut-off date. [#164] Let me start with what changed. [#85]

**Associate Dean:** Go ahead.

**You:** Your figure was as of the 8th. This view refreshes nightly, so it includes late confirmations through yesterday. Caveat: this is based on partial data through Friday for the executive programs. [#88]

**Associate Dean:** So the number will move again.

**You:** It will, slightly. If you only remember one number, remember 412 — that's the one we'll report externally. [#90]

**Faculty Director:** Can we not lock the number so it stops changing?

**You:** That's a fair point. [#27] I'd push back gently on that, though. [#121] Freezing it hides late confirmations, and last cycle those were about four percent of the total. What I'd propose is a two-week spike to build a snapshot view alongside the live one, then decide. [#107]

**Associate Dean:** Fine. What do you need?

**You:** Here's the ask. [#181] Two weeks of one analyst's time, and a decision from you on which number is official for reporting.

**Associate Dean:** Take it. Use the live one for internal, snapshot for external.

**You:** Just to recap what we've decided... [#74] live for internal, snapshot for external, and I'll follow up in writing so nothing gets lost. [#78]

**Why this worked:**
- You resolved the conflict as a definition issue, not a mistake — no defensiveness, no blame on the source system.
- The caveat came *before* the number, so the later movement wasn't a surprise.
- The disagreement with the Faculty Director was softened, evidenced, and immediately paired with a proposal.

### Scenario 2.2 — A vendor is overpromising on an integration timeline

**Setting:** Video call with a US-based vendor's solution consultant. They've just claimed a four-week delivery.

**Vendor:** Four weeks end to end. We've done this integration dozens of times.

**You:** Good to hear. Just so I'm clear, [#2] does four weeks include the data mapping, or does the clock start after we hand over the schema?

**Vendor:** After handover, typically.

**You:** Right. So, if I understand correctly, [#1] our side owes you a mapped schema before week one begins.

**Vendor:** Correct.

**You:** Could you walk me through your thinking on the mapping effort? [#3] Our student records sit in two systems that don't share a key.

**Vendor:** We'd normally handle that in the transform layer.

**You:** I'm not sure that holds when volume triples during admissions season. [#128] My worry is what it costs us later. [#129]

**Vendor:** We can look at that.

**You:** Let me manage expectations on the timeline. [#175] I'm not going to take four weeks to my leadership until we've agreed what's in scope. I'd suggest we start with the smaller pilot [#103] — one program, one term, real data.

**Vendor:** We could do a pilot in two weeks.

**You:** That works for me. [#24] I'll flag the risk now so it's not a surprise later [#173] — if the pilot shows key-matching problems, the four-week number changes and I'll need a revised plan, not a reassurance.

**Vendor:** Understood.

**Why this worked:**
- You tested the timeline's definition before challenging its length — the vendor conceded the scope gap themselves.
- The objection was framed as scalability and future cost, not as doubting their competence.
- You committed to a smaller, verifiable step and named in advance what would invalidate the plan.

### Scenario 2.3 — Being put on the spot with a number you don't have

**Setting:** You join a call five minutes late. The CFO's analyst asks a question about cost per applicant.

**Analyst:** Quick one — what's our cost per applicant this cycle versus last?

**You:** Good question — give me a second. [#44] Let me make sure I answer that properly. [#45]

**Analyst:** Sure.

**You:** Before I answer, can I check one thing? [#49] Are we counting the marketing spend that sits under the central budget, or only program-level spend?

**Analyst:** Program-level, ideally both.

**You:** Then there's a short answer and a long answer. [#57] Program-level, I can get you a reliable figure. Central spend, I don't have visibility into that part yet. [#152]

**Analyst:** Ballpark on program-level?

**You:** Off the top of my head, [#53] somewhere in the range of 40 to 55 per applicant — but I'd rather give you a range than a false number. [#148]

**Analyst:** The CFO will want one figure.

**You:** Understood. I don't want to guess on that one. [#140] Can I come back to you on that by end of day? [#51] I'll caveat the central-spend piece heavily [#144] and name who owns it, so the gap isn't mine to explain.

**Analyst:** That works.

**You:** I'll send a short note rather than a deck — answer first, then the reasoning. [#187]

**Why this worked:**
- Two time-buying phrases in a row bought roughly eight seconds without a single "actually" or "basically".
- The clarifying question reframed an unanswerable question into two answerable ones.
- The range plus deadline gave the analyst something usable immediately and protected your accuracy.

### Scenario 2.4 — Disagreeing with your own manager in front of the team

**Setting:** Sprint planning. Your manager wants to rebuild the reporting layer and pause data-quality work.

**Manager:** New layer first. The old one looks dated and leadership notices that.

**You:** Can I offer a different read? [#122] I see a couple of concerns with this approach. [#120]

**Manager:** Go on.

**You:** The complaints we get aren't about the look — they're about numbers not matching. That solves the symptom, not the cause. [#132]

**Manager:** A better layer would surface mismatches faster.

**You:** I agree with you to an extent, but [#28] it'd surface them faster on the same broken joins. Two systems are counting the same student twice. [#158] A new front end shows that error more beautifully.

**Manager:** We don't have time for both.

**You:** Agreed. If we have to choose, I'd protect the leadership dashboard [#114] and fix the joins behind it. I'd rather do one thing properly than three halfway. [#110]

**Manager:** Leadership will still see an old-looking dashboard.

**You:** They will. My strong preference is to fix the pipeline first [#109] — and I'd frame it as a phased rollout [#113] so the visual refresh is a named phase two, not a cancellation.

**Manager:** Alright. Phase one is data quality, phase two the rebuild. Put it in writing.

**You:** Then we're aligned — I'll write it up. [#42]

**Why this worked:**
- You disagreed on the diagnosis, not the goal, so your manager didn't have to lose face to agree.
- The "phased rollout" framing preserved their idea instead of killing it, which is why they conceded.
- The concrete technical detail (double counting) did the persuading; the softeners only kept the room comfortable.

### Scenario 2.5 — Telling a stakeholder their report will be late

**Setting:** A faculty director expects an accreditation data pack on Thursday. It won't be ready.

**You:** I want to raise something directly rather than let it sit. [#194] I need to be honest about where we are. [#198] Thursday isn't achievable.

**Director:** The submission window closes on the 30th.

**You:** Understood, and we'll make the 30th. Here's the headline. [#81] Two of six data sources failed validation, and one is a genuine mismatch, not a load error.

**Director:** How late are we talking?

**You:** My best estimate is around three working days. [#138] Two unknowns are driving the range [#145] — how fast the registry confirms the student list, and whether the historical file needs re-extraction.

**Director:** What do you need from me?

**You:** I'm not asking for budget — I'm asking for air cover. [#189] If the registry team hears the ask from you, it moves in a day instead of a week.

**Director:** I'll email them today.

**You:** Thank you. I'd rather over-communicate here than surprise you. [#180] I'll send a short update Friday, same format each week [#178] — status, blockers, next step, nothing longer than five lines.

**Director:** Good. And if it slips again?

**You:** You'll hear it from me the day I know, not the week it's due. Let's agree on what changes, and check in in two weeks. [#200]

**Why this worked:**
- The bad news came in the first two sentences, followed immediately by the reassurance that the real deadline holds.
- You converted an apology into a request — air cover — which gave the director something to do rather than something to feel.
- Committing to a fixed update format and rhythm removed the need for anyone to chase you.

## 2.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| Please do the needful. | Could you take it from here? | Names the action; the original is dated and unclear |
| I will revert back to you. | I'll get back to you by 4. | "Revert" reads as Indian English globally; adds a time |
| Kindly find the attached report. | Report's attached — headline is on slide 2. | Drops letter-writing register, points to the value |
| Why did you do like this? | Could you walk me through your thinking? | Turns an accusation into a request for reasoning |
| It is not possible. | That's tight — here's what's achievable by Friday. | Offers a path instead of a wall |
| You are wrong. | I'm not entirely convinced that's the best approach. | Keeps the disagreement about the idea |
| I don't know. | I'm not sure yet. Let me look into it. | Same honesty, plus ownership of the next step |
| As per my previous mail... | Following up on my note from Tuesday... | "As per" sounds legalistic and mildly accusing |
| The same is attached herewith. | I've attached it. | One clause instead of three; no archaic filler |
| Doing the analysis only. | I'm mid-analysis — first cut by end of day. | "Only" as a filler doesn't travel; gives a deadline |
| We are having some issues in the pipeline. | Two loads failed overnight; both re-ran clean. | Specific and closed, not vaguely alarming |
| This is very very urgent. | This blocks the Thursday submission. | Names the consequence instead of shouting |
| Myself Dhyanendra, program analytics. | I'm Dhyanendra — I run program analytics. | Standard self-introduction in global English |
| Can you please do it fast? | Is there any chance of getting it by Wednesday? | Gives a date; leaves room for a real answer |
| Actually, basically, see, the thing is... | Let me answer that in two parts. | Replaces filler with a structure the listener can follow |
| I have a doubt. | Quick question on the join logic. | "Doubt" means suspicion in most English; "question" is right |
| Discuss and revert. | Let's talk Thursday, and I'll confirm after. | Two clear actions with owners |
| Prepone the meeting. | Can we move the meeting earlier? | "Prepone" isn't used outside South Asia |
| I am not agreeing with this. | Where I'd differ is on the timeline. | Narrows the disagreement to something solvable |
| Sir, as you say. | Happy to go with that — one thing to flag first. | Signals partnership, not deference |
| Kindly ignore my earlier mail. | Correction to my last note — the figure is 412, not 389. | Fixes the record instead of asking people to forget |
| The report is showing wrong data. | The report's reading yesterday's data — refresh failed at 2 a.m. | Diagnosis beats accusation |
| We should do something about this. | My recommendation would be to fix the joins first. | Converts a worry into a position |
| Let me know your good self's availability. | What does your calendar look like Thursday? | Natural register; "good self" is archaic |
| Thanks and regards, awaiting your reply. | Thanks — I'll assume we're good unless I hear otherwise. | Closes the loop instead of waiting passively |

## 2.6 Practice Drills

### Drill 1 — The 10× rep

Anchor phrase: **"I'd like to dig a little deeper into this."** Say each variation aloud, twice, at normal speaking speed. Notice that the stress lands on *deeper*, not on *I*.

1. I'd like to dig a little deeper into this.
2. I'd like to dig a little deeper into the week-four drop-off.
3. I'd like to dig into that before we decide.
4. I want to dig a bit deeper on the withdrawal numbers.
5. Can we dig into this a little further?
6. Let's dig deeper into why the two dashboards disagree.
7. Before we move on, I'd like to dig into the source data.
8. I'd like to spend two more minutes on this.
9. Worth digging deeper here — the pattern isn't random.
10. Let me dig into it and bring you something concrete Thursday.

### Drill 2 — Slot swap

Pattern: `I agree with you to an extent, but {qualification}` — speak all eight.

1. ...but the effort estimate looks optimistic.
2. ...but that assumes the registry file arrives clean.
3. ...but we'd be reporting a number we can't reconcile.
4. ...but I'd want a fallback report before we switch off the old one.
5. ...but the sample is 40 students, not 400.
6. ...but the vendor hasn't committed to that in writing.
7. ...but that only holds for the full-time programs.
8. ...but I'd rather validate the source before we present it.

### Drill 3 — Cold open

Respond aloud in one sentence. No preamble, no apology.

1. Your Dean asks in a corridor: "Are we ahead or behind on applications this year?"
2. A colleague says: "Your dashboard number is wrong."
3. A vendor asks: "Can you confirm the four-week timeline works for you?"
4. A director asks: "Why is this taking so long?"
5. Your manager says: "Can you also own the weekly finance report from next month?"

### Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt

```
You are Margit Sørensen, a Danish-based Director of Institutional Research at a
European business school. You are precise, quiet, and allergic to overstatement.
You dislike hedging that hides a real answer, and you dislike confident numbers
that turn out to be unverified. You speak in short sentences and you ask one
follow-up question after every answer I give.

Scenario: I am presenting this month's program analytics update. Our enrollment
dashboard shows 412 confirmations; the figure you have from last week is 389. One
of six data sources failed validation and the accreditation data pack will be
three working days late. I have not yet reconciled central marketing spend.

Run a 12-turn conversation. Push me on:
 - which number is official and why
 - what exactly failed and whether it will recur
 - whether the three-day slip endangers the 30th
 - anything I state without evidence

Stay in character throughout. Do not coach me during the conversation.

Afterwards, step out of character and give me feedback in exactly four parts:
 1. Where I sounded blunt or abrupt, with the phrase I used and a better one.
 2. Where I over-hedged and buried the answer, with a tighter rewrite.
 3. Whether I gave you the headline first or made you wait for it.
 4. Three phrases from this chapter I should have used and didn't.
```

## 2.7 Master these 10 first

1. **So, if I understand correctly...** [#1] — Prevents most rework and misunderstanding.
2. **Could you walk me through your thinking?** [#3] — Asks why without sounding accusatory.
3. **Just to make sure we're aligned...** [#23] — Locks agreement before work begins.
4. **Let me think about that for a moment.** [#43] — Buys seconds without any filler.
5. **Here's the headline.** [#81] — Trains you to lead with the answer.
6. **My recommendation would be to...** [#101] — Turns analysis into a position.
7. **I'm not entirely convinced that's the best approach.** [#119] — Disagrees without any friction.
8. **I'm not sure yet. Let me look into it.** [#137] — Honest, credible, always safe.
9. **That's a reasonable ask — it just moves something else.** [#171] — Accepts work while naming cost.
10. **I'll flag the risk now so it's not a surprise later.** [#173] — Buys enormous goodwill in advance.

## Part II — The Situations


---

# Chapter 3 — Meetings

> **One-line promise:** You'll be able to hold your ground in any meeting — on camera, on mute, or on the spot — and leave the room with clear actions instead of vague goodwill.

## 3.1 Why this chapter matters

- Most non-native professionals lose meetings not on grammar but on **timing**. You wait for a clean gap that never comes, the topic moves on, and your point dies in your notes.
- The default fallback moves are the two worst ones: staying silent, or going in too hard ("No, that is wrong"). Neither reads as senior. The middle register — firm, warm, specific — is the one that gets you invited back.
- Being put on the spot is the highest-risk moment in your week. Guessing a number in a leadership review costs more than saying "let me confirm" — but only if you know how to say "let me confirm" without sounding evasive.
- Remote and hybrid is now the default, and it has its own grammar: audio drops, mute, screen share, chat, lag, hard stops, and timezones that give you a 45-minute overlap with a US counterpart. Fluency here is a separate skill from face-to-face fluency.
- Chairing is where analytics people get promoted or stall. If you can run a 30-minute meeting to a decision and send the actions the same day, you become the person the leadership team routes work through.

## 3.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)

Use these until they're automatic.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | Can everyone hear me? | Opening on a call, checking audio | Neutral |
| 2 | You're on mute. | Someone's talking to nobody | Casual |
| 3 | Sorry, you cut out there — can you say that again? | Audio dropped mid-sentence | Neutral |
| 4 | Give me one second, I'll share my screen. | Buying two seconds of setup time | Neutral |
| 5 | Can you see my screen? | Right after you hit share | Neutral |
| 6 | Let me just pull up the dashboard. | Moving to live numbers | Neutral |
| 7 | Sorry, go ahead. | You and someone else spoke at once | Neutral |
| 8 | Didn't mean to talk over you. | Recovering from a collision | Casual |
| 9 | Quick one before we start — | Raising something pre-agenda | Casual |
| 10 | Should we give it a minute for people to join? | Two people on a six-person call | Neutral |
| 11 | I think we're still waiting on a couple of people. | Explaining the delay | Neutral |
| 12 | Let's get started — we can catch the others up. | Protecting the clock | Neutral |
| 13 | Just to set the context — | Before your update | Neutral |
| 14 | The short version is... | Compressing for a busy audience | Neutral |
| 15 | Where we landed was... | Reporting a prior decision | Neutral |
| 16 | Here's what the numbers are telling us. | Handing over to your analysis | Neutral |
| 17 | Two things from my side. | Signposting your update | Neutral |
| 18 | I've got a quick update on the pipeline. | Claiming your slot | Neutral |
| 19 | Nothing blocking on my end. | Your turn, nothing to report | Casual |
| 20 | Can I jump in here? | Entering a live discussion | Neutral |
| 21 | Can I add one thing to that? | Extending someone's point | Neutral |
| 22 | Sorry to interrupt, but — | Time-critical interruption | Neutral |
| 23 | Just to build on what Priya said — | Agreeing and adding | Neutral |
| 24 | Before we move on — | Stopping a premature transition | Neutral |
| 25 | Let me finish that thought and I'll come to you. | Holding the floor politely | Neutral |
