## 3.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `Can I jump in on [TOPIC]? [YOUR POINT IN ONE SENTENCE]`
- Can I jump in on the intake numbers? I think we're double-counting deferrals.
- Can I jump in on timelines? Two weeks is only realistic if the extract is stable.
- Can I jump in on the definition? We're using two different versions of "active student".
*Watch out:* Say the topic before the point. If you open with the point, people spend three seconds working out what you're answering and you lose the floor.

**Pattern 2:** `I'd push back on [X] — [SPECIFIC REASON], so [WHAT YOU'D DO INSTEAD]`
- I'd push back on the 12% figure — it includes withdrawals, so I'd rerun it on completions only.
- I'd push back on shipping Friday — UAT hasn't started, so I'd move it to Wednesday.
- I'd push back on that framing — it treats a reporting gap as a demand drop, so I'd check the feed first.
*Watch out:* "I'd push back" needs a reason attached in the same breath. Alone, it lands as obstruction rather than analysis.

**Pattern 3:** `Can we park [TOPIC] and pick it up [WHEN / HOW]?`
- Can we park the licensing question and pick it up in Thursday's review?
- Can we park the naming convention and pick it up over email?
- Can we park the ML roadmap and pick it up once the pipeline's stable?
*Watch out:* Always attach the "when". Parking without a return date sounds like burying, and someone will drag it back within five minutes.

**Pattern 4:** `I don't have [DATA] in front of me — let me [ACTION] and come back to you by [TIME]`
- I don't have the placement split in front of me — let me pull it and come back to you by end of day.
- I don't have last year's baseline in front of me — let me check the archive and come back tomorrow morning.
- I don't have the vendor SLA in front of me — let me look it up and come back to you this afternoon.
*Watch out:* "I will check and revert" reads as dated Indian-English in a global room. Use "come back to you" and always name a time.

**Pattern 5:** `Off the top of my head, [ESTIMATE] — but let me confirm before you use it`
- Off the top of my head, around 340 applications — but let me confirm before you use it.
- Off the top of my head, mid-June — but let me confirm before you put it in the deck.
- Off the top of my head, three working days — but let me confirm with the DBA.
*Watch out:* Don't stack hedges. "Maybe roughly possibly around" destroys the estimate's value; one hedge plus one commitment is the correct dose.

**Pattern 6:** `We've got [TIME] and [N] items — I'd like to spend most of it on [PRIORITY]`
- We've got thirty minutes and four items — I'd like to spend most of it on the enrolment variance.
- We've got fifteen minutes and two decisions — I'd like to spend most of it on the vendor choice.
- We've got an hour and a long list — I'd like to spend most of it on what's blocked.
*Watch out:* Say this in the first sixty seconds. After ten minutes it sounds like a complaint about the meeting rather than a plan for it.

**Pattern 7:** `[NUMBER] looks off to me — [WHAT YOU EXPECTED] versus [WHAT'S SHOWN]`
- The conversion rate looks off to me — I'd expect around 18%, and that's showing 31%.
- The headcount looks off to me — finance had 214, and the dashboard says 227.
- Week 32 looks off to me — it's double every other week, which usually means a duplicate load.
*Watch out:* Attack the number, never the person. "Your slide is wrong" starts a defence; "that number looks off" starts an investigation.

**Pattern 8:** `Just so I understand — are you asking about [A] or [B]?`
- Just so I understand — are you asking about applications or confirmed admits?
- Just so I understand — are you asking whether we can build it, or whether we can build it by March?
- Just so I understand — is this for the board pack or for internal tracking?
*Watch out:* Ask this before you answer, not after you've spent ninety seconds answering the wrong question. Clarifying early looks sharp; clarifying late looks lost.

**Pattern 9:** `So [OWNER] takes [ACTION] by [DATE] — is that right?`
- So Anil takes the reconciliation by Thursday — is that right?
- So I take the definition doc and you sign it off by Monday — is that right?
- So procurement takes the vendor questions by next Friday — is that right?
*Watch out:* Say the owner's name out loud. "Someone will look at it" is how actions die; naming a person in the room creates a commitment nobody wants to break.

**Pattern 10:** `Give me one second — I'll [share my screen / dial in / rejoin]`
- Give me one second — I'll share my screen and show you the query.
- Give me one second — I'll dial in on my phone, the audio's dropping.
- Give me one second — I'll rejoin, my video's frozen.
*Watch out:* Narrate the fix in one line, then do it silently. Long apologies about your internet cost more meeting time than the problem itself.

**Pattern 11:** `I know it's [late / early] your side, so [COMPRESSION MOVE]`
- I know it's late your side, so I'll keep this to the two decisions.
- I know it's early your side, so I'll send the detail and we'll just cover blockers.
- I know it's the end of your day, so let's agree the next step and I'll write it up.
*Watch out:* Acknowledge the timezone once, at the start. Repeating it every few minutes turns courtesy into pressure.

**Pattern 12:** `So to recap — [1], [2], [3]. Anything I've missed?`
- So to recap — Anil fixes the feed, I rerun the numbers, we meet Thursday. Anything I've missed?
- So to recap — we're going with vendor B, contract by month-end, pilot in two cohorts. Anything I've missed?
- So to recap — the number stands, with a footnote on the definition. Anything I've missed?
*Watch out:* Three items maximum, spoken as a list. A recap of nine things is not a recap, and nobody remembers items four to nine.

**Pattern 13:** `I've had my hand up for a bit — can I get [TIME] on [TOPIC]?`
- I've had my hand up for a bit — can I get thirty seconds on the data quality issue?
- I've had my hand up for a bit — can I get two minutes on what this means for the March release?
- I've had my hand up a while — can I get a moment on the definition before we vote?
*Watch out:* Naming a small, specific amount of time is what gets you the floor. An open-ended "can I say something" is easy for a chair to defer.

**Pattern 14:** `I disagree on [X], but I'll commit to [Y] and [SUPPORT ACTION]`
- I disagree on the timeline, but I'll commit to it and flag early if it slips.
- I disagree with using that metric, but I'll commit to the decision and footnote the caveat.
- I disagree on the sequencing, but I'll commit and get the team moving today.
*Watch out:* Don't relitigate afterwards. This phrase only carries weight if your behaviour after the meeting matches the second half of the sentence.

**Pattern 15:** `Before you use that number — [CAVEAT], so [SAFE USE]`
- Before you use that number — it's pre-reconciliation, so treat it as directional only.
- Before you use that figure — it excludes the exec programme, so label it accordingly.
- Before that goes external — the cohort definition changed in 2024, so we'd need a footnote.
*Watch out:* Deliver the caveat before the number travels, not after. A caveat that arrives after the slide is in a board pack reads as an excuse.

## 3.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 3.1 — A number looks wrong in the weekly status meeting

**Setting:** Monday status call, twelve people, the associate dean is on. A conversion metric on your team's dashboard has jumped from 18% to 31%.

**Program Head:** Great news on conversions — we're at 31% this week, up from 18%. I'll take that to the leadership review on Wednesday.

**You:** Can I jump in here? [#20] Before that goes to Wednesday — the conversion rate looks off to me. [#94] I'd expect somewhere around 18 to 20, and 31 is a big jump for one week.

**Program Head:** The dashboard's showing 31.

**You:** It is, and that's what worries me. Help me understand how we got to that figure — [#92] are we comparing like for like? [#96] Last week's number was applications to admits, and I think this week's denominator changed when we filtered out the deferrals.

**Analyst:** That could be the new filter I added on Friday.

**You:** That would explain it. I'd want to sanity-check it before we share it. [#95]

**Program Head:** I do need something for Wednesday, though. Can you give me a number now?

**You:** Off the top of my head it'll come back close to 19 — but let me confirm before you use it. [#43] I don't want to guess on something this visible. [#132]

**Program Head:** Fair. When can you get it to me?

**You:** Let me rerun it and come back to you by end of day. [#42] If it's clean, you've got two days. If it isn't, better we know today than Wednesday morning.

**Program Head:** Do that.

**You:** So the action for me is the rerun and a one-line note on the definition change, by end of day. [#48] And Sameer, can I put your name against documenting the filter? [#51]

**Why this worked:**
- The challenge was aimed at the number, not the analyst — so nobody had to defend themselves and the room could investigate.
- The hedged estimate plus a hard deadline gave the program head something to work with without you certifying a figure you didn't trust.
- Actions were named with owners and a date before the topic moved on, so the fix survives the meeting.

### Scenario 3.2 — Chairing a meeting that's running over

**Setting:** You're chairing a 30-minute data governance call. At minute 26, two people are deep into a debate about naming conventions and one agenda item is untouched.

**You:** I'm going to steer us back to the agenda. [#121] We're five minutes from the top of the hour [#110] and we still haven't touched the source-of-truth decision.

**Vendor Lead:** But the naming thing affects everything downstream.

**You:** It does, and it's a real issue. Can we park it and pick it up in Thursday's working session? [#33] I'll put it top of that agenda.

**Vendor Lead:** As long as it doesn't disappear.

**You:** It won't — I'll capture it as an open item in the notes. [#160] Right, source of truth. Rather than reopen the whole discussion, let me summarize where we are: [#53] we've got two candidate systems, and the proposal is that the warehouse becomes the single source for program metrics from next quarter.

**Data Engineer:** I still have concerns about the refresh lag.

**You:** Noted, and I'd rather hear the objection properly than move past it. [#125] Is that a reason not to decide, or a condition on the decision?

**Data Engineer:** A condition. Hourly refresh and I'm fine.

**You:** Then let's capture that as a decision, not a discussion: [#159] warehouse becomes the source of truth, conditional on hourly refresh. Is anyone uncomfortable with that? [#115]

**Team:** No. Fine. Good.

**You:** Let me read back the actions. [#116] Ravi confirms the refresh schedule by Wednesday, I write up the decision today, naming conventions go to Thursday. Anything I've missed?

**Data Engineer:** That's it.

**You:** I'm going to cut us off there — we're out of time. [#122] Thanks everyone, notes by this evening. [#57]

**Why this worked:**
- Parking came with a specific return slot, which is the only version of parking people accept.
- "Is that a reason not to decide, or a condition on the decision?" converted an open objection into a closeable one in a single question.
- The chair closed on a decision plus actions, not on the clock running out — the meeting ended, but the work didn't stay ambiguous.

### Scenario 3.3 — Asked a question you genuinely can't answer live

**Setting:** Quarterly leadership review, twenty people, camera on. The dean asks you about attrition drivers, which sits with a different team.

**Dean:** You've got the data. Why is attrition up in the two-year program specifically?

**You:** Just so I understand — are you asking about the overall increase, or what's different about the two-year cohort? [#46]

**Dean:** What's different about the two-year cohort.

**You:** Then I'll be straight with you — I can't answer that properly today. [#136] I can tell you the two-year attrition is up 2.4 points year on year, and that it's concentrated in the first semester. What's driving it, I'd need to look at the exit interview data, and that sits with student affairs.

**Dean:** Can't you take a view?

**You:** I could, but I'd rather give you a right answer next week than a wrong one now. [#131] If I speculate here and it goes into the board narrative, we'll be correcting it in November.

**Dean:** Reasonable.

**You:** The best person for the qualitative side is Meera — I'll loop her in [#134] and we'll come back jointly. What I'll commit to today is the quantitative picture: which courses, which weeks, which segments.

**Dean:** How long?

**You:** Ten days if I need student affairs, four if I don't. Can I confirm by Friday which of those it is? [#45]

**Dean:** Do that. And I want it before the November pack.

**You:** Understood. Just noting for the record [#106] that the exit interview data isn't currently in the warehouse — that's the reason for the delay, and it's worth fixing separately.

**Why this worked:**
- You separated what you did know from what you didn't, so "I can't answer" landed as precision rather than unpreparedness.
- The refusal to speculate was justified by consequence ("we'll be correcting it in November"), not by discomfort.
- You left the meeting with a scoped commitment and quietly planted the structural fix on the record.

### Scenario 3.4 — Cross-timezone call: interrupting a European counterpart

**Setting:** 6:30pm your time, 3:00pm Amsterdam. Forty-minute window with a European vendor lead who talks in long paragraphs. You need to correct a scope assumption before she finishes.

**Vendor Lead (Karin):** ...so once the migration completes we'll point all the reporting at the new instance, and because the schema is broadly the same we don't expect any changes needed on your side, which means we can keep the go-live as the fifteenth, and the training can then run in the week after, assuming...

**You:** Sorry to come in across you, Karin [#141] — one thing before you go further, because it changes the plan.

**Karin:** Go ahead.

**You:** The schema isn't broadly the same for us. Fourteen of our stored procedures reference the old column names directly. Those will break on day one.

**Karin:** That wasn't in the assessment.

**You:** I don't think it was scoped. And I know we're tight with the timezone [#142], so rather than debate the assessment now, can we agree the next step? We won't overlap again until Monday. [#145]

**Karin:** What do you propose?

**You:** Two things. One, I send you the dependency list tonight — I'll leave it in the shared doc overnight so you have it first thing. [#146] Two, we treat the fifteenth as at risk until you've costed the remediation. I'm not asking you to move it today.

**Karin:** I can look at it tomorrow morning my time. But I'd like to keep the fifteenth if at all possible.

**You:** Understood, and I'd like that too. If we want the fifteenth, something else has to give [#109] — most likely the training week. It's end of day here, so I'd like to close the loop now: [#144] you cost the remediation by Wednesday, I send the dependency list tonight, and we decide on the date Thursday. Is that right? [Pattern 9]

**Karin:** Agreed. And thank you for stopping me — better now than in two weeks.

**You:** Thanks for taking it well. [#199] I'll write it up and copy your project manager.

**Why this worked:**
- The interruption was pre-justified in the same breath ("because it changes the plan"), so it read as useful rather than rude.
- The timezone was used as a reason to converge, not as small talk — you traded debate for a decision because you had no second window.
- Every commitment had an owner, a time, and an asynchronous handoff, which is how cross-timezone work actually closes.

### Scenario 3.5 — Hybrid call with tech friction and a hijacked agenda

**Setting:** Hybrid kickoff for a dashboard rebuild. Four people in a room, three remote, one with bad audio. You're presenting.

**You:** Can everyone hear me? [#1] Room, I'll come to you first if the remote folks can't hear.

**Remote (Nikhil):** You're a bit faint.

**You:** Let me move closer to the mic. Better? Good. I've dropped the agenda in the chat. [#65] Thirty minutes, three things [#66] — scope, timeline, and who owns what. Feel free to jump in as I go. [#69]

**You:** Give me one second, I'll share my screen. [#4] ... Can you see my screen? [#5]

**Nikhil:** Yes, but could you make it a bit bigger? [#74]

**You:** How's that? Right — current state on the left, target on the right.

**Stakeholder (in room):** While you're there, can we also add the alumni giving numbers to this dashboard?

**You:** That's a good ask, and it's a separate conversation. [#102] Can we park it [#33] and pick it up once scope is signed off? I'll add it to the backlog today so it isn't lost.

**Stakeholder:** Fine.

**Nikhil:** Sorry, my audio's choppy — let me dial in. [#77]

**You:** No problem, I'll keep going and you can jump back in. Sameer, I'll stop sharing so we can talk [#76] about the timeline.

**Sameer:** Can we have it by month-end?

**You:** Realistically, that's a two-week job [#108] after the feed is stable, and the feed isn't stable yet. If we want month-end, we'd ship without historicals.

**Sameer:** Let's talk about that.

**You:** Happy to, but we've got eight minutes and one item left. [#163] Can I put the trade-off in the notes and we decide in Thursday's stand-up? So to recap — scope is agreed as three tabs, alumni giving is parked, timeline is at risk pending the feed, and Sameer decides on historicals Thursday. Anything I've missed? [Pattern 12]

**Sameer:** Nothing.

**You:** Someone's asked in the chat [#82] about access permissions — Nikhil, that's yours, can I put your name against it? [#51] I'll send the notes after this. [#57]

**Why this worked:**
- Tech problems were handled in one line each and the meeting kept moving; nobody spent three minutes apologising for a microphone.
- The scope grab was parked with a visible destination (the backlog), which protects the agenda without dismissing the stakeholder.
- The chat was treated as part of the meeting record, so a remote question got an owner instead of scrolling away.

## 3.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| No, that is wrong. | I see it a bit differently — can I show you what I'm looking at? | Opens an investigation instead of a fight. |
| Kindly repeat. | Sorry, you cut out — could you say that again? | Blames the connection, not the speaker. |
| I did not get you. | I didn't quite follow — can you say a bit more? | Sounds curious rather than accusatory. |
| Please revert back with the numbers. | Could you send the numbers over when you get a chance? | "Revert" reads as dated; this sounds current. |
| Same is not clear to me. | That part isn't clear to me yet. | Natural word order, and "yet" keeps it collaborative. |
| Myself Dhyanendra, I am handling program analytics. | I'm Dhyanendra — I look after program analytics. | Standard self-introduction in a global room. |
| I have a doubt. | One thing I'm unsure about — | "Doubt" implies suspicion to native ears. |
| Sir, may I say something? | Can I jump in here? | Peer-level, not permission-seeking. |
| It is not possible. | That's tough by Friday — here's what is realistic. | Converts refusal into a negotiation. |
| You did not inform me. | I don't think that reached me — can we tighten the handoff? | Fixes the process, not the blame. |
| This is very simple, anyone can do it. | It's fairly quick — I can turn it around today. | Keeps your value without dismissing the ask. |
| As per my knowledge, the feed runs daily. | As far as I know, the feed runs daily. | "As per my knowledge" is non-idiomatic. |
| Please do the needful. | Could you take it from here? | Names an actual action. |
| I will do it EOD. | I'll have it with you by end of day. | Says who receives it and when. |
| Explain again, I am not getting it. | Let me play it back to you and you can correct me. | Shifts the effort onto you, which raises your credibility. |
| Your data is wrong. | The numbers aren't matching mine — can we reconcile? | Makes it a joint problem with a next step. |
| We must discuss this later. | Let's take that offline. | Standard idiom, no obligation-heavy tone. |
| Anyways, moving on. | Let's move on — I'll come back to that. | Promises a return instead of dropping it. |
| Hello? Hello? Can you hear? | I think we've lost you — I'll carry on and you can jump back in. | Keeps the meeting moving during a dropout. |
| Sorry sorry sorry, my mistake. | My mistake — let me correct it. | One apology, then a fix. |
| I am not the right person for this. | That's outside my area — the best person is Meera, I'll loop her in. | Redirects with a name instead of a dead end. |
| Kindly bear with me. | Bear with me one second. | Same meaning, no formal padding. |
| Let us close the meeting now. | Let's wrap up — I'll send the notes. | Closes and assigns the record in one move. |
| Nothing from my side. | All good on my side — nothing to flag. | Sounds engaged rather than checked out. |
| Please ignore my previous statement. | Scratch that — let me restate it. | How native speakers self-correct live. |
| I am having a query regarding the dashboard. | Quick question on the dashboard — | Shorter, and it signals you'll be brief. |
| Why did you not raise this in the meeting? | It would help to hear that in the meeting so we can plan around it. | Corrects the behaviour without a public reprimand. |

## 3.6 Practice Drills

**Drill 1 — The 10× rep**

Anchor phrase: **"Can I jump in here?"** Say each of these out loud twice, keeping your voice level and unhurried. The goal is to enter a conversation without your pitch rising.

1. Can I jump in here?
2. Can I jump in on that point?
3. Can I jump in before we move on?
4. Sorry, can I jump in quickly?
5. Can I come in on that?
6. Can I add one thing there?
7. Can I get thirty seconds on this?
8. Before we move on — can I flag something?
9. Sorry to interrupt, but this changes the timeline.
10. Can I jump in? I think we're using two different definitions.

**Drill 2 — Slot swap**

Pattern: `I don't have [DATA] in front of me — let me [ACTION] and come back to you by [TIME]`

Speak all eight, full sentence each time, no notes:

1. the placement numbers / pull them / end of day
2. last year's baseline / check the archive / tomorrow morning
3. the vendor SLA / look it up / this afternoon
4. the exact headcount / confirm with HR / Thursday
5. the query runtime / test it on staging / first thing Monday
6. the cohort definition / check the data dictionary / within the hour
7. the model's precision score / rerun the evaluation / Wednesday
8. the invoice detail / speak to procurement / by close of play Friday

**Drill 3 — Cold open**

One sentence only, out loud, within three seconds of reading each prompt.

1. Your associate dean says: "So can I use the 31% in Wednesday's deck?" — and you don't trust the number yet.
2. You're chairing. Minute 27 of 30, one agenda item untouched, two people arguing about column names.
3. A US director asks a question that belongs to another team entirely, in front of twenty people.
4. It's 7pm your time, your German counterpart is mid-monologue, and she's about to commit to a date that will break.
5. You've just realised the figure on your own slide, currently on screen, is wrong.

**Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt**

```
You are Karin de Vries, a Dutch operations director at a European
education-technology vendor. You are 46, direct to the point of bluntness,
and you speak in long unbroken paragraphs. You genuinely dislike being
interrupted but you respect people who interrupt you with a reason. You are
under pressure to hold a go-live date of the fifteenth.

I am a senior program analytics lead at a business school in India. It is
3pm your time and 6:30pm mine. We have a 40-minute call about migrating our
reporting to your new platform.

Run the call in character, one turn at a time. Do the following:
- Speak in paragraphs of at least four sentences. Do not pause helpfully.
- Twice during the call, talk over me when I start speaking.
- Assume our schema needs no changes and defend the fifteenth.
- Push me for a firm commitment on a number I clearly don't have yet.
- Near the end, ask me to confirm the date in writing today.

Stay in character until I write "END SCENE".

After END SCENE, drop character and give me feedback on:
1. Whether my interruptions were early enough to be useful, or too late.
2. Whether I justified each interruption in the same sentence, or apologised
   without giving a reason.
3. How I handled the pressure to commit — did I hedge cleanly or waffle?
4. Any phrasing that sounded either too soft or too blunt for a European
   vendor relationship, with a better version of each line.
5. One habit to drill before my next cross-timezone call.
```

## 3.7 Master these 10 first

1. **Can I jump in here?** [#20] — Your default door into any discussion.
2. **I'd push back on that slightly.** [#30] — Disagree without starting a fight.
3. **Can we park that for now?** [#33] — Protects your agenda from drift.
4. **I don't have that number in front of me.** [#41] — Stops the guess before it starts.
5. **Let me check and come back to you by end of day.** [#42] — Turns a gap into a commitment.
6. **Just so I understand — are you asking about A or B?** [#46] — Answers the right question first.
7. **The number looks off to me.** [#94] — Challenges data, not people.
8. **Let me read back the actions.** [#116] — The single move that makes meetings stick.
9. **I'd rather give you a right answer tomorrow.** [#131] — Buys time and builds trust.
10. **I disagree, but I'll commit and make it work.** [#178] — Loses a decision, keeps your standing.


---

# Chapter 4 — Presentations

> **One-line promise:** You will be able to open, signpost, narrate a live dashboard, survive Q&A, compress 30 minutes into 10, and close with a clear ask — in front of your own team or in front of the Dean.

## 4.1 Why this chapter matters

- Most data professionals present the *work* instead of the *decision*. You walk the room through every join, filter and caveat, and by the time you reach the recommendation the senior person in the room has already stopped listening. The fix is one habit: takeaway first, evidence second.
- Non-native speakers tend to lose the room in the gaps *between* points. The individual sentences are correct, but there is no "That brings me to..." — so the audience never knows whether you are still on point one or already on point three. Signposting is cheap and it makes you sound structured, which reads as senior.
- Under pressure — a hostile question, a number you don't have — the instinct is to over-apologize ("Sorry, sorry, actually I have not calculated this") or to over-formalize ("I would humbly request your kind indulgence"). Both cost credibility. The native-level move is short, calm, and specific: "I don't have that in front of me. I'll come back to you today."
- Live dashboards are a distinct skill. If you cannot say "on the x-axis we've got weeks, on the y-axis applications, and the dotted line is target" in one breath, your audience spends the first thirty seconds decoding the chart instead of hearing you.
- Time gets taken from you constantly. A 30-minute slot becomes 10 because the previous item overran. Professionals who can say "here's the ten-minute version" without visible panic get invited back.
- What changes when you fix this: you stop being the person who *supplies* numbers and start being the person whose recommendation gets adopted in the room.

## 4.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)

Use these until they're automatic. These are the phrases that carry an ordinary weekly review, a stand-up demo, or a fifteen-minute dashboard walkthrough.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | Thanks for making the time. | Opening any scheduled review | Neutral |
| 2 | Let me start with the headline. | Leading with the takeaway | Neutral |
| 3 | Here's where we are. | Framing status in one line | Neutral |
| 4 | Quick agenda — three things. | Setting the shape early | Neutral |
| 5 | By the end of this I want a decision on intake targets. | Naming the ask up front | High-stakes |
| 6 | I'll keep this to ten minutes. | Making a time contract | Neutral |
| 7 | Feel free to jump in as we go. | Inviting interruptions, small team | Casual |
| 8 | I'd suggest we hold questions to the end. | Protecting a tight slot | Formal |
| 9 | Can everyone see my screen? | Starting a screen share | Neutral |
| 10 | Let me share my screen. | Moving to the dashboard | Neutral |
| 11 | Bear with me for a second. | Covering a fumble or slow load | Casual |
| 12 | Slide two, if you're following along. | Pointing to a slide reference | Neutral |
| 13 | This is the dashboard we ship every Monday. | Introducing a familiar visual | Neutral |
| 14 | On the x-axis we've got weeks; on the y-axis, applications. | Explaining axes fast | Neutral |
| 15 | The takeaway is at the top of the slide. | Directing attention | Neutral |
| 16 | Let me walk you through it. | Starting a walkthrough | Neutral |
| 17 | Zooming in on this part. | Narrowing to a detail | Neutral |
| 18 | The trend is up and to the right. | Summarizing direction | Casual |
| 19 | It's been flat since March. | Describing no change | Neutral |
| 20 | That spike in April is the deadline effect. | Explaining an outlier | Neutral |
| 21 | Ignore the last bar — it's partial data. | Pre-empting a misread | Neutral |
| 22 | Two things stand out here. | Structuring the read | Neutral |
| 23 | Let me put a number on that. | Adding precision on request | Neutral |
| 24 | Roughly speaking, about a third. | Approximating out loud | Casual |
| 25 | Directionally, we're fine. | Confidence without false precision | US |
| 26 | That brings me to the next point. | Standard transition | Neutral |
| 27 | Which leads into the second piece. | Standard transition | Neutral |
| 28 | Before I move on — anything on this? | Mid-deck checkpoint | Neutral |
| 29 | Coming back to what I said earlier. | Referring back | Neutral |
| 30 | As I mentioned at the top. | Referring back briefly | Neutral |
| 31 | I'll come back to that in a minute. | Deferring inside the deck | Neutral |
| 32 | Good question — let me take that. | Accepting a question | Neutral |
| 33 | Let me make sure I've understood the question. | Clarifying something vague | Neutral |
| 34 | Say a bit more about what you're looking for. | Unpacking a vague ask | Neutral |
| 35 | I don't have that number in front of me. | Admitting a gap cleanly | Neutral |
| 36 | I'd rather check than guess. | Refusing to invent a figure | Neutral |
| 37 | Let me come back to you on that today. | Committing to a follow-up | Neutral |
| 38 | That's a fair challenge. | Absorbing pushback | Neutral |
| 39 | Fair point — here's how we handled it. | Answering a methodology query | Neutral |
| 40 | To be clear, this counts applications, not applicants. | Correcting a definition | Neutral |
| 41 | That's outside what I've brought today. | Deferring an off-topic question | Neutral |
| 42 | Happy to take that offline. | Parking a tangent politely | Neutral |
| 43 | Let's park that and keep moving. | Reclaiming time | Neutral |
| 44 | In the interest of time, I'll skip ahead. | Cutting content live | Formal |
| 45 | I'll jump straight to the punchline. | Compressing on the fly | Casual |
| 46 | We've got five minutes left, so let me land this. | Signalling the runway | Neutral |
| 47 | Two quick things and I'll hand back. | Wrapping up | Neutral |
| 48 | So — where does that leave us? | Setting up the close | Neutral |
| 49 | What I need from you is a yes or no on the extra headcount. | The explicit ask | High-stakes |
| 50 | If I don't hear objections, I'll proceed as planned. | Default-to-action close | Neutral |
| 51 | Does that answer it? | Confirming after a question | Neutral |
| 52 | Anything I've missed? | Opening the floor | Neutral |
| 53 | Let's take one last question. | Closing Q&A | Neutral |
| 54 | I'll send the deck round after this. | Follow-through | Neutral |
| 55 | I'll follow up with the numbers in writing. | Follow-through on a gap | Written |
| 56 | Thanks, everyone — I'll give you the time back. | Ending early, deliberately | Neutral |
| 57 | Let me set the context in one line. | Framing before detail | Neutral |
| 58 | Short answer, yes — the longer answer is on slide six. | Layered answering | Neutral |
| 59 | Sorry, you cut out — could you say that again? | Remote audio drop | Neutral |
| 60 | Just to close the loop on that earlier question. | Returning to a deferred item | Neutral |

### Tier 2 — Weekly (phrases 61–120)

These come out in a monthly program review, a leadership readout, a vendor demo, a Q&A that goes longer than you planned.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 61 | Let me frame the problem before the solution. | Structuring a longer talk | Neutral |
| 62 | Three numbers, then a recommendation. | Promising a shape | Neutral |
| 63 | I've got twelve slides but I'll use four. | Signalling discipline | Casual |
| 64 | The rest is in the appendix. | Deferring detail | Neutral |
| 65 | I'll flag the assumptions as I go. | Building trust early | Neutral |
| 66 | If I could keep only one chart, it'd be this one. | Elevating the key visual | Neutral |
| 67 | Read this one left to right. | Guiding the eye | Neutral |
| 68 | The dotted line is target; the bars are actual. | Decoding a combo chart | Neutral |
| 69 | The shaded band is the confidence interval. | Explaining uncertainty visually | Neutral |
| 70 | Careful with the scale — that's a log axis. | Preventing a misread | Neutral |
| 71 | Both charts share an axis, so they're comparable. | Justifying a side-by-side | Neutral |
| 72 | The grey series is last year, for reference. | Explaining a baseline | Neutral |
| 73 | Each dot is one program. | Explaining a scatter | Neutral |
| 74 | That gap is where the pipeline failed, not a real drop. | Explaining missing data | Neutral |
| 75 | That's an outlier and I've left it in deliberately. | Defending a chart choice | Neutral |
| 76 | Strip out the outlier and the picture barely moves. | Pre-empting the obvious question | Neutral |
| 77 | In percentage terms it looks dramatic; in absolute terms it's forty students. | Right-sizing a number | Neutral |
| 78 | Let me put that in context. | Before a comparison | Neutral |
| 79 | Against the same week last year, we're ahead by nine percent. | Making a like-for-like point | Neutral |
| 80 | Month on month it's down; year on year it's up. | Two-speed reporting | Neutral |
| 81 | So what does that mean for us? | Pivoting to implications | Neutral |
| 82 | The "so what" is this. | Forcing the point | US |
| 83 | I'll give you the headline and then the caveats. | Sequencing honestly | Neutral |
| 84 | One caveat before anyone acts on this. | Protecting a soft number | Neutral |
| 85 | I want to be upfront about the data quality here. | Disclosing a weakness | Neutral |
| 86 | Let me tell you how this metric is defined. | Heading off definition fights | Neutral |
| 87 | We changed the definition in March, which is why the line jumps. | Explaining a discontinuity | Neutral |
| 88 | That's a methodology question — worth doing properly. | Legitimizing a challenge | Neutral |
| 89 | Let me walk you through how we calculated it. | Opening the black box | Neutral |
| 90 | We tested it two ways and both give the same answer. | Building confidence | Neutral |
| 91 | I'd defend that number; I'd be more careful with this one. | Grading your own evidence | Neutral |
| 92 | I'm confident on the direction, less so on the magnitude. | Calibrated honesty | High-stakes |
| 93 | That's a reasonable alternative read. | Conceding partially | Neutral |
| 94 | I hear you — and here's where I'd push back. | Disagreeing without friction | Neutral |
| 95 | Let me separate the two questions in there. | Untangling a multi-part question | Neutral |
| 96 | I'll answer the second part first, if that's alright. | Reordering a messy question | Neutral |
| 97 | I want to make sure Priya gets her question in too. | Managing the room | Neutral |
| 98 | Hold that thread — I think slide nine covers it. | Deferring to your own content | Neutral |
| 99 | If we go down that path we'll run out of time. | Naming the trade-off | Neutral |
| 100 | I'd rather give you a right answer tomorrow than a wrong one now. | Refusing to be rushed | High-stakes |
| 101 | My honest answer is I don't know yet. | Owning a genuine gap | Neutral |
| 102 | I can get you that by end of day. | Bounding the follow-up | Neutral |
| 103 | It's a fair ask and it's not a small one — let me scope it. | Pricing a request | Neutral |
| 104 | Let me take that as an action. | Converting a question to a task | Neutral |
| 105 | I'm noting that as an open item. | Recording without committing | Written |
| 106 | Can I check whether that's a blocker for the decision? | Testing the objection's weight | Neutral |
| 107 | Does that change your view? | Probing after an answer | Neutral |
| 108 | Where do you land on this? | Asking for a position | US |
| 109 | What would you need to see to be comfortable? | Converting doubt into a spec | High-stakes |
| 110 | I'm looking for a steer, not a sign-off. | Lowering the stakes deliberately | UK/EU |
| 111 | Two options — I'd recommend the first. | Presenting a choice | Neutral |
| 112 | Here's my recommendation, and here's what I'd need to deliver it. | Linking ask to resource | Neutral |
| 113 | If we do nothing, here's what happens. | The cost-of-inaction frame | High-stakes |
| 114 | The decision we need today is scope, not timeline. | Narrowing the debate | Neutral |

| 119 | Let me give the last two minutes back to you. | Ending generously | Neutral |

### Tier 3 — Situational (phrases 121–170)

Specific rooms, specific trouble: live filtering, a chart that isn't landing, a slot that just got halved, a question you shouldn't answer.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 121 | Before I start — has everyone seen the pre-read? | Calibrating the opening | Neutral |

| 139 | Let me try that again, more simply. | Self-correcting | Neutral |
| 140 | Put it this way. | Reframing in plain words | Casual |
| 141 | Think of it as a funnel with three leaks. | Offering an analogy | Neutral |
| 142 | To use your own framing from last month. | Borrowing their language | Formal |
| 143 | That's a different question from the one on the slide — but a good one. | Naming a drift politely | Neutral |
| 144 | Honestly, that's above my pay grade — I'd want the Dean's view. | Declining to overreach | Casual |
| 145 | I don't want to speak for the faculty on that. | Staying in your lane | Neutral |
| 146 | I'd be guessing, and a guess from me ends up in someone's board deck. | Explaining why you won't guess | High-stakes |
| 147 | Let me be careful here, because this number gets quoted. | Flagging sensitivity | High-stakes |
| 148 | I'd want to sanity-check that before it leaves the room. | Containing a soft figure | Neutral |
| 149 | Can I push back on the premise a little? | Challenging the question | Neutral |
| 150 | I think we're solving a different problem from the one you're describing. | Reframing the disagreement | High-stakes |
| 151 | That assumption held last year; it doesn't hold now. | Retiring an old belief | Neutral |
| 152 | We looked at that and ruled it out — here's why. | Showing prior work | Neutral |
| 153 | If methodology is the sticking point, let's give it thirty seconds now. | Timeboxing a challenge | Neutral |
| 154 | I'd rather you challenge it here than after we ship it. | Welcoming scrutiny | Neutral |
| 155 | Here's the same chart with your assumption instead of mine. | Live sensitivity check | High-stakes |
| 156 | We've just lost twenty minutes, so here's the ten-minute version. | Compressing on the spot | Neutral |
| 157 | I'll skip the build and go straight to the recommendation. | Cutting the narrative | Neutral |
| 158 | Slides four to eleven are background — I'll leave those with you. | Offloading to reading | Neutral |
| 159 | If we're out of time, the one thing I need is the go-ahead on the hire. | Protecting the ask | High-stakes |
| 160 | I'm happy to take the rest in writing if that's easier. | Moving Q&A to email | Written |
| 161 | Let me hand over to Ravi for the technical piece. | Passing the baton | Neutral |
| 162 | Ravi, do you want to take that one? | Redirecting a question | Casual |
| 163 | Coming back to you, Meera — does that cover it? | Closing a loop with a person | Neutral |
| 164 | I promised you a number earlier, and here it is. | Delivering on a defer | Neutral |
| 165 | I said I'd check, and I did — it's eighteen percent. | Visible follow-through | Neutral |
| 166 | Let me leave the ask on the screen while you discuss it. | Keeping the decision visible | High-stakes |
| 167 | I'll stay on afterwards if anyone wants to go deeper. | Offering overflow | Neutral |
| 168 | That's the last slide, but not the last word. | Inviting continued debate | Neutral |
| 169 | Thank you — and thank you for the hard questions. | Closing a tough session | Neutral |
| 170 | Let's decide it now so we don't spend another meeting on it. | Forcing closure | High-stakes |

### Tier 4 — Rare but high-impact (phrases 171–200)

You may use these twice a year. When you do, they change the outcome of the meeting.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 171 | I'd like to change the shape of this meeting. | Abandoning the plan openly | High-stakes |
| 172 | I'm going to put the deck down for a minute. | Switching to conversation | High-stakes |
| 173 | Can I take the slides off the screen and just talk to you? | Removing the crutch | High-stakes |
| 174 | I'll be blunt, because the number deserves it. | Prefacing bad news | High-stakes |
| 175 | This is the slide I'd want on the record. | Marking accountability | Formal |
| 176 | I've brought you a problem, not a solution — deliberately. | Reframing expectations | High-stakes |
| 177 | I got this wrong last quarter; here's what I changed. | Rebuilding credibility | High-stakes |
| 178 | The figure I gave you in June was wrong. This is the corrected one. | Owning an error | High-stakes |
| 179 | I'd like to withdraw the recommendation I made earlier. | Reversing in public | High-stakes |
| 180 | I'm not going to defend that chart — it shouldn't have shipped. | Refusing to spin | High-stakes |
| 181 | If we can't agree the definition, the number is meaningless. | Stopping a circular debate | High-stakes |
| 182 | Respectfully, I think that read is wrong, and I'd like to show you why. | Contradicting a senior leader | High-stakes |
| 183 | I'd like to escalate this properly rather than settle it in the room. | Refusing a corridor decision | Formal |
| 184 | I don't think this group can decide it — we need the Dean here. | Naming the missing authority | High-stakes |
| 185 | Can I ask for a decision rather than a discussion? | Interrupting a drift | High-stakes |
| 186 | I need a yes, a no, or a date. | Maximum-clarity ask | High-stakes |
| 187 | I'll take silence as agreement, but I'd rather have the yes. | Forcing a verbal commitment | High-stakes |
| 188 | If the answer is no, tell me now and I'll stop the work. | Making refusal cheap | High-stakes |
| 189 | This is the third time we've discussed it — let me propose we close it. | Ending a recurring loop | High-stakes |
| 190 | I'd like to hold us to the decision we took in March. | Enforcing a prior agreement | Formal |
| 191 | Let me put my own credibility behind this one. | Staking personal capital | High-stakes |
| 192 | If it goes wrong, that's on me. | Absorbing risk visibly | High-stakes |
| 193 | I'd rather disappoint you now than surprise you in December. | Delivering early bad news | High-stakes |
| 194 | I want to say the uncomfortable thing. | Opening a hard truth | High-stakes |
| 195 | Give me two minutes uninterrupted, then all the pushback you want. | Buying air in a hostile room | High-stakes |
| 196 | Let me answer the question you're actually asking. | Going under the surface | High-stakes |
| 197 | I think the real question is whether we trust the data. | Naming the elephant | High-stakes |
| 198 | Let's stop presenting and start deciding. | Breaking a stalemate | High-stakes |
| 199 | I'll close there — the ask is one line and it's on the screen. | Disciplined ending | High-stakes |
| 200 | Whatever you decide, I'll make it work — but I'd like you to decide today. | Loyal pressure | High-stakes |

## 4.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `Let me start with {THE HEADLINE / THE CONCLUSION / WHAT CHANGED}.`
- Let me start with the headline: applications are up nine percent, quality is flat.
- Let me start with the conclusion, and then show you how we got there.
- Let me start with what changed since the last review.
*Watch out:* Don't say "Let me start with the introduction of the topic" — English speakers start with content, not with an announcement of an announcement.

**Pattern 2:** `On the {AXIS} we've got {UNIT}; the {VISUAL ELEMENT} is {MEANING}.`
- On the x-axis we've got application weeks; the red line is last year.
- On the y-axis we've got completion rate; the shaded band is the confidence interval.
- On the left axis we've got headcount; the bars are budgeted, the dots are actual.
*Watch out:* "We've got" is the natural spoken form. "In the x-axis there is..." is the giveaway — it's "on the axis", not "in".

**Pattern 3:** `That brings me to {NEXT POINT}.` / `Which leads into {NEXT POINT}.`
- That brings me to the second issue — the refresh window.
- Which leads into the part I actually need your help with.
- That brings me neatly to cost.
*Watch out:* Use one transition per section, not per slide. Repeated "That brings me to..." six times in eight minutes sounds mechanical; alternate with "Before I move on" and "Coming back to".

**Pattern 4:** `The {NUMBER / TREND} is {DIRECTION}, and the reason is {CAUSE}.`
- Enrollment is down four percent, and the reason is the visa timeline, not demand.
- The dip in week six is real, and the reason is a broken ETL job.
- Conversion is up, and the reason is the earlier deadline, not the new campaign.
*Watch out:* Say "down four percent", not "down by four percentage" — and keep "percent" versus "percentage points" straight when comparing rates.

**Pattern 5:** `I don't have {THAT NUMBER} in front of me — I'll {ACTION} by {TIME}.`
- I don't have the vendor spend split in front of me — I'll send it by end of day.
- I don't have that broken out by cohort in front of me — I'll have it in the deck tomorrow.
- I don't have the exact figure in front of me — I'll confirm it before you present on Thursday.
*Watch out:* Never pad this with apology stacking ("Sorry, very sorry, actually I did not check"). One clean sentence plus a deadline is stronger than three sentences of regret.

**Pattern 6:** `That's a fair {CHALLENGE / POINT / QUESTION} — here's {HOW WE HANDLED IT / WHY WE CHOSE IT}.`
- That's a fair challenge — here's why we excluded deferrals.
- That's a fair point — here's how we handled the duplicate records.
- That's a fair question — here's why we didn't use last year's baseline.
*Watch out:* Don't say "fair enough" in response to a critique of your work; it reads as grudging concession rather than engagement.

**Pattern 7:** `In the interest of time, I'll {SKIP / COMPRESS / LEAVE} {CONTENT}.`
- In the interest of time, I'll skip the methodology slides and leave them in the appendix.
- In the interest of time, I'll compress the middle section into one number.
- In the interest of time, I'll leave the regional breakdown for you to read.
*Watch out:* Say it once. Repeating it makes the audience feel the meeting is being rationed, and it signals you over-prepared.

**Pattern 8:** `What I need from you today is {DECISION}, not {ADJACENT THING}.`
- What I need from you today is approval on scope, not a view on the timeline.
- What I need from you today is a yes or no on the second analyst, not a hiring plan.
- What I need today is a steer on priority, not sign-off on the design.
*Watch out:* Keep the "not" clause short and non-defensive; a long one sounds like you're pre-arguing with the room.

**Pattern 9:** `Let me {PARK / HOLD / TAKE} that {OFFLINE / as an action / for slide nine}.`
- Let me take that as an action and come back with numbers.
- Let me park that — I think it's a bigger conversation than this slot.
- Let me hold that; slide nine has exactly this comparison.
*Watch out:* "Take it offline" only works if you actually follow up. Used as a brush-off, people notice by the second time.

**Pattern 10:** `Compared with {BASELINE}, we're {DIRECTION} by {AMOUNT}.`
- Compared with the same week last year, we're ahead by nine percent.
- Compared with the January target, we're short by about sixty applications.
- Compared with the other two programs, we're roughly in line.
*Watch out:* British and European colleagues say "compared with"; US colleagues often say "compared to". Both are fine — just don't say "comparing to last year, we are ahead".

**Pattern 11:** `I'm confident on {X}, less so on {Y}.`
- I'm confident on the direction, less so on the magnitude.
- I'm confident on the domestic numbers, less so on the international split.
- I'm confident this is a real drop, less so on the cause.
*Watch out:* This is a strength move, not a hedge — say it in a level tone. Delivered apologetically it sounds like you don't trust your own work.

**Pattern 12:** `Before I move on — {CHECK}?`
- Before I move on — is this the cut of the data you wanted?
- Before I move on — any objection to the definition?
- Before I move on — Meera, does this answer what you asked last time?
*Watch out:* Only ask if you have time to absorb an answer. Asking and then talking over the response is worse than not asking.

**Pattern 13:** `If {CONDITION}, the one thing I need is {ASK}.`
- If we're out of time, the one thing I need is the go-ahead on the hire.
- If you only act on one slide, make it this one.
- If the budget question stays open, the one thing I need is permission to start the pilot.
*Watch out:* Put this near the *front* if your slot is at risk. Saved for the end, it may never get said.

**Pattern 14:** `Respectfully, I think {THEIR READ} is {WRONG / incomplete}, and I'd like to show you why.`
- Respectfully, I think that read is incomplete, and slide seven shows why.
- Respectfully, I don't think the drop is seasonal, and I can show you the three-year pattern.
- Respectfully, I'd argue the opposite, and it takes one chart to explain.
*Watch out:* "Respectfully" earns you exactly one use per meeting, and only if you follow it immediately with evidence. Without evidence it reads as sarcasm.

**Pattern 15:** `So the actions are — I'll {YOUR TASK}, {NAME} will {THEIR TASK}, by {DATE}.`
- So the actions are — I'll rebuild the cohort view, Ravi will confirm the source table, by Thursday.
- So the actions are — I'll scope the model refresh, you'll get me the budget line, by end of month.
- So the actions are — I'll circulate the corrected number, and Finance will re-run the forecast this week.
*Watch out:* Say the names out loud. "Someone will look into it" is how actions die.

## 4.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 4.1 — Dashboard walkthrough, small internal team

**Setting:** Weekly analytics stand-up with four colleagues. You're demoing the refreshed admissions dashboard on a screen share.

**You:** Thanks for making the time. [#1] Let me share my screen [#10] — can everyone see it? [#9]

**Ravi:** Yep, all good.

**You:** Quick agenda — three things: [#4] the new funnel view, one data quality issue, and a question for you on the default filter. Feel free to jump in as we go. [#7]

**You:** So, this is the dashboard we ship every Monday. [#13] On the x-axis we've got application weeks; on the y-axis, applications. [#14] The dotted line is target; the bars are actual. [#68]

**Meera:** What's that grey series behind them?

**You:** Good question — [#32] the grey is last year, for reference. [#72] Two things stand out here. [#22] One, the trend is up and to the right [#18] since week four. Two — ignore the last bar, it's partial data. [#21]

**Ravi:** And the gap around week nine?

**You:** That gap is where the pipeline failed, not a real drop. [#74] The refresh runs at six, so this is as of last night. [#134] I've logged it, and I'll follow up with the backfill in writing. [#55]

**You:** That brings me to the question for you. [#26] Right now the default filter is all programs. Let me filter this down to your program [#131] — watch what happens when I change the date range. [#132]

**Meera:** That's much more useful as a default, honestly.

**You:** Then I'll take that as an action [#104] and ship it Thursday. Anything I've missed? [#52] ... Right — thanks, everyone, I'll give you the time back. [#56]

**Why this worked:**
- The axes and every non-obvious visual element were decoded in one sentence each, so nobody spent the demo reverse-engineering the chart.
- The data quality gap was surfaced by the presenter before anyone could "catch" it — which converts a weakness into evidence of rigor.
- The meeting closed with a named action and a date, and ended early, which is the cheapest reputation win available in a recurring stand-up.

### Scenario 4.2 — Methodology challenge from a senior stakeholder

**Setting:** Program leadership review. You're presenting a yield dashboard. The Associate Dean questions how yield is calculated.

**You:** Let me start with the headline: [#2] yield is down four percent, and the reason is the visa timeline, not demand.

**Associate Dean:** Hold on. How are you defining yield? Because the number I have from the program office is quite different.

**You:** That's a fair challenge. [#38] Let me tell you how this metric is defined. [#86] To be clear, this counts accepted offers over made offers, not enrollments over applications. [#40]

**Associate Dean:** The program office uses enrollments. So which is right?

**You:** Both are defensible; they answer different questions. If we can't agree the definition, the number is meaningless [#181] — so let's settle it here. If methodology is the sticking point, let's give it thirty seconds now. [#153]

**Associate Dean:** Go on.

**You:** Offers-to-accepts tells you whether our proposition is competitive. Enrollments-to-applications tells you whether the whole funnel works. For today's decision — whether we extend the deadline — the first one is the right lens. I'd rather you challenge it here than after we ship it. [#154]

**Associate Dean:** And if I insisted on the program office definition?

**You:** Here's the same chart with your assumption instead of mine. [#155] The level shifts by about two points; the direction doesn't change. I'm confident on the direction, less so on the magnitude. [#92]

**Associate Dean:** Fine. Then I'll accept the direction.

**You:** Then let me summarize what I heard: [#116] we use offers-to-accepts for competitiveness, I'll add the second definition as a secondary tile, and I'll circulate the definition note by Friday. [#118]

**Why this worked:**
- The challenge was treated as legitimate ("that's a fair challenge") rather than as an attack, which stopped the room from taking sides.
- Instead of arguing definitions abstractly, the presenter showed the alternative live and demonstrated the conclusion was robust to it — that's the single most credibility-building move available with a live dashboard.
- The exchange was timeboxed out loud, so a definitional debate didn't consume the decision slot.

### Scenario 4.3 — Q&A: the number you don't have

**Setting:** End of a 20-minute readout to a leadership group of eight. Questions are coming quickly.

**Faculty Director:** What's the cost per enrolled student for the paid campaigns?

**You:** I don't have that number in front of me. [#35] I've got total spend and total enrollments, but not the attribution split, and dividing one by the other would be misleading. I'd rather check than guess. [#36]

**Faculty Director:** Ballpark?

**You:** I'd be guessing, and a guess from me ends up in someone's board deck. [#146] Let me come back to you on that today. [#37] I can get you a defensible figure by end of day. [#102]

**Faculty Director:** Alright. And how does that compare with our peers?

**You:** That's outside what I've brought today [#41] — and honestly, that one's above my pay grade; I'd want the Dean's view [#144] on which peer set we're benchmarking against. Happy to take that offline. [#42]

**Another attendee:** Can you at least say whether the paid channel is working?

**You:** Let me separate the two questions in there. [#95] On volume, yes — paid contributed about a third of applications. [#24] On efficiency, that's exactly the number I owe you. Directionally, we're fine; [#25] I wouldn't yet stake a budget increase on it.

**Faculty Director:** That's a reasonable answer.

**You:** Let's take one last question, [#53] and then I'll follow up with the cost figure in writing. [#55]

**Why this worked:**
- "I don't have it" was followed immediately by *why* the obvious shortcut would mislead — that turns a gap into a display of judgment rather than a lapse in preparation.
- Every deferral came with a time bound. Deferrals without deadlines are what make people stop trusting presenters.
- The presenter answered the question that *could* be answered (volume) and clearly labeled the part that couldn't (efficiency), so the room got value without getting a fabricated number.

### Scenario 4.4 — Thirty minutes cut to ten, on the spot

**Setting:** You're third on the agenda in a senior committee. The previous item overran. The chair turns to you with ten minutes left.

**Chair:** We're badly over. Can you do yours in ten?

**You:** I can. I've got twelve slides but I'll use four. [#63] The rest is in the appendix. [#64]

**Chair:** Go ahead.

**You:** I'll be direct about the shape: one number, one risk, one ask. If we're out of time, the one thing I need is the go-ahead on the second analyst. [#159]

**You:** The number: applications are up nine percent year on year, but conversion is flat, so net intake lands roughly on target. Compared with the January plan, we're short by about sixty applications. [#79]

**You:** The risk — and I'll be blunt, because the number deserves it: [#174] our reporting depends on one person and one un-versioned SQL job. There's a number on this slide I'm not proud of: [#128] four of the last eleven Monday refreshes failed.

**Chair:** Is that why the deck was late last month?

**You:** Yes. I'll skip the build and go straight to the recommendation. [#157] Slides four to eleven are background — I'll leave those with you. [#158]

**You:** The recommendation is one analyst, six months, funded from the existing vendor line. Here's my recommendation, and here's what I'd need to deliver it. [#112] If we do nothing, [#113] the refresh stays fragile through the admissions peak.

**Chair:** Anyone against?

**You:** Before we move on — [#12 as slide ref: slide two] the ask is on the screen and it's one line. [#199] I need a yes, a no, or a date. [#186]

**Chair:** Yes, in principle. Send me the funding note.

**You:** Done — I'll write that up and circulate by Friday. [#118] Thanks — I'll give the time back. [#56]

**Why this worked:**
- The presenter accepted the cut without negotiating for more time, and announced a new structure in one sentence — which is what makes a chair trust you with the next slot.
- The ask was stated at the *beginning*, not the end, so a further interruption couldn't kill it.
- "A yes, a no, or a date" removed the option of a vague nod, which is how these decisions usually evaporate.

### Scenario 4.5 — Closing with a decision, hostile room

**Setting:** Final ten minutes of a budget review. Two stakeholders are openly skeptical about your data platform proposal. This is the third meeting on it.

**You:** Give me two minutes uninterrupted, then all the pushback you want. [#195]

**Finance lead:** Two minutes.

**You:** This is the third time we've discussed it — let me propose we close it. [#189] The proposal is unchanged: one shared warehouse, one definition layer, retire four spreadsheets. Cost, eleven lakh. Payback, one admissions cycle.

**Finance lead:** We've heard payback claims before and they didn't land.

**You:** I got this wrong last quarter; here's what I changed. [#177] I promised savings from automation and counted hours that nobody actually gave back. This time the saving is a named vendor contract we cancel on day one. It's a line item, not an estimate.

**Program head:** I still think this is a tooling answer to a process problem.

**You:** Can I push back on the premise a little? [#149] I think we're solving a different problem from the one you're describing. [#150] Yours is that people don't trust the numbers. Mine is that there are four numbers to choose from. Fix the second and the first improves on its own.

**Program head:** That's... arguably true.

**You:** Let me leave the ask on the screen while you discuss it. [#166] What would you need to see to be comfortable? [#109]

**Finance lead:** A cancellation date on that vendor contract, in writing.

**You:** You'll have it Monday. Then let's stop presenting and start deciding. [#198] If the answer is no, tell me now and I'll stop the work. [#188]

**Finance lead:** It's a yes, conditional on the vendor note.

**You:** Noted, and thank you — including for the hard questions. [#169] Whatever you decide, I'll make it work — but I'm glad we decided today. [#200]

**Why this worked:**
- Owning the previous quarter's error up front removed the strongest weapon in the room before anyone could use it.
- The reframe ("four numbers to choose from") disagreed with a senior stakeholder without contradicting them personally — the objection was answered, not the person.
- "What would you need to see to be comfortable?" converted a vague objection into a single deliverable, which is what actually unlocks a stuck decision.

## 4.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| Today I will present about the admissions dashboard. | Three things today, and I need a decision on one of them. | States value and the ask instead of announcing a topic. |
| As you can see in the graph, the numbers are increasing. | On the y-axis we've got applications — up nine percent since week four. | Names the axis and the magnitude; "as you can see" adds nothing. |
| This is my next point. | That brings me to the second issue. | Signposts direction rather than just marking a break. |
| Kindly note the highlighted portion. | Look at the shaded band — that's the confidence interval. | Spoken English, and it explains what the reader is looking at. |
| I have not calculated that number, sorry. | I don't have that in front of me — I'll send it by end of day. | Same admission, plus a deadline; no apology stacking. |
| Maybe approximately it could be around forty percent, I think. | Roughly a third. I'd rather check than guess on the exact figure. | One hedge, not four. Stacked hedges read as no confidence. |
| Your question is not related to this presentation. | That's a different question from the one on the slide — but a good one. | Redirects without making the questioner feel corrected. |
| I don't know. | My honest answer is I don't know yet — here's how I'd find out. | Preserves credibility by showing a route to the answer. |
| You are wrong about this. | Respectfully, I think that read is incomplete, and slide seven shows why. | Disagrees with the reading, not the person, and offers evidence. |
| Please do the needful and approve the budget. | What I need from you today is a yes or no on the extra headcount. | Live corporate English; states exactly the decision required. |
| I will try my best to complete it. | You'll have it Monday. | A commitment beats an intention, and people plan around it. |
| Due to time constraints, I am compelled to skip a few slides. | In the interest of time, I'll skip ahead and leave these in the appendix. | Natural register; not apologetic or bureaucratic. |
| Any questions? (asked into silence) | Anything I've missed? | Easier to answer, and it invites gaps rather than challenges. |
| This is only a small issue, nothing serious. | There's a number on this slide I'm not proud of. | Honesty here buys trust for everything else you present. |
| The data is not correct because of the ETL. | That gap is where the pipeline failed, not a real drop. | Explains the *chart* first; the cause second, in plain words. |
| Sir, as per your suggestion I have done the changes. | I've made the change you asked for — here's the new view. | Peer register. Titles and "as per" read as deferential and dated. |
| Let me explain everything from the beginning. | Let me set the context in one line. | Senior audiences want the line, not the history. |
| I think so it will improve next quarter. | I'd expect it to recover next quarter, and here's what I'm watching. | Removes the "I think so" tic and adds a checkable signal. |
| We can discuss this later. | Happy to take that offline — I'll come back to you Thursday. | "Later" is where questions go to die; a date keeps trust. |
| Actually, actually the real reason is different. | The underlying reason is different, and it matters. | "Actually" twice sounds like correction; this sounds like insight. |
| It is not possible to get that data. | That's a bigger ask than it looks — let me scope it and come back. | Keeps the door open and shows you understand the cost. |
| So that's all from my side. Thank you. | I'll stop there and take questions. | Standard, confident handover; "from my side" is a giveaway. |
| Everyone please give your valuable inputs. | Where do you land on this? | Asks for a position, which is what you actually need. |
| I hope this is clear to everybody. | Stop me if the chart isn't landing. | Puts the burden on you, not on them to admit confusion. |
| Shall we conclude the meeting now? | Two quick things and I'll hand back. | Signals the runway instead of asking permission to finish. |

## 4.6 Practice Drills

**Drill 1 — The 10× rep.** Anchor phrase: *"I don't have that number in front of me."* Say each of these aloud, standing, at conversational speed. The goal is a level tone with no rising apology at the end.

1. I don't have that number in front of me.
2. I don't have that in front of me — I'll send it by end of day.
3. I don't have the cohort split in front of me; I'll have it before Thursday.
4. I don't have that broken out by program yet.
5. I don't have a defensible figure for that today.
6. I've got total spend, but not the attribution split.
7. I'd rather check than guess on that one.
8. I could give you a rough number, but it would be misleading.
9. I'd be guessing, and a guess from me ends up in someone's deck.
10. Let me come back to you on that today.

**Drill 2 — Slot swap.** Pattern: `On the {AXIS} we've got {UNIT}; the {ELEMENT} is {MEANING}.` Speak all eight without pausing to translate:

1. x-axis / application weeks / red line / last year
2. y-axis / completion rate / shaded band / confidence interval
3. left axis / headcount / bars / budgeted
4. bottom axis / months / dotted line / target
5. y-axis / applications per day / grey series / three-year average
6. x-axis / days to decision / each dot / one applicant
7. right axis / conversion rate / solid line / this cohort
8. y-axis / cost per lead / green band / acceptable range

**Drill 3 — Cold open.** Respond to each in exactly one spoken sentence. No preamble, no "that's a good question" unless it's doing work.

1. "We're twenty minutes over. Can you do yours in eight?"
2. "How exactly is this metric defined? Because Finance has a different number."
3. "What's the cost per enrolled student?" (You don't have it.)
4. "Isn't this just seasonality?"
5. "So what do you actually need from us?"

**Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt.** Paste this into an AI chat and speak your answers aloud rather than typing full sentences where you can.

```
You are Dr. Annelie Vogt, a German-born Associate Dean of Programs at a
business school. You are numerate, sceptical of dashboards, direct without
being rude, and allergic to vague answers. You have 10 minutes and you have
read nothing in advance.

I am the senior program analytics lead. I am about to present a yield
dashboard and ask you to approve one additional analyst for six months.

Run the meeting in real time, one turn at a time. Do this:
1. Open by telling me my slot has been cut from 30 minutes to 10.
2. Within the first three turns, challenge how I define "yield" and claim the
   program office has a different number.
3. At some point, ask me a specific number I am unlikely to have
   (cost per enrolled student by channel), and press me once for a ballpark.
4. Ask one question that is off-topic (peer benchmarking against INSEAD).
5. Near the end, drift into open discussion instead of deciding.
6. Only give a decision if I explicitly ask for one.

Stay in character. Keep your turns to two or three sentences. Do not coach
me during the roleplay.

When I write "END SCENE", stop and give me feedback in this format:
- Did I lead with the takeaway or bury it? Quote my opening line.
- Did I signpost transitions, or did points run together?
- How did I handle the number I didn't have? Rate 1-5 and quote what I said.
- Did I over-apologize or over-hedge? List every hedge word I used.
- Did I ask for a decision in a single unambiguous sentence? Quote it.
- Three phrases I should have used and didn't.
- Rewrite my closing 30 seconds the way a confident native-level
  presenter would have said it.
```

## 4.7 Master these 10 first

1. **Let me start with the headline.** [#2] — Buys attention in one line.
2. **On the x-axis we've got weeks; on the y-axis, applications.** [#14] — Decodes any chart instantly.
3. **That brings me to the next point.** [#26] — Makes structure audible to listeners.
4. **I don't have that number in front of me.** [#35] — Protects credibility without any apology.
5. **That's a fair challenge.** [#38] — Absorbs pushback, keeps room calm.
6. **Let me come back to you on that today.** [#37] — Turns a gap into commitment.
7. **In the interest of time, I'll skip ahead.** [#44] — Compresses without looking rattled.
8. **What I need from you is a yes or no.** [#49] — Converts presentation into decision.
9. **Let me summarize what I heard.** [#116] — Locks agreement before people leave.
10. **I need a yes, a no, or a date.** [#186] — Kills the vague nod dead.


---

# Chapter 5 — Interviews

> **One-line promise:** You'll be able to run an interview conversation — from the first "thanks for making the time" to the salary number and the follow-up email — sounding like a senior professional who is choosing, not begging.

## 5.1 Why this chapter matters

- Most senior data people don't lose interviews on technical depth. They lose them on **narration**: the answer is correct but shapeless, so the interviewer can't tell what the candidate actually did.
- The classic non-native failure mode here is the **flat monologue**: four minutes of detail, no signposting, no numbers, no check-in. Native-level interviewees constantly signpost ("I'll take this in three parts"), compress ("net effect: refresh went from four hours to twenty minutes"), and check ("does that answer what you were asking?").
- The second failure mode is **over-honesty without framing**. "The project failed because the stakeholder changed the requirement" is true and it sounds like blame-shifting. The same story with "the part I got wrong was X" reads as senior.
- Comp conversations are where politeness turns into money lost. Blunt ("I want 40% more") and bookish ("I would be most grateful if you could kindly reconsider") both fail. The middle register — warm, specific, unembarrassed — is a learnable set of about eight sentences.
- Fix this and two things change: your answers get shorter and land harder, and you start behaving like a peer in the room — asking the hiring manager questions that make them want to sell you the job.

## 5.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first
