## 7.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `Dear {title + surname}, ... {Kind regards / With kind regards},`
- Dear Dr Weber, — thank you for your note of yesterday. ... Kind regards, Dhyanendra
- Dear Ms Bergström, — the revised figures are below. ... Best regards, Dhyanendra
- Dear colleagues, — a short update on the admissions dashboard. ... Kind regards, Dhyanendra
*Watch out:* European email wants a greeting and a sign-off in every message, including the fourth reply in a thread — dropping to bare "Thanks" reads as impatient, not efficient.

**Pattern 2:** `I'm afraid {constraint} — {alternative}.`
- I'm afraid the extract won't be ready before Monday — I can send yesterday's file today.
- I'm afraid we can't include individual scores — an aggregated view is possible.
- I'm afraid Thursday is full — would Friday at 09:00 work instead?
*Watch out:* "I'm afraid" is a refusal marker, not an emotion. Never pair it with an exclamation mark or "very sorry sorry" — one apology only.

**Pattern 3:** `{Understatement adverb} + {negative}` = strong British disagreement
- I'm not entirely convinced by that figure.
- That's slightly concerning, given the deadline.
- The result is somewhat different from what we expected.
*Watch out:* When a British colleague uses this on you, upgrade it two levels: "slightly concerned" means worried, "not entirely convinced" means no.

**Pattern 4:** `To be direct: {plain statement}.`
- To be direct: the deadline isn't realistic with two people.
- To be direct: the source data has never been validated.
- To be direct: I'd rather we didn't promise this to the Dean.
*Watch out:* Announce the shift, then be genuinely plain. Saying "to be direct" and then hedging for three sentences destroys the effect.

**Pattern 5:** `That is not possible, because {reason}. What is possible is {option}.`
- That is not possible, because the field is personal data. What is possible is a banded version.
- That is not possible before the freeze. What is possible is a read-only preview.
- That is not possible with the current licence. What is possible is a single shared workspace.
*Watch out:* This German-style construction is neutral, not rude, but it needs the reason and the alternative — "that is not possible" alone sounds like a door closing.

**Pattern 6:** `Point one is {X}; point two is {Y}; my conclusion is {Z}.`
- Point one is the definition; point two is the ownership; my conclusion is that we pause a week.
- Point one is data quality; point two is timing; my recommendation is a phased release.
- There are three aspects: legal, technical and political. I'll take them in that order.
*Watch out:* French, Belgian and Brussels audiences expect the structure announced before the content. Do not deliver the conclusion first and the reasoning as an afterthought.

**Pattern 7:** `{Consensus verb} + together / with everyone, before {decision}.`
- We should agree this together before we commit to a date.
- Let's hear from everyone before we decide the metric.
- I'd like to check with the other units first, then confirm.
*Watch out:* In Nordic and Dutch settings, a decision announced without this step is treated as invalid, even if you were technically authorised to take it.

**Pattern 8:** `The team {verb} — I {small role}.`
- The team rebuilt the pipeline; I mostly reviewed the logic.
- The credit belongs to Marta and Jonas — I was part of the group.
- It was a joint effort with the admissions office.
*Watch out:* Nordic culture reads "I single-handedly transformed reporting" as a defect, not a strength. State the outcome plainly and let the numbers carry the claim.

**Pattern 9:** `We'd need {safeguard} before {action}, on {legal ground}.`
- We'd need a data-processing agreement before the vendor touches the extract.
- We'd need pseudonymised IDs before this goes into the shared workspace.
- We'd need a documented lawful basis before we add the nationality field.
*Watch out:* Say "personal data", not "PII" (that is American), and "supervisory authority", not "the GDPR police".

**Pattern 10:** `That's subject to {approval body} — {expected timeline}.`
- That's subject to works-council approval, which usually takes four to six weeks.
- That's subject to the DPO's sign-off before the pilot starts.
- That's subject to the steering committee, which next meets on 12 October.
*Watch out:* Never call works-council consultation a formality. Build it into the plan as a real dependency with a real duration.

**Pattern 11:** `I'm on annual leave from {date} to {date}; {name} is covering {scope}.`
- I'm on annual leave from the 4th to the 18th; Marta is covering the daily refresh.
- I'm away for the last two weeks of August; Jonas has full authority on the model.
- We shut down from 24 December to 2 January — nothing will be deployed in that window.
*Watch out:* Say "annual leave" or "holiday", not "vacation", and never imply you will be reachable. In much of Europe, promising to answer email on leave undermines the person covering for you.

**Pattern 12:** `{Number/date} — {unambiguous restatement}.`
- The 9th of May — 09/05, not the ninth of the fifth in US format.
- Twelve point four per cent — with a decimal point, not a comma.
- A hundred and twenty thousand euros — one two zero, then three zeros.
*Watch out:* On mixed calls, always restate dates with the month spelled out and post the number in the chat. One misread separator can move a figure by a factor of a thousand.

**Pattern 13:** `Would you prefer {formal option} or {informal option}?`
- Would you prefer Dr Weber, or is Thomas fine?
- May I call you Anna, or do you prefer Ms Bergström?
- Shall we stay on first-name terms in writing as well?
*Watch out:* Let the more senior or local person offer the downgrade. With German, Austrian and Swiss contacts, keep the title until invited to drop it — and if they use "Sie" in German, keep their surname in English too.

**Pattern 14:** `Let me {slow-down move} for the room.`
- Let me read those figures slowly, for the room.
- Let me put the two definitions in the chat so we're all looking at the same thing.
- Let me summarise in three sentences before we continue.
*Watch out:* Speak clearly, not loudly, and drop idiom rather than vocabulary. "Let's circle back and ballpark it" is harder for a Finnish or Italian listener than "let's estimate it later".

**Pattern 15:** `I'd like {formal record verb} that {fact}.`
- I'd like it noted in the minutes that the risk was flagged in March.
- I'd like to place on record that Legal has not yet approved the transfer.
- I'd like to raise a concern formally, so it's documented.
*Watch out:* This is a heavy move in European meetings — it signals you expect the matter to be reviewed later. Use it once a quarter, not once a week.

## 7.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 7.1 — A German vendor call about a delayed pipeline

**Setting:** A 30-minute call with a Munich-based data vendor. Their nightly feed has been late four times this month.

**You:** Guten Tag, Herr Doktor Weber. I'll continue in English, if that's all right. [#70] I'll get straight to the point, as your time is short. [#155]

**Herr Dr Weber:** Please. We have 25 minutes.

**You:** The feed arrived after 08:00 CET on four days this month. Our refresh runs at 06:00 CET every day, [#56] so the dashboards were empty when the programme directors opened them. To be direct: that isn't workable for us. [#88]

**Herr Dr Weber:** The delay comes from our upstream provider. A 06:00 delivery is not possible.

**You:** Understood. What is possible?

**Herr Dr Weber:** 07:30 CET, guaranteed. Earlier, no.

**You:** Then let's work with 07:30 and move our refresh to 08:00. Just to confirm, we're talking about 07:30 CET, every working day, from 1 October. [#7]

**Herr Dr Weber:** Correct. Not calendar days — working days.

**You:** Good, that's the distinction I wanted. We count that in working days, not calendar days. [#58] Let me put that in writing so we have it on record. [#40] And if the guarantee slips twice in a month, that would need a change request rather than an email chain. [#143]

**Herr Dr Weber:** Agreed. Send the note today.

**You:** I'll circulate it by 17:00. Thank you for the clear answer.

**Why this worked:**
- No small talk, no "hope you're having an awesome week" — the opener named the topic in the first twenty seconds.
- "That is not possible" was accepted as information, and answered with "what is possible?" rather than pushing back on the person.
- Precision was mirrored: exact time, exact date, working days versus calendar days, and a written record.

### Scenario 7.2 — Decoding a British stakeholder

**Setting:** A review with a London-based associate dean about a new ranking-submission dashboard.

**Associate Dean:** Thanks for this. It's an interesting approach. [#81]

**You:** Thank you. Before I take that as approval — am I right in thinking you have reservations? [#21]

**Associate Dean:** Well, I suppose I'm slightly concerned about the faculty-level view. [#122] It could be quite challenging in the current climate. [#123]

**You:** That's helpful, and I'd rather hear it now. Let me be very clear about what I've built [#89] — the faculty view shows publication counts per department, not per individual.

**Associate Dean:** Ah. That's rather better than I expected. [#194] I'd assumed individual names.

**You:** No names anywhere. There may have been a data-protection issue with that, [#41] so we designed it out from the start.

**Associate Dean:** Then my only remaining point is the timing. It might be worth waiting until after the committee meets. [#86]

**You:** I'll read that as: don't release before the committee. Shall I circulate a read-only preview to the three of you first, and hold the wider release?

**Associate Dean:** That would be ideal.

**You:** Fine. Subject to your comments, I'll circulate the final version after the committee. [#108]

**Why this worked:**
- "Interesting approach" and "slightly concerned" were correctly upgraded to real objections instead of being logged as approval.
- "It might be worth ..." was translated into an instruction and read back explicitly, without embarrassing the speaker.
- The reply matched the register — hedged, no defensiveness, no "absolutely, 100 per cent!".

### Scenario 7.3 — A Nordic consensus workshop

**Setting:** A Stockholm-based analytics group deciding a shared definition of "active student". Four people, mostly quiet.

**You:** Thanks for making the time. Before we get into it — I'd rather we agreed the definition first [#36] and left the tooling for another day. Does that suit everyone?

**Anna:** That's sensible.

**You:** So: two candidate definitions are on the table. Rather than my recommending one, what does everyone think before we decide? [#91]

**Jonas:** Both work technically.

**You:** (pause) I get the impression this isn't fully agreed yet. [#181]

**Anna:** The second one changes our retention numbers. That would be visible externally.

**You:** That's exactly the kind of thing I needed. Silence isn't agreement here — may I hear from each of you, briefly? [#182]

**Erik:** Second definition, but with a footnote in the published report.

**Lise:** Agreed, with the footnote.

**You:** Then we have a proposal: definition two, footnoted, effective from Q1 2026 — that's January to March. [#118] I don't want anyone feeling this was decided over their heads, [#168] so I'll write it up and give everyone a chance to comment before Friday. [#169]

**Jonas:** Good. I'd rather have a slow yes than a fast maybe.

**You:** Then we agree on method as well as substance. The definition came from this group, not from me. [#92]

**Why this worked:**
- Silence was treated as unresolved dissent and surfaced, rather than being banked as consent.
- The decision was framed as the group's, with a written comment window — the Nordic legitimacy test.
- No self-promotion: the credit was handed back to the room, which is exactly what raises your standing there.

### Scenario 7.4 — Works council and GDPR on a new dashboard

**Setting:** A German campus. Meeting with HR, the works council chair and the data protection officer about a staff-workload dashboard.

**You:** Thank you all for coming. May I be very frank for a moment? [#146] I've built something that I now think needs redesigning before it goes anywhere.

**Works Council Chair:** That is unusual. Please continue.

**You:** The prototype shows workload per individual member of staff. If I've understood the works council correctly, the pilot needs a formal agreement before any individual-level view exists. [#171]

**Works Council Chair:** Correct. Individual performance monitoring is subject to works-council approval. [#97] Without an agreement, that is not possible. [#14]

**You:** Then let's not spend six weeks negotiating something we may not need. We could offer an aggregated version — no individual scores at all, [#172] minimum group size of five.

**DPO:** What's the lawful basis for the underlying data? [#42]

**You:** Existing timetabling records, already processed for scheduling. Using them for workload analysis would be a new purpose, so we'd need a fresh basis. [#100]

**DPO:** Aggregated to groups of five or more, with no re-identification, and I can work with that. Retention?

**You:** We keep it for twenty-four months, then it's deleted. [#101] Access is strictly on a need-to-know basis [#102] — three people in the programme office.

**Works Council Chair:** With those limits, the works council would not object. Put it in writing.

**You:** I'll circulate the note this week, and I'd like that noted in the minutes [#106] as the agreed scope of the pilot.

**Why this worked:**
- Volunteering the problem, rather than being caught, converted an adversarial meeting into a design session.
- Correct vocabulary — lawful basis, new purpose, retention, need-to-know, works-council approval — signalled that the constraints were understood, not resented.
- Redesigning to the constraint (aggregation, minimum group size) got a yes in one meeting instead of a six-week negotiation.

### Scenario 7.5 — Southern European warmth and the August shutdown

**Setting:** A video call with a Milan-based partner school about a joint intake report, in mid-July.

**Giulia:** Ciao! Finally we speak.

**You:** Giulia, it's good to see you. Before the agenda — how are things with you? [#152] How was your summer so far? [#151]

**Giulia:** Busy, but we go to Puglia in two weeks. And you?

**You:** Two weeks in September. Which brings me neatly to the difficult part. Before the August shutdown, we should freeze the model. [#32]

**Giulia:** Ah. August. In Italy, nothing happens in August.

**You:** That's what I assumed, and I'd rather plan around it than pretend otherwise. I'm on annual leave from the 4th to the 18th of September; [#29] you're away in late July and August. So the realistic windows are next week, or the second half of September.

**Giulia:** Next week is very tight for us.

**You:** Then let's not force it. I'd rather have a slow yes than a fast maybe. [#170] Proposal: I send the draft definitions before you leave, you read them when you're back, and we meet on the 22nd of September — that's 22/09.  [#47]

**Giulia:** That works. And send it as a short document, not twelve slides.

**You:** In brief: three definitions, one page. [#134] Could we fix the date now, while we're both here? [#111]

**Giulia:** Doing it now. And when you come to Milan, we have dinner — properly.

**You:** I'd like that. It was a pleasure to meet you in person in March; [#154] let's repeat it.

**Why this worked:**
- Two minutes of genuine relationship talk before the agenda — skipping it in Milan costs more time than it saves.
- The August shutdown was treated as a fact of the calendar, not an obstacle to be negotiated away.
- The date was fixed live, in unambiguous European format, while both parties were on the call.

## 7.5 Avoid This → Say This

American-style enthusiasm and Indian-English formality both land badly in Europe, for opposite reasons. The first sounds like sales, the second like a 1950s letter.

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| Kindly revert at the earliest. | Could you come back to me by Thursday? | "Revert" means "go back to a previous state" in European business English; the date removes the vagueness. |
| Please do the needful. | Could you approve the request and let me know? | Names the actual action instead of leaving the reader guessing. |
| Awesome! This is amazing! | That's good news, thanks. | Superlatives on routine items make your genuine praise worthless in European ears. |
| I am extremely excited to share this game-changing dashboard! | Here's the new dashboard. It cuts the monthly close from three days to one. | Lets the number do the selling, which is the only argument that works in Germany or the Nordics. |
| This will be a total game changer for you guys. | This should save the programme office roughly two days a month. | Quantified, modest, checkable — the European credibility test. |
| We are 100% confident this is the best solution in the market. | On balance, we think this is the strongest option, and here's why. | Absolute claims trigger suspicion; hedged claims with reasoning survive scrutiny. |
| Hey! Quick one — need this ASAP!! | Dear Anna, could you send the extract by 16:00 today? Many thanks. | Greeting, full sentence, specific time — the baseline European written register. |
| Thanks & Regards, | Kind regards, | The ampersand-and-abbreviation close reads as a mail-merge signature. |
| Respected Sir, | Dear Dr Weber, | Titles-plus-surname is the European formal norm; "Respected Sir" sounds archaic. |
| Please find attached herewith the said report. | I've attached the report. | Six words carry the same information without the legalese. |
| Myself Dhyanendra, I am handling reporting. | I'm Dhyanendra — I look after programme reporting. | Standard self-introduction; "myself X" doesn't exist in European English. |
| Doing the same by EOD today itself. | I'll send it today, before 18:00. | "Itself" and "EOD" don't travel; a clock time does. |
| Let us discuss and finalise on call. | Shall we settle this on Thursday's call? | Sounds like a proposal rather than an instruction. |
| I will update you shortly. | I'll come back to you by tomorrow midday. | "Shortly" means anything from an hour to a fortnight. |
| It is not possible from our side, sorry sorry. | I'm afraid that isn't possible before Monday. | One clean refusal marker beats a doubled apology, which reads as anxiety. |
| Your dashboard has many mistakes. | I've found three discrepancies in the totals — shall I walk you through them? | Counted, specific and collaborative rather than accusatory. |
| Circling back to bubble this up and ballpark it. | Coming back to this: shall we estimate it and raise it with the director? | Idiom-free English is the courtesy that matters most on a pan-European call. |
| Let's touch base offline and take a rain check. | Let's speak separately and postpone the decision. | Non-native listeners lose sports and business idioms completely. |
| We crushed our targets this quarter! | We came in nine per cent above target this quarter. | Achievement language in Europe is factual; hyperbole shifts the focus to you. |
| I single-handedly built the entire reporting stack. | I led the reporting rebuild, with two colleagues on the pipelines. | In Nordic settings especially, credit-sharing raises your status rather than lowering it. |
| Just following up! Just checking in!! Bumping this!! | A gentle reminder about the extract — do let me know if the timing is difficult. | One polite chase; three chases in a week reads as distrust of the recipient. |
| Can you send the PII file? | Could you send the pseudonymised extract? | "PII" is US vocabulary; European reviewers hear "personal data" and expect a safeguard. |
| Don't worry about GDPR, it's an internal report. | Let's confirm the lawful basis before we build it — internal use still counts. | Dismissing compliance ends the conversation; owning it keeps the project alive. |
| Are you working during the holidays? I'll ping you anyway. | Who's covering while you're away? | Respects the handover culture instead of quietly ignoring it. |
| As per my below mail, please revert urgently. | As mentioned in my message of Tuesday, could you confirm by Friday? | Removes "as per", "below mail" and "revert" in one move, and dates both ends. |

## 7.6 Practice Drills

**Drill 1 — The 10× rep**

Anchor phrase: **"I'm afraid that won't be possible before Monday."** [#13] Say each of these aloud, keeping your voice flat and unapologetic — no rising tone, no second apology.

1. I'm afraid that won't be possible before Monday.
2. I'm afraid that won't be possible before the freeze.
3. I'm afraid the extract won't be ready before Wednesday.
4. I'm afraid we can't include individual names.
5. I'm afraid the licence doesn't cover external sharing.
6. I'm afraid Legal hasn't approved that transfer yet.
7. I'm afraid I don't have the mandate to approve that.
8. I'm afraid that sits outside my remit.
9. I'm afraid August is unrealistic for both teams.
10. I'm afraid that would need works-council approval first.

**Drill 2 — Slot swap**

Pattern: `That is not possible, because {reason}. What is possible is {option}.` Speak all eight, aloud, at a slow German pace.

1. ... because the field is personal data. What is possible is a banded version.
2. ... because the vendor contract ends in June. What is possible is a three-month extension.
3. ... because the works council hasn't been consulted. What is possible is an aggregated pilot.
4. ... because the source refreshes weekly. What is possible is a Tuesday snapshot.
5. ... because the data can't leave the EEA. What is possible is a Frankfurt-hosted workspace.
6. ... because we're in the August shutdown. What is possible is a September start.
7. ... because the definitions differ between systems. What is possible is one agreed metric.
8. ... because I'm on annual leave that week. What is possible is a handover to Marta.

**Drill 3 — Cold open**

Answer each in exactly one sentence, out loud, in the register named.

1. A German colleague opens with: "Your numbers are wrong." (Respond without defensiveness.)
2. A British dean says: "That's certainly an interesting approach." (Surface the real objection.)
3. A Dutch analyst says: "This dashboard is badly designed." (Absorb it and stay warm.)
4. A Swedish manager's team goes silent after your proposal. (Force a genuine consensus check.)
5. A French director asks: "How is your answer structured?" (Announce your structure in one sentence.)

**Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt**

```
You are Frau Dr Katharina Vogel, Head of Controlling at a German business
school in Mannheim. You are 52, precise, and you dislike vagueness and
small talk. You have 20 minutes. You speak fluent but German-accented
English: short sentences, no idiom, and you say "that is not possible"
neutrally when something cannot be done. You do not soften refusals and
you do not use superlatives.

Context: I am the analytics lead at a partner school. I want your team to
send us programme-level cost data monthly so we can benchmark two joint
degrees. Your concerns, in order: (1) the works council has not been
consulted about anything touching staff cost lines, (2) the lawful basis
for sharing across institutions is unclear to you, (3) our last two
delivery promises slipped by three weeks.

Rules for you:
- Open with one sentence of greeting, then go straight to the agenda.
- Interrupt me if I use vague quantifiers ("soon", "shortly", "a lot").
- Interrupt me if I use hype words ("awesome", "game-changing", "huge").
- Ask for exact dates, exact figures and exact scope every time.
- If I ask for something impossible, say "That is not possible" and give
  the reason, but do not offer an alternative unless I ask for one.
- End the call at 20 minutes whether or not we have finished.

Run the meeting turn by turn. Wait for my reply after each of your turns.
```

Afterwards, ask for feedback in these exact terms:

```
Now score me out of 5 on each, with one concrete example of each from
the transcript:
1. Did I open efficiently, without unnecessary small talk?
2. Did I ever use vague time or quantity language? List every instance.
3. Did I ever use American-style hype? List every instance.
4. When you refused, did I ask "what is possible?" instead of arguing?
5. Did I use compliance vocabulary correctly (lawful basis, purpose,
   retention, works-council approval)?
6. Rewrite my three weakest sentences as a German-registered
   professional would have said them.
```

## 7.7 Master these 10 first

1. **Kind regards,** [#2] — The safe European sign-off, everywhere.
2. **I'm afraid that won't be possible before Monday.** [#13] — Refuse cleanly, once, with a date.
3. **That is not possible, because ...** [#14] — Neutral constraint, German-style, no offence.
4. **Just so I've understood you correctly, ...** [#20] — Catches misunderstandings before they cost weeks.
5. **I'm not entirely convinced by that figure.** [#37] — Disagreement that British ears respect.
6. **What's the lawful basis for that field?** [#42] — One question that prevents legal rework.
7. **That's subject to works-council approval.** [#97] — Names a real dependency, early.
8. **What does everyone think before we decide?** [#91] — Buys legitimacy in Nordic and Dutch rooms.
9. **I'm on annual leave from the 4th to the 18th.** [#29] — Correct absence register, no apology.
10. **The credit belongs to the team, not to me.** [#195] — Understatement that raises your standing.

## 7.8 Same sentence, three countries

One message, three registers. Read each row across: the content is identical, the packaging is not. The fourth column tells you what to actually do when you receive the phrasing in that column.

| Situation | UK register | German register | Nordic register (SE/DK/NO/FI) |
|---|---|---|---|
| The deadline is impossible | I'm afraid the timeline might be a little tight. | That deadline is not possible. We need three more weeks. | We should look at this together — I don't think three weeks is realistic for the team. |
| Their figures are wrong | I wonder whether the totals quite reconcile. | The totals are wrong. Row 412 is duplicated. | I found a difference in the totals. Shall we check the join together? |
| You disagree with the boss | With respect, I'd question whether that's the right metric. | I disagree. Completion rate is the correct metric, not headcount. | I see it differently, and others may too — could we hear the room? |
| You want to say no to a request | That could be quite challenging in the current climate. | No. That is outside the agreed scope. | That's difficult for us. Could we discuss what we drop instead? |
| Announcing your own good result | The new pipeline seems to be holding up rather well. | The pipeline now runs in eleven minutes instead of ninety. | The team rebuilt the pipeline; it now runs in eleven minutes. |
| Chasing a late deliverable | Just a gentle nudge on the extract, whenever you have a moment. | The extract was due on Tuesday. When will it arrive? | Any news on the extract? Let me know if something's blocking it. |
| Praising a colleague's work | That's really rather good. | The analysis is correct and well documented. | Good work — and thanks to everyone who contributed. |
| Refusing to share personal data | I'm not sure we'd be entirely comfortable sharing that. | That is not permitted. There is no lawful basis. | We shouldn't share that. Let's ask the DPO and decide together. |
| Ending a meeting on time | Shall we perhaps draw things to a close? | It is 15:00. We finish now. | We're at time — anything anyone must raise before we stop? |
| Escalating a problem | It might be worth mentioning this to the director. | I will inform the director today. | I'd like to tell the director, so nobody is surprised. Is that all right? |
| Asking for a decision | Do we feel able to take a view on this? | We decide now: option A or option B? | Where does everyone stand? I'd like a decision we all support. |
| Going on holiday | I'm off for a couple of weeks from the 4th, if that's all right. | I am on leave from 4 to 18 August. Marta is my deputy. | I'm away from the 4th — Marta is covering, and she has full authority. |

**How to use this table**

- If you are the sender, pick the column of the person receiving the message, not the column you find comfortable.
- If you are the receiver of a UK-column phrase, translate it up: "a little tight" means impossible, "quite challenging" means no, "I wonder whether" means it is wrong.
- If you are the receiver of a German-column phrase, translate it down: it is about the work, not about you, and no relationship damage is intended or recorded.
- If you are the receiver of a Nordic-column phrase, notice the invitation: "shall we", "together", "the room". Answering only to the senior person, or deciding alone afterwards, is the mistake.

## Part IV — The Technical Translation


---

# Chapter 8 — Explaining ML and AI

> **One-line promise:** You will be able to explain any model you have built — its intuition, its accuracy, its limits, its risks — to people who will never read a line of your code, and turn that explanation into a decision they can actually make.

## 8.1 Why this chapter matters

- The people who fund your work — deans, program directors, finance, marketing, vendors — will never read your notebook. They will judge the model entirely by the sixty seconds in which you describe it. That sixty seconds is the deliverable.
- The classic non-native failure here is not grammar. It is altitude. You open at the level you find interesting ("we used stratified k-fold with a gradient-boosted ensemble") instead of the level they can act on ("it ranks students by risk; the top 200 get a call"). The room switches off in the first ten seconds and never comes back.
- The second failure is defensive precision. Asked "is it accurate?", you say "well, it depends on the metric, and precision at threshold 0.6 is 0.41..." — all true, and it reads as evasion. Leaders hear hedging as hiding.
- The third failure is the opposite: over-claiming to please the room. Say "yes, AI can do that" once about something it cannot do, and every honest limitation you state afterwards sounds like an excuse.
- Fix the altitude and three things change: you get invited to the decision meeting rather than the status meeting, you can refuse an unrealistic AI request without looking uncooperative, and when a model fails in production — it will — you are the person who explains it rather than the person being explained.

## 8.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)
Use these until they're automatic.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | At a high level, it does one thing: ... | Opening any model explanation | Neutral |
| 2 | Let me start with the intuition, then the mechanics. | Structuring an explanation | Neutral |
| 3 | Here's the short version. | Someone senior has two minutes | Neutral |
| 4 | Think of it as a very good pattern-matcher. | First-line plain-language framing | Neutral |
| 5 | In plain terms, it ranks students by risk. | Replacing jargon with the outcome | Neutral |
| 6 | It's a prediction, not a fact. | Setting expectations early | Neutral |
| 7 | What it gives you is a probability, not a yes or no. | Correcting binary thinking | Neutral |
| 8 | We set the cut-off — the model doesn't. | Explaining thresholds | Neutral |
| 9 | Out of every 100 we flag, about 30 actually drop out. | Making precision concrete | Neutral |
| 10 | Accuracy on its own is misleading here. | Pushing back on one number | Neutral |
| 11 | The real question is what a wrong answer costs us. | Reframing to business terms | Neutral |
| 12 | There are two ways to be wrong, and they cost different amounts. | Introducing error types | Neutral |
| 13 | A false positive means we call someone who was fine. | Explaining precision, no jargon | Neutral |
| 14 | A false negative means we miss someone who needed help. | Explaining recall, no jargon | Neutral |
| 15 | Precision is: when we flag someone, how often are we right? | Defining precision in one line | Neutral |
| 16 | Recall is: of the people who did drop out, how many did we catch? | Defining recall in one line | Neutral |
| 17 | You can push one up, but the other comes down. | The trade-off in plain words | Neutral |
| 18 | Training is teaching it; inference is asking it. | Training vs inference, simply | Neutral |
| 19 | It learns from the past and assumes next year looks similar. | Naming the core assumption | Neutral |
| 20 | Features are just the columns we let it look at. | Demystifying "features" | Neutral |
| 21 | It doesn't know why — only what usually goes together. | Blocking causal misreading | Neutral |
| 22 | Let me put a number on that. | Moving from vague to specific | Neutral |
| 23 | Compared to what we do today, it's about twelve points better. | Framing against a baseline | Neutral |
| 24 | The baseline is what we should be measuring against. | Introducing the comparison | Neutral |
| 25 | What would you do differently if you had this score? | Forcing the decision question | Neutral |
| 26 | If nobody acts on it, the model has no value. | Linking model to action | Neutral |
| 27 | That's a data problem, not a model problem. | Redirecting a complaint | Neutral |
| 28 | We don't capture that anywhere yet. | Explaining a missing input | Neutral |
| 29 | It can't answer that — it was never trained on it. | Refusing a bad question | Neutral |
| 30 | That's outside anything it has seen. | Out-of-scope request | Neutral |
| 31 | Give me the business question first, and I'll tell you if it's a model problem. | Someone starts with the solution | Neutral |
| 32 | Honestly, a simple rule gets you most of the way. | Deflating over-engineering | Casual |
| 33 | This doesn't need AI — it needs a clean report. | Saying no early | Neutral |
| 34 | Let me set expectations on timing. | Before anyone commits a date | Neutral |
| 35 | Two weeks to a prototype, two months to something you can trust. | Timeline framing | Neutral |
| 36 | The modeling is the fast part; the data work is the slow part. | Explaining where time goes | Neutral |
| 37 | I'd rather under-promise on this one. | Managing pressure to commit | Neutral |
| 38 | I can't put a number on it until I've seen the data. | Refusing a premature estimate | Neutral |
| 39 | Overfitting means it memorized the answers instead of learning the pattern. | Explaining overfitting | Neutral |
| 40 | It looked excellent in testing and fell apart in the real world. | Describing a failed model | Neutral |
| 41 | Drift is the world changing while the model stays the same. | Defining drift | Neutral |
| 42 | The model didn't break — the population changed. | After a live degradation | Neutral |
| 43 | Models age. This one needs retraining about twice a year. | Setting maintenance expectations | Neutral |
| 44 | We monitor it the way we monitor a pipeline — it needs check-ups. | Framing ongoing cost | Neutral |
| 45 | Bias here means it performs worse for one group than another. | Defining bias operationally | Neutral |
| 46 | We checked whether it behaves the same across programs. | Reporting a fairness check | Neutral |
| 47 | It learned our past decisions, including the bad ones. | Explaining inherited bias | High-stakes |
| 48 | A hallucination is the model inventing something, fluently. | Defining hallucination | Neutral |
| 49 | It's optimized to sound right, not to be right. | Explaining LLM failure mode | Neutral |
| 50 | It has no way of knowing what it doesn't know. | Explaining false confidence | Neutral |
| 51 | That's why there's a human in the loop. | Introducing the control | Neutral |
| 52 | Nothing goes to a student without a person signing off. | Naming the safeguard | Neutral |
| 53 | Let me walk you through one case, end to end. | Making it concrete | Neutral |
| 54 | Would an example help more than the theory? | Reading the room | Neutral |
| 55 | Stop me if I'm going too deep. | Calibrating altitude live | Casual |
| 56 | Is that the level of detail you wanted? | Checking mid-explanation | Neutral |
| 57 | Happy to go deeper — otherwise I'll leave it there. | Closing an explanation | Neutral |
| 58 | Short answer: yes, with one caveat. | Answering a yes/no question well | Neutral |
| 59 | Let me answer that in two parts. | Structuring a messy question | Neutral |
| 60 | So the practical implication is ... | Landing the "so what" | Neutral |

## 8.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `At a high level, {system} does one thing: {outcome in business words}.`
- At a high level, it does one thing: it ranks students by how likely they are to withdraw.
- At a high level, the pipeline does one thing: it gives every program a same-day headcount.
- At a high level, the tool does one thing: it drafts the first version so faculty edit instead of write.
*Watch out:* "One thing" must be an outcome, not a technique. "It applies gradient boosting to enrollment features" is still the inside of the machine, not the thing it does for the listener.

**Pattern 2:** `Think of it as {familiar thing}. Where that breaks down is {limit}.`
- Think of it as autocomplete that has read most of the internet. Where that breaks down is arithmetic.
- Think of it as a very well-read intern. Where that breaks down is that the intern never says "I don't know".
- Think of it as a smoke alarm. Where that breaks down is that it can't tell you what's burning.
*Watch out:* Always ship the analogy with its expiry date in the same breath. An analogy without a stated limit becomes policy within a week — someone will quote your "brain" metaphor back to you in a budget meeting.

**Pattern 3:** `Let me start with the intuition, then the mechanics — stop me when you've got what you need.`
- Let me start with the intuition, then the mechanics. Intuition first: it's looking for students who look like last year's leavers.
- I'll give you the intuition, then the detail. The intuition is that we're re-ranking, not deciding.
- Two levels here: the idea, then how we built it. Tell me when the idea is enough.
*Watch out:* Do not announce two levels and then deliver only the technical one. If you say "intuition first", the next sentence must contain no acronym at all.

**Pattern 4:** `Out of every {N} we {flag / recommend}, about {k} are {real outcome}.`
- Out of every 100 we flag, about 30 actually withdraw.
- Out of every 20 applications it recommends for review, roughly 13 are ones we'd have picked anyway.
- Out of every 50 alerts, we expect around 8 to be genuine data errors.
*Watch out:* Use whole people, not decimals. "0.31 precision" and "31 out of 100" are the same fact; only one of them gets a reaction in the room.

**Pattern 5:** `There are two ways to be wrong here: {FP consequence} or {FN consequence}. Which costs us more?`
- Two ways to be wrong: we call a student who was fine, or we miss a student who wasn't. Which costs us more?
- We can waste an advisor's hour, or we can lose a student. Those aren't equal.
- Either we over-invite to the workshop, or we under-invite. Tell me which mistake you prefer.
*Watch out:* End on the question. If you answer it yourself, you have quietly taken a policy decision that belongs to the program director — and you will own the consequences alone.

**Pattern 6:** `That's a {data / definition / process} problem, not a model problem.`
- That's a definition problem, not a model problem — we have three definitions of "enrolled".
- That's a data-collection problem; no algorithm recovers a field nobody fills in.
- That's a process problem: the score arrives after the decision is already made.
*Watch out:* Say it once, calmly, and immediately follow with what you propose. Repeated without a next step, it sounds like blame-shifting rather than diagnosis.

**Pattern 7:** `{Impressive number} sounds strong until you look at {baseline}.`
- Ninety-two percent sounds strong until you see that only eight percent withdraw.
- The 0.87 AUC sounds strong until you compare it to the counselor's own shortlist.
- A 40% response rate sounds strong until you notice last year's campaign hit 38%.
*Watch out:* Deliver this without triumph. You are correcting a number, not a person — "sounds strong until" keeps the credit with whoever brought it in.

**Pattern 8:** `It's not {overclaim} — it's {accurate description}.`
- It's not deciding anything — it's sorting a list.
- It's not understanding the essay — it's recognizing patterns in the words.
- It's not learning from every conversation — it's frozen until we retrain it.
*Watch out:* Keep the second half generous. "It's not intelligent, it's just statistics" makes the work sound trivial; "it's not reasoning — it's very good pattern recognition, which is enough for this job" defends both truth and value.

**Pattern 9:** `The model {ranks / flags / drafts}; {human body} decides.`
- The model flags; the advising team decides.
- The system drafts the summary; the program director signs it.
- The score orders the queue; the committee still reviews every case.
*Watch out:* Only say this if it is architecturally true. If the "decision" is a rubber stamp on 4,000 rows a week, a regulator will call it an automated decision no matter what you named it.

**Pattern 10:** `I can build it. What I'd rather do is {cheaper alternative}, because {reason}.`
- I can build it. What I'd rather do is fix the intake form, because that's where the errors start.
- I can build the chatbot. I'd rather rewrite the twelve FAQ pages it would be reading from.
- We could train a model for this. A sorted list and two filters would answer it by Friday.
*Watch out:* Lead with capability, not reluctance. "We can't do that" gets you overruled by someone who thinks you mean "I don't want to"; "I can build it, and here's why I wouldn't" gets you listened to.

**Pattern 11:** `Let me be straight about the limits, and then about what it does well.`
- Let me be straight about the limits first, then the upside — it's weakest on first-year international students.
- Two honest caveats, then the good news.
- Here's where I wouldn't trust it; here's where it beats what we do today.
*Watch out:* Limits first, strengths second — never the reverse. Ending on the weakness leaves the room with doubt; ending on the strength after full disclosure leaves them with calibrated confidence.

**Pattern 12:** `Let's agree now what "not working" looks like: if {metric} is below {threshold} by {date}, we {action}.`
- Let's agree now: if precision is under 25% by March, we switch it off.
- If it hasn't beaten the counselor shortlist by the end of term one, we stop and say so.
- If drift alerts fire twice in a quarter, it goes back for retraining automatically.
*Watch out:* Get this agreed before launch, in writing. Proposing a kill-criterion after a model starts failing reads as damage control, not governance.

**Pattern 13:** `Fair challenge. {Evidence}. Where you're right is {concession}.`
- Fair challenge. We tested both on held-out data. Where you're right is that I haven't checked calibration by cohort.
- That's a reasonable objection. Here's the validation run. And yes — the training window is short.
- Good catch. The benchmark holds. Your point about the January data stands, though.
*Watch out:* The concession must be real and specific. A fake concession ("you're right that this is complex") tells a technical skeptic you weren't listening, and the next challenge will be harder.

**Pattern 14:** `So the decision in front of you is {A} or {B}. My recommendation is {A}, and the risk I'm accepting is {risk}.`
- So it's launch in shadow mode now, or wait a term for cleaner data. I'd launch in shadow, and accept a noisier first month.
- Two options: buy the vendor tool, or build it in-house by June. I'd buy, and accept the lock-in.
- Either we tune for recall and call more students, or tune for precision and call fewer. I'd tune for recall.
*Watch out:* Name the risk you are accepting out loud. A recommendation with no stated downside sounds like sales, and senior people discount it automatically.

**Pattern 15:** `{Fast thing} in {short time}; {trustworthy thing} in {longer time}.`
- A prototype in two weeks; something you can put in front of the Dean in two months.
- A working demo by Friday; a monitored, documented model by end of quarter.
- Numbers you can look at next week; numbers you can quote externally in April.
*Watch out:* Never give a single date for both. One number invites the listener to hear the short one and plan for the trustworthy one.

## 8.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 8.1 — "It's 92% accurate — why isn't it live?"

**Setting:** Monthly analytics review. The Associate Dean has seen the accuracy figure in a slide and wants the student-retention model deployed this term.

**Associate Dean:** Your deck says 92% accurate. That sounds finished. What's holding it up?

**You:** Let me answer that in two parts. [#59] The 92% is real — and it's the wrong number to decide on. Ninety-two percent sounds strong until you see that only eight percent of our students withdraw. [#94]

**Associate Dean:** Meaning?

**You:** Meaning a model that predicts "nobody withdraws", every single time, also scores 92%. [#95] It would be accurate and completely useless. That number is technically true and practically useless. [#96]

**Associate Dean:** All right. So what should I be looking at?

**You:** Two questions. When we flag a student, how often are we right? [#15] And of the students who actually withdrew, how many did we catch? [#16] Right now the answers are 31 out of 100, and 68 out of 100.

**Finance Director:** Thirty-one percent right sounds terrible.

**You:** It's better than it sounds — but let me convert that into people. [#98] On 5,000 students, at the current threshold, that's roughly 400 outreach calls a term that go to students who were never at risk. [#99] The advising team has capacity for about 250.

**Associate Dean:** So it's a capacity problem, not an accuracy problem.

**You:** Exactly — and that's your call, not mine. My job is to make the trade-off clear. [#103] I can tune it to flag fewer students and be right more often, and we'll miss more real cases. Trading precision for recall is a business choice, not a technical one. [#102]

**Associate Dean:** What would you do?

**You:** I'd run it in shadow mode for a term: it predicts, we don't act. [#166] We compare its list against what the advisors would have done anyway. And let's agree now what "not working" looks like [#170] — if it hasn't beaten the advisor shortlist by March, we switch it off. [#168]

**Associate Dean:** Fine. Bring the threshold options to the next meeting.

**You:** I'll bring three at three risk levels. [#104] What I need from you at that meeting is a decision on the threshold. [#107]

**Why this worked:**
- The accuracy number was never called wrong — it was reframed against a base rate, so nobody had to be embarrassed.
- The statistic became 400 phone calls against 250 people of capacity, which converted a metric argument into a resourcing decision the Dean could actually make.
- The trade-off was explicitly handed back upward, with a pre-agreed kill criterion, so the analyst holds the method and the Dean holds the risk.

### Scenario 8.2 — "Can we add AI to the application portal?"

**Setting:** The Marketing Director wants an AI feature announced before the admissions campaign. There is no defined problem behind the request.

**Marketing Director:** Competitors are advertising AI-powered admissions. Can we add AI to the portal? Something with ChatGPT.

**You:** Let me push back gently on the framing. [#121] What problem would the AI actually be solving for an applicant? [#123]

**Marketing Director:** Applicants email us a hundred times asking the same things. Fees, deadlines, document formats.

**You:** Good — that's a real problem, and it's answerable. But notice it isn't a prediction problem. It's a "our information is scattered across twelve pages" problem.

**Marketing Director:** So can't we just use ChatGPT for it?

**You:** Partly — and here's exactly where it breaks. [#73] Out of the box, it doesn't look anything up unless we give it something to look at. [#63] It would answer confidently about our fee structure, and it would be confidently wrong, because it's optimized to sound right, not to be right. [#49]

**Marketing Director:** Then what's the version that works?

**You:** We hand it our documents before it answers — that's all retrieval-augmented generation means. [#64] Retrieval finds the right paragraph in the handbook; the model writes it in plain English. [#65] And it shows the source link every time. If it can't cite something in our handbook, it shouldn't answer at all. [#67]

**Marketing Director:** Can it also tell applicants their chances of admission?

**You:** No — and I'd want to be firm on that one. This model must never be used for individual decisions. [#176] The moment it says "your chances are low", we've made an admissions statement we can't defend, and we'd want the ethics committee involved before any applicant sees it. [#179]

**Marketing Director:** And the timeline? Campaign launches in six weeks.

**You:** A working demo in two weeks; something you can put in front of applicants in two months. [Pattern 15] The modeling is the fast part; cleaning up the twelve source pages is the slow part. [#36] Honestly — I can build the assistant. What I'd rather do first is rewrite those twelve pages, because that fixes the emails whether or not we ship the bot. [#126]

**Marketing Director:** That's less exciting.

**You:** It is. But if the bot reads bad pages, we've just automated the confusion — garbage in, fluent garbage out. Give me three weeks on the content and you'll get a better demo for the same launch date.

**Why this worked:**
- The request was never refused outright; it was converted from "add AI" into a specific, solvable information problem.
- The ChatGPT question got a real answer — partly yes, with the exact failure point named — instead of a defensive lecture.
- The hard boundary (no admission-chance predictions) was stated once, with a governance route attached, so it read as protection rather than obstruction.

### Scenario 8.3 — Explaining drift after a live failure

**Setting:** The retention model has been flagging almost nobody since January. A program director noticed. This is the post-incident meeting.

**Program Director:** For six weeks the model told us everything was fine. We lost eleven students. What happened?

**You:** Let me explain what went wrong in three sentences. [#148] In January the admissions criteria changed and we started taking a different intake profile. The model was still looking for students who resembled last year's leavers. The past stopped predicting the future. [#150]

**Program Director:** So the model broke.

**You:** The model didn't break — the population changed. [#42] That's what we call drift: the world moves while the model stays frozen. [#41] It kept doing exactly what it was trained to do, on students it had never seen.

**Program Director:** Why did it take nine weeks to notice?

**You:** Because we were monitoring the wrong thing. We watched the output — how many flags per week — and flags dropping looked like good news. We weren't watching whether the inputs had shifted. This is a monitoring gap, not a maths error. [#151] We should have caught it in week two; we caught it in week nine. [#152] I'll own that one — the monitoring was mine. [#155]

**Registrar:** Will it happen again?

**You:** Something like it will, and that's normal — models age. [#43] What changes is how fast we see it. Here's what I'm putting in place. [#153] From now on we alert when the inputs shift, not just when the output looks odd. [#154] Second, this model gets retrained twice a year on a fixed calendar, not when someone remembers. Third, any change to admissions criteria comes to us as a change notification — that's a process fix, not a technical one.

**Program Director:** Should we be using it at all?

**You:** Yes, with a narrower claim. Let me be straight about the limits, then about what it does well. [#137] It is not reliable in the first term after an intake change. Outside that window, it caught 68 out of 100 leavers against 40 for the manual process. [#116] Those limits make it a tool, not an oracle. [#139]

**Program Director:** And this term?

**You:** This term we run it alongside the advisor list and trust the advisors where they disagree. I'd rather narrow the claim than lose your confidence in the whole thing.

**Why this worked:**
- The three-sentence cause came before any defense, so the room got its answer before it got the context.
- Ownership was specific ("the monitoring was mine") rather than either vague apology or blame on the admissions office.
- The recovery ended with a narrowed but genuine claim plus a real comparison number, which rebuilt confidence instead of asking for it.

### Scenario 8.4 — Defending a model choice to a technical skeptic

**Setting:** A newly hired data scientist, in an open review, questions why the team shipped logistic regression instead of a boosted ensemble.

**Data Scientist:** Logistic regression in 2026? A boosted model would beat this easily. It feels like we're leaving performance on the table.

**You:** Fair challenge. Let me show you the validation. [#161] I benchmarked it against gradient boosting on a held-out year — the gap wasn't meaningful. [#159] AUC 0.79 against 0.81, and it flipped depending on the split.

**Data Scientist:** Two points is still two points.

**You:** It is. And the complex model gained two points and cost us all explainability. [#158] This score goes to a student-affairs committee that has to justify individual outreach decisions. If we can't explain it, we can't defend it. [#87]

**Data Scientist:** SHAP values are explanations.

**You:** For us, yes. Not for the room I have to sit in. The question there won't be "how accurate" — it'll be "how did you decide". [#88] I need a committee member to say out loud, "attendance and two missed assessments moved this student up the list." That sentence is worth more than two AUC points.

**Data Scientist:** That feels like optimizing for politics.

**You:** That's the objection I worried about too. [#162] Here's where you're right, though: I haven't checked calibration by cohort, and the international first-years are the group I'd expect to break. If the boosted model is materially better there, that's a real argument and I'd change the choice.

**Data Scientist:** I could run that this week.

**You:** Please do. Show me a case where it fails and I'll take it seriously. [#160] And if you'd approach the whole thing differently, I'd genuinely like to see it. [#163] I'd rather be corrected here than in front of the Dean. [#164]

**Data Scientist:** Fair enough. What's the bar for switching?

**You:** Let's set it now: a three-point AUC gain that holds across two splits, plus an explanation I can put in one sentence for a committee. Beat that, and we switch. Otherwise — I'd rather have a model we understand than one that wins a leaderboard. [#200]

**Why this worked:**
- The challenge was answered with evidence in the first reply, not with seniority, which kept it a technical conversation.
- A real, specific concession (calibration by cohort) turned the skeptic from an opponent into the person running the next experiment.
- The switching criterion was defined openly, so the disagreement now has a resolution path instead of becoming a standing grudge.

### Scenario 8.5 — Explaining an agent to a vendor selection committee

**Setting:** Procurement, IT, and two program directors are evaluating a vendor's "autonomous AI assistant" for student services.

**Procurement Lead:** They're calling it an autonomous agent. What does that actually mean for us?

**You:** In plain terms: an agent is a model that's allowed to act, not just talk. [#68] A chatbot answers a question. An agent can send the email, update the record, and cancel the appointment. The moment it can act, the risk profile changes completely. [#69]

**IT Director:** Their demo was flawless.

**You:** Demos are built on the happy path. [#81] That's a demo, not a deployment. [#80] I'd want to test it on our messy cases — deferred students, name changes, dual registrations. [#83]

**Program Director:** What's your main worry?

**You:** Two. First, hallucination — the model inventing something, fluently. [#48] In a chat window that's an annoyance. In an agent that sends emails to students, it's a letter from us that we never wrote. Second, accountability: who is accountable when the model is wrong? [#85]

**Procurement Lead:** What do we ask them?

**You:** Three questions. What's the failure mode when it's wrong? [#84] Does every action get logged so it can be audited later? [#90] And can we explain one decision to a student who challenges it? [#86] If they answer those three well, they're a serious vendor.

**IT Director:** And the data question — they say they'll train on our data.

**You:** That phrase needs unpicking. Fine-tuning changes its style; retrieval changes its facts. [#70] For our use case we don't want training at all — we're not training a model on our data, we're pointing it at our data. [#71] And I want confirmation that nothing leaves our tenant, in writing, in the contract. [#72]

**Program Director:** Are you against it?

**You:** Not at all. Let me separate what's proven from what's promising. [#79] Drafting replies from our handbook — proven, and it would save the office real hours. Taking actions on student records unsupervised — promising, and not this year. My recommendation is we buy the drafting capability with actions disabled, and accept that we revisit in twelve months. [#106]

**Procurement Lead:** That's a workable position.

**You:** One more thing for the contract: there's an off-switch, and we should know who holds it. [#169] If it's the vendor and not us, that's a different conversation.

**Why this worked:**
- "Talk versus act" gave a non-technical committee a single distinction that carried the entire risk argument.
- The three vendor questions were handed over as a usable script, which made the analyst the committee's asset rather than its blocker.
- The recommendation split the product into a proven part and a promising part, so enthusiasm was preserved while exposure was cut.

## 8.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| We used a gradient-boosted classifier with SMOTE. | At a high level, it ranks students by risk of withdrawing. | Opens at decision altitude, not implementation altitude. |
| The model is 92% accurate. | It's right 92% of the time, but only 8% of students withdraw — so that number flatters it. | Pre-empts the wrong conclusion instead of inviting it. |
| Precision is 0.31 and recall is 0.68. | Of every 100 we flag, 31 really are at risk; we catch about two-thirds of real cases. | Whole people beat decimals in a room full of non-analysts. |
| It's impossible. | We can't predict that with what we currently collect — here's what we'd need. | Converts a wall into a requirement. |
| That's not how AI works. | That's a reasonable expectation, and here's where the technology stops. | Corrects without embarrassing the person who asked. |
| You don't understand the model. | Let me try that explanation again from a different angle. | Puts the failure on your explanation, not their intelligence. |
| It learns and improves over time. | It's frozen until we retrain it — that's a scheduled job, not magic. | Kills the single most expensive misconception early. |
| The model thinks this student will drop out. | The model scores this student high on the same signals last year's leavers showed. | Removes intent and reasoning the model doesn't have. |
| AI can do that easily. | That's technically feasible; whether it's worth doing is a separate question. | Separates capability from value, which is where the real decision lives. |
| We need more data. | The model is limited by one field that's 40% empty — fixing that intake form is the highest-value change. | Specific and actionable instead of a standing complaint. |
| It failed. | Its inputs changed and it kept answering the old question. | Names the mechanism, so the fix is obvious. |
| The model is biased. | It performs noticeably worse for first-year international students — here's the gap. | Turns an alarming word into a measurable, fixable finding. |
| It's a black box. | I can't give you a formula, but I can show you the three factors that moved this case. | Preserves confidence while staying honest about the limit. |
| ChatGPT can't do this. | It'll draft this well; it won't reconcile the numbers, so anything that becomes a report figure comes from the database. | Concedes the real strength, then draws the line where it matters. |
| That's a stupid use case. | What problem would the AI be solving there? | Makes them find the flaw themselves, without a bruise. |
| Kindly do the needful regarding the model approval. | Could you confirm the threshold decision by Thursday so we can freeze the model? | Live corporate English with a real ask and a real date. |
| As per my earlier explanation, the model is not deterministic. | As I mentioned, it won't give word-for-word identical answers twice — that's normal for this kind of model. | Drops the letter-writing register; keeps the correction. |
| I will try my best to complete it soon. | A prototype in two weeks; something you can quote externally in two months. | Two concrete dates beat one sincere promise. |
| The result is not significant. | The difference is small enough that it could be noise — I wouldn't build a decision on it. | Statistical language translated into decision language. |
| We should not deploy this. | My professional recommendation is that we don't ship this yet, and here's the one thing that would change that. | Firm, senior, and leaves a path forward. |
| This is very complex to explain. | Here's the one-sentence version; tell me if you want the layer underneath. | Offers depth on demand instead of refusing to explain. |
| Sorry, sorry — I made a mistake in the monitoring. | I'll own that one — the monitoring was mine, and here's the fix. | One apology, then accountability. Repeated apology reads as panic. |
| The vendor's numbers are fake. | Vendor benchmarks are run on vendor data — I'd want a test on our messy cases. | Skeptical without an accusation you'd have to defend. |
| Trust me, the model is good. | We're not asking you to trust it — run it in shadow mode for a term and check it. | Replaces a request for faith with a request for evidence. |
| It depends. | It depends on one thing: whether you'd rather over-contact or under-contact. Which is it? | Turns a non-answer into a decision request. |

## 8.6 Practice Drills

**Drill 1 — The 10× rep.** Anchor phrase: **"At a high level, it does one thing: ..."** Say all ten aloud, once slowly, once at conversational speed.

1. At a high level, it does one thing: it ranks students by risk of withdrawing.
2. At a high level, it does one thing: it sorts applications into a review order.
3. At a high level, this pipeline does one thing: it gives every program a same-day headcount.
4. At a high level, the tool does one thing: it drafts the first reply so the office edits instead of writes.
5. At a high level, the model does one thing: it estimates how many seats will actually be filled.
6. At a high level, we're doing one thing here: turning twelve reports into one number people trust.
7. At a high level, it does one job: flagging records that don't reconcile, before the audit does.
8. At a high level, all it does is compare this year's cohort to the last five.
9. At a high level, it does one thing — and everything else in the deck is detail underneath that.
10. At a high level, the whole system exists to answer one question: who needs a conversation this week?

**Drill 2 — Slot swap.** Pattern: `Think of it as {familiar thing}. Where that breaks down is {limit}.` Speak all eight.

1. Think of it as autocomplete that has read most of the internet. Where that breaks down is arithmetic.
2. Think of it as a very well-read intern. Where that breaks down is that it never says "I don't know".
3. Think of it as a smoke alarm. Where that breaks down is that it can't tell you what's burning.
4. Think of it as a spam filter for at-risk cases. Where that breaks down is that a missed student matters more than a missed email.
5. Think of it as a librarian who finds the page. Where that breaks down is that it also rewrites the page.
6. Think of it as a weather forecast. Where that breaks down is that we can change the weather by acting on it.
7. Think of it as a credit score for course completion. Where that breaks down is that nobody has a right of appeal yet.
8. Think of it as a very fast intern with no memory of yesterday. Where that breaks down is anything needing context from last term.

**Drill 3 — Cold open.** One sentence each, out loud, no preamble.

1. A dean says: "So it's basically like a human brain, right?" — Correct the analogy without deflating the project.
2. A finance director says: "The vendor says 99% accurate." — Respond in one sentence that makes them ask a better question.
3. A program director says: "Just add AI to the dashboard." — Refuse the framing and get to the real problem.
4. A colleague says: "The model was wrong about that student." — Answer in one sentence that neither defends nor collapses.
5. A trustee says: "Give me the whole thing in one sentence." — Deliver the model in one repeatable line.

**Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt.** Paste this into an AI assistant and run it for fifteen minutes.

```
You are Dr. Anita Raghavan, Associate Dean for Programs at a business school.
You have a PhD in organizational behavior, thirty years of academic leadership,
and no technical background at all. You are intelligent, impatient with jargon,
and slightly suspicious that "AI" is being sold to you. You care about two things:
student outcomes and the school's reputation if something goes publicly wrong.

I am the program analytics lead. I am about to explain a student-retention model
to you and ask you to approve a shadow-mode pilot for one term.

Rules for you:
- Interrupt me the moment I use an unexplained term (AUC, feature, threshold,
  inference, model drift). Say "In English, please."
- Ask "so what would I actually do differently?" at least twice.
- At some point, claim that a colleague told you ChatGPT could do all of this
  for free, and make me answer it seriously.
- Push once for a firm launch date and resist my hedging.
- Near the end, ask: "What happens the day this model is publicly wrong about
  a student?"
- Do not accept vague reassurance. Do not be hostile. Be a smart, busy leader.

Run this as a live conversation, one turn at a time. Wait for my reply each time.

After 15 turns, stop and give me feedback in four parts:
1. Altitude — where did I explain at the wrong level, quote the exact sentence.
2. Jargon — every term I used without defining, in order.
3. Confidence — where I over-claimed, and where I undersold the work.
4. The decision — did I actually ask for a decision, and was it clear?
Then rewrite my three weakest sentences the way a confident native-level
professional would have said them.
```

## 8.7 Master these 10 first

1. **At a high level, it does one thing: ...** [#1] — Opens every explanation at decision altitude.
2. **It's a prediction, not a fact.** [#6] — Sets expectations before anyone over-trusts.
3. **Out of every 100 we flag, about 30 actually withdraw.** [#9] — Turns precision into people, instantly.
4. **There are two ways to be wrong.** [#12] — Frames the trade-off leaders must own.
5. **That's a data problem, not a model problem.** [#27] — Redirects blame toward the actual fix.
6. **Ninety-two percent sounds strong until you see the base rate.** [#94] — Kills the most common vanity metric.
7. **The model recommends; the committee decides.** [#92] — Places accountability where governance needs it.
8. **This doesn't need AI — it needs a clean report.** [#33] — Refuses unnecessary AI without sounding obstructive.
9. **Let me be straight about the limits, then about what it does well.** [#137] — Honest disclosure that still builds confidence.
10. **Let's agree now what "not working" looks like.** [#170] — Pre-commits to failure criteria before launch.


---

# Chapter 9 — Explaining Data and Analysis

> **One-line promise:** You will be able to lead with the number, describe movement precisely, hedge without sounding unsure, say any figure aloud so a US or European listener gets it first time, defend a result that contradicts the room, and retract a published number without losing the room's trust.

## 9.1 Why this chapter matters

- Most analysts explain the *journey* to the number instead of the number. You start with the source system, the join, the filter logic, and by the time you say "so it's up nine percent," the VP has already asked someone else. The single highest-return habit in this chapter is: headline first, method on request.
- Movement verbs are where non-native speakers flatten. Everything becomes "increased" and "decreased." Native corporate English has a whole gearbox — *ticked up, climbed, spiked, plateaued, held steady, dipped, slid, fell off a cliff* — and each one carries a size and a shape. Using the right verb means you've already communicated the magnitude before you say a single digit.
- Percent versus percentage points is the error that quietly destroys credibility with finance and leadership people, because they hear it and you don't. "Conversion went up three percent" and "conversion went up three points" are different claims, and saying the wrong one once makes every later number feel loose.
- Hedging is a skill, not a weakness — but stacked hedges are fatal. "Maybe approximately it could be around forty percent, I think" reads as *no confidence at all*. One hedge, chosen deliberately (*directionally, roughly, on the order of*), reads as precision about your own uncertainty, which is what senior people actually want.
- Numbers said aloud are a specific, trainable skill and Indian-English defaults collide with Western ones constantly. *Lakh* and *crore* do not exist for your US and European counterparts. "One two three four five six" is not how anyone says 123456. "Two comma four percent" is European written convention read the wrong way. Getting this wrong costs you a re-ask every time, and re-asks read as lack of command.
- What changes when you fix this: you stop being the person who *sends* numbers and become the person whose number ends the argument — including on the days the number is inconvenient, disputed, or wrong.

## 9.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)

Use these until they're automatic. These carry an ordinary stand-up, a Slack thread about a dashboard, and the first ninety seconds of any conversation that starts with someone asking "what's the number?"

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | The headline is, applications are up nine percent. | Leading with the takeaway number | Neutral |
| 2 | Short version — we're ahead of last year. | Compressing before detail | Casual |
| 3 | Let me give you the number first. | Buying yourself the method later | Neutral |
| 4 | Up about nine percent year over year. | Standard growth statement | Neutral |
| 5 | That's up from 1,240 to 1,355. | Giving both endpoints | Neutral |
| 6 | It's been climbing since March. | Sustained upward trend | Neutral |
| 7 | Flat since March. | No meaningful change | Neutral |
| 8 | It ticked up slightly last week. | Small, real increase | Neutral |
| 9 | It's holding steady. | Stable, no action needed | Neutral |
| 10 | It dipped in week six, then recovered. | Temporary drop | Neutral |
| 11 | That's a spike, not a trend. | Killing an over-read | Neutral |
| 12 | It's plateaued around two thousand. | Growth has stopped | Neutral |
| 13 | Roughly a third. | Fast approximation | Casual |
| 14 | On the order of two thousand. | Magnitude, not precision | Neutral |
| 15 | Call it fifteen hundred. | Rounding out loud | Casual |
| 16 | Give or take a hundred. | Attaching a tolerance | Casual |
| 17 | Directionally, we're fine. | Confidence without false precision | US |
| 18 | Ballpark, about twelve percent. | Rough figure on the spot | US |
| 19 | Just under five thousand. | Rounding down honestly | Neutral |
| 20 | A shade over sixty percent. | Rounding up honestly | Neutral |
| 21 | That's three percentage points, not three percent. | Correcting a unit error | Neutral |
| 22 | Conversion went from 12 to 15 — three points. | Rate change stated correctly | Neutral |
| 23 | Year over year, not month over month. | Naming the comparison basis | Neutral |
| 24 | Compared with the same week last year. | Making a like-for-like claim | Neutral |
| 25 | That's the run rate, not the actual. | Distinguishing projection from fact | Neutral |
| 26 | As of last night's refresh. | Timestamping any figure | Neutral |
| 27 | This is through the fifteenth. | Naming the cut-off date | Neutral |
| 28 | Numbers are as of Monday morning. | Pre-empting a staleness question | Neutral |
| 29 | Small caveat before you read this. | Framing a limitation early | Neutral |
| 30 | One thing to flag on this number. | Surfacing an issue yourself | Neutral |
| 31 | It's directional, but the direction is clear. | Caveating without weakening | Neutral |
| 32 | I'd treat this as indicative. | Signalling low precision, real signal | Formal |
| 33 | The number is right; the label was wrong. | Isolating what actually broke | Neutral |
| 34 | Let me check that and come back. | Buying time on a challenge | Neutral |
| 35 | Good catch — let me look. | Someone spots a real problem | Casual |
| 36 | That doesn't look right to me either. | Agreeing something is off | Neutral |
| 37 | Where are you seeing that? | Locating the disputed number | Neutral |
| 38 | Which report are you pulling from? | Starting a reconciliation | Neutral |
| 39 | Can you send me the file you're looking at? | Getting the actual artifact | Neutral |
| 40 | What date range are you on? | First reconciliation question | Neutral |
| 41 | Those are two different definitions. | Diagnosing a mismatch | Neutral |
| 42 | We're counting applications; you're counting applicants. | Naming the exact divergence | Neutral |
| 43 | The dashboard filters out test records. | Explaining a systematic gap | Neutral |
| 44 | Your export ran before the refresh. | Explaining a timing mismatch | Neutral |
| 45 | Same source, different cut. | Reassuring on data integrity | Neutral |
| 46 | Let me reconcile the two and come back to you. | Committing to close the gap | Neutral |
| 47 | The gap is about forty records, and I know where they are. | Quantifying the discrepancy | Neutral |
| 48 | It's a timing difference, not a data issue. | Downgrading a false alarm | Neutral |
| 49 | Sample is small, so I'd be careful. | Flagging weak evidence | Neutral |
| 50 | That's based on 43 responses. | Making the base explicit | Neutral |
| 51 | Too small to draw a conclusion from. | Refusing an over-read | Neutral |
| 52 | The two move together, but that's not causation. | Correlation warning, plain words | Neutral |
| 53 | Correlation, not cause. | Fast version of the same | Casual |
| 54 | There could be something else driving both. | Introducing a confounder simply | Neutral |
| 55 | Short answer, we don't know why yet. | Honest on mechanism | Neutral |
| 56 | The data doesn't tell us that. | Closing off an unsupported claim | Neutral |
| 57 | What decision is this feeding? | Opening a requirements conversation | Neutral |
| 58 | What would you do differently depending on the answer? | Testing whether the ask is real | Neutral |
| 59 | When do you need it by? | Pinning the deadline early | Neutral |
| 60 | Let me scope that before I promise a date. | Refusing to over-commit | Neutral |

### Tier 2 — Weekly (phrases 61–120)

These show up in methodology questions, data quality conversations, refresh failures, and the weekly ritual of someone asking for a number they haven't thought through.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 61 | Let me talk you through how it's calculated. | Offering method briefly | Neutral |
| 62 | In one line — it's completions over starts. | Compressing a definition | Neutral |
| 63 | The denominator is the piece people miss. | Pointing at the real issue | Neutral |
| 64 | We exclude withdrawals; that's the main choice. | Naming the key judgment call | Neutral |
| 65 | There are two ways to cut this — here's the one I'd use. | Presenting a method decision | Neutral |
| 66 | That's a definition question, not a data question. | Reframing a dispute | Neutral |
| 67 | We changed the definition in April. | Explaining a series break | Neutral |
| 68 | Before April, this included deferrals. | Giving the old rule | Neutral |
| 69 | Which is why the series looks like it jumped. | Linking definition to visual | Neutral |
| 70 | I've restated last year on the new basis. | Fixing comparability | Neutral |
| 71 | Both years are on a comparable basis now. | Confirming the fix | Formal |
| 72 | Like for like, we're up two percent. | Clean comparison | Neutral |
| 73 | Apples to apples, it's flat. | Casual version | Casual |
| 74 | Seasonally, this is normal for August. | Defusing a seasonal scare | Neutral |
| 75 | Strip out the seasonality and it's flat. | Adjusting out loud | Neutral |
| 76 | There's a base effect — last year was unusually low. | Explaining an inflated percentage | Neutral |
| 77 | It's growth off a low base. | Same, compressed | Neutral |
| 78 | The absolute number is small, so the percentage looks dramatic. | Deflating a scary percent | Neutral |
| 79 | Forty percent sounds big; it's eleven students. | Translating percent to people | Neutral |
| 80 | In absolute terms, it's forty-two. | Giving the raw count | Neutral |
| 81 | Let me convert that into something usable. | Reframing a number | Neutral |
| 82 | Per thousand applicants, that's about six. | Normalizing a rate | Neutral |
| 83 | Annualized, that's roughly 4.8 million. | Projecting to a year | Neutral |
| 84 | That's the trailing twelve months. | Naming the window | Neutral |
| 85 | Rolling four-week average, to smooth the noise. | Explaining a smoothed line | Neutral |
| 86 | I've smoothed it — the weekly series is too jumpy. | Justifying a chart choice | Neutral |
| 87 | Weekly is noisy; monthly tells the story. | Choosing a grain | Neutral |
| 88 | There's an outlier in there I'd exclude. | Flagging a distortion | Neutral |
| 89 | One record is doing a lot of work in that average. | Vivid outlier warning | Casual |
| 90 | Median, not mean — the mean is skewed. | Defending a statistic choice | Neutral |
