| 91 | Let me flag a data quality issue. | Opening a DQ conversation | Neutral |
| 92 | About four percent of rows are missing region. | Quantifying the gap | Neutral |
| 93 | We're picking up duplicates from the old form. | Naming a known defect | Neutral |
| 94 | The source system changed that field last month. | Upstream root cause | Neutral |
| 95 | Nulls are being read as zeros downstream. | Classic silent corruption | Neutral |
| 96 | It's clean enough for the decision you're making. | Right-sizing rigor | Neutral |
| 97 | It's not clean enough to publish externally. | Drawing a hard line | High-stakes |
| 98 | The pipeline failed overnight; it's rerunning now. | Refresh failure update | Neutral |
| 99 | Refresh is delayed — you'll have it by eleven. | Delay with a commitment | Neutral |
| 100 | The upstream feed came in late. | Assigning cause without blame | Neutral |
| 101 | Nothing's wrong with the numbers; the load is behind. | Calming a false alarm | Neutral |
| 102 | I'd hold off circulating that until after the refresh. | Preventing a bad send | Neutral |
| 103 | Is this a one-off, or something you'll want weekly? | Scoping cadence | Neutral |
| 104 | Who else is going to see this number? | Sizing the blast radius | Neutral |
| 105 | Let me make sure I'm solving the right problem. | Slowing down a rushed ask | Neutral |
| 106 | Tell me what "engagement" means in your head. | Unpacking a vague term | Casual |
| 107 | That request is broader than it sounds. | Early scope warning | Neutral |
| 108 | I can get you eighty percent of it by Friday. | Offering a tradeoff | Neutral |
| 109 | Which of these three do you actually need first? | Forcing prioritization | Neutral |
| 110 | Done properly it's two weeks; quick and dirty, two days. | Pricing the options | Neutral |
| 111 | Happy to do the quick version, with caveats attached. | Agreeing with conditions | Neutral |
| 112 | I'd rather give you one number you can trust. | Choosing depth over breadth | Neutral |
| 113 | That's not a number I'd stand behind yet. | Withholding a soft figure | Neutral |
| 114 | Let me sanity-check it against a second source. | Verification habit | Neutral |
| 115 | It cross-checks with finance to within one percent. | Reporting a successful check | Neutral |
| 116 | Two independent methods, same answer. | Strongest confidence claim | Neutral |
| 117 | Let me show you the query logic. | Offering transparency | Neutral |
| 118 | Everything's in the appendix if you want to audit it. | Inviting scrutiny | Written |
| 119 | I'll document the definition so we stop relitigating it. | Ending a recurring fight | Neutral |
| 120 | Let's write the definition down once and stick to it. | Proposing a standard | Neutral |

### Tier 3 — Situational (phrases 121–170)

Numbers said aloud, fiscal calendars, significance, defending a figure, and reconciling two reports that both look right.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 121 | Twelve lakh — sorry, one point two million. | Self-correcting mid-sentence | Neutral |
| 122 | A crore is ten million, so that's forty million. | Converting for a Western listener | Neutral |
| 123 | Let me say that in millions for everyone on the call. | Pre-empting the confusion | Neutral |
| 124 | I'll use dollars throughout so we're not converting live. | Setting a unit convention | US |
| 125 | Fifteen hundred — one thousand five hundred. | Disambiguating a spoken number | Neutral |
| 126 | Two point four percent. | Reading a decimal aloud | Neutral |
| 127 | Nought point eight of a percent. | Reading a sub-one decimal | UK/EU |
| 128 | Somewhere between twelve and fifteen percent. | Stating a range | Neutral |
| 129 | In the low teens. | Vague-but-useful range | Casual |
| 130 | High single digits. | Range under ten percent | Casual |
| 131 | Mid-thirties, percentage-wise. | Approximate band | Casual |
| 132 | Twenty-odd thousand. | Loose count, spoken | Casual |
| 133 | A little over half a million. | Large round number aloud | Neutral |
| 134 | Three hundred K. | Fast spoken thousands | US |
| 135 | Two and a half million euros. | Currency plus magnitude | UK/EU |
| 136 | Twenty-five K dollars, all in. | Total cost, spoken | US |
| 137 | Q3 for us is January to March. | Explaining a fiscal quarter | Neutral |
| 138 | Our fiscal year runs April to March. | Setting the calendar | Neutral |
| 139 | FY twenty-six — the year ending March twenty-six. | Removing FY ambiguity | Neutral |
| 140 | Calendar year, not fiscal. | Clarifying the basis | Neutral |
| 141 | The fifteenth of September, or September fifteenth if you prefer. | Bridging date conventions | Neutral |
| 142 | Week commencing the eighth. | Naming a week | UK/EU |
| 143 | End of Q2, so end of September. | Translating a quarter to a date | Neutral |
| 144 | First half versus second half. | H1 and H2 spoken out | Neutral |
| 145 | Statistically, that difference isn't real. | Killing a false finding | Neutral |
| 146 | That's within the margin of error. | Standard significance line | Neutral |
| 147 | The gap is two points and the error bar is four. | Making it concrete | Neutral |
| 148 | With this sample, I can't separate them. | Honest limitation | Neutral |
| 149 | Significant in the statistical sense, not the important sense. | Defusing a loaded word | Neutral |
| 150 | We'd need about four hundred responses to detect that. | Sizing a future study | Neutral |
| 151 | Plus or minus three points, at ninety-five percent. | Stating a confidence interval | Formal |
| 152 | I'm confident on the direction, less so on the size. | Splitting your confidence | Neutral |
| 153 | I stand by the number. | Flat, calm defence | High-stakes |
| 154 | I've checked it three ways; it holds. | Backing the defence with method | High-stakes |
| 155 | I understand it's not what you were expecting. | Acknowledging the discomfort | Neutral |
| 156 | I'd rather tell you now than in the board meeting. | Justifying bad news early | High-stakes |
| 157 | Let's separate whether it's right from whether we like it. | Reframing an emotional dispute | High-stakes |
| 158 | If the definition had changed, the number would move — it hasn't. | Pre-empting a deflection | Neutral |
| 159 | Send me the number you have and I'll show you where they diverge. | Inviting reconciliation | Neutral |
| 160 | Both numbers are correct; they answer different questions. | The classic reconciliation line | Neutral |
| 161 | The spreadsheet's a snapshot; the dashboard is live. | Explaining a moving target | Neutral |
| 162 | Yours is filtered to your program, mine isn't. | Naming the scope difference | Neutral |
| 163 | Your copy is from before we deduplicated. | Version-based explanation | Neutral |
| 164 | The data doesn't support that conclusion. | The core refusal | High-stakes |
| 165 | That's a reasonable hypothesis, but it's not what we're seeing. | Softened version | Neutral |
| 166 | I can't get from this data to that claim. | Naming the logical gap | Neutral |
| 167 | We can't answer that with what we collect today. | Coverage limitation | Neutral |
| 168 | To answer that properly we'd have to start capturing exit reasons. | Naming what's missing | Neutral |
| 169 | I'd be guessing, and a guess here is expensive. | Refusing to speculate | High-stakes |
| 170 | I'll give you a number, but I want the caveat travelling with it. | Conditional release | Neutral |

### Tier 4 — Rare but high-impact (phrases 171–200)

Retractions, contradicting the leadership narrative, and the sentences that decide whether people trust your numbers for the next three years.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 171 | I published a number yesterday that was wrong. | Opening a retraction | High-stakes |
| 172 | Correcting a figure I sent out on Tuesday. | Subject line and opening | Written |
| 173 | The corrected figure is 1,284, not 1,482. | Stating the fix precisely | High-stakes |
| 174 | Here's what happened, in one line. | Signalling a short explanation | Neutral |
| 175 | It doesn't change the decision, but you should have the right number. | Right-sizing the impact | High-stakes |
| 176 | It does change the recommendation, which is why I'm flagging it now. | Escalating honestly | High-stakes |
| 177 | Everyone who got the original has this correction. | Closing the loop | Written |
| 178 | I've added a check so this can't happen silently again. | Showing the fix | High-stakes |
| 179 | That one's on me. | Owning it in three words | Casual |
| 180 | I'd rather own it now than have it found later. | Explaining the speed | High-stakes |
| 181 | This result cuts against what we've been saying publicly. | Naming the collision | High-stakes |
| 182 | I've tried hard to make this say something else; it doesn't. | Showing you looked | High-stakes |
| 183 | If it's useful, I'll show you every way I tried to break it. | Offering your working | High-stakes |
| 184 | My recommendation is that we change the story, not the number. | The hard ask | High-stakes |
| 185 | I'm not comfortable putting my name on that version. | Refusing a spin | High-stakes |
| 186 | If we publish it that way, I'd want the caveat in the same sentence. | Negotiating a compromise | High-stakes |
| 187 | That chart is technically true and practically misleading. | Naming a presentation problem | High-stakes |
| 188 | I'd advise against leading with that number. | Protecting a colleague | Formal |
| 189 | I can build it, but I don't think you should use it. | Compliance with a warning | High-stakes |
| 190 | Before we go further — is this going into a public document? | Rigor triage | Neutral |
| 191 | If this goes to an accreditor, we need a different level of rigor. | Raising the bar | Formal |
| 192 | I'd want a second pair of eyes before that goes out. | Requesting review | Neutral |
| 193 | Let me put a confidence level on this — medium. | Explicit calibration | Neutral |
| 194 | What would change my mind is two more weeks of the same pattern. | Stating your falsifier | High-stakes |
| 195 | If the next quarter looks like this, I'll have been wrong. | Pre-committing to being checkable | High-stakes |
| 196 | We were both looking at real numbers; the definitions diverged. | Ending a blame spiral | High-stakes |
| 197 | I think we should retire one of these two reports. | Structural fix proposal | Formal |
| 198 | Going forward, one source of truth — and it should be this one. | Claiming the standard | High-stakes |
| 199 | I'll take the hit on this one, but I want the root cause fixed. | Trading blame for change | High-stakes |
| 200 | Here's the number, here's how sure I am, and here's what I'd do. | The complete analyst sentence | High-stakes |

## 9.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `The headline is {METRIC} {DIRECTION} {AMOUNT} {COMPARISON}.`
- The headline is applications are up nine percent year over year.
- The headline is yield is down four points on last year's cohort.
- The headline is nothing moved — we're flat on every metric that matters.
*Watch out:* Don't front-load the source ("As per the SQL Server report, the headline is..."). The number comes first; provenance comes when someone asks.

**Pattern 2:** `{METRIC} {MOVEMENT VERB} {AMOUNT} {PERIOD}.`
- Applications ticked up two percent last week — small, but it's the third week running.
- Completion rate slid from 88 to 84 over the semester.
- Enquiries spiked the day after the deadline email, then fell straight back.
*Watch out:* Match verb to magnitude. *Spiked* for a sharp, temporary jump; *ticked up* for something barely visible; *climbed* only for a sustained rise. Calling a two percent move a "spike" makes everything you say sound inflated.

**Pattern 3:** `{METRIC} went from {A} to {B} — that's {DIFFERENCE} points, or {RELATIVE} percent.`
- Conversion went from 12 to 15 — that's three points, or twenty-five percent relative.
- Attrition went from 8 to 6 — two points down, a quarter lower in relative terms.
- Yield went from 42 to 44 — two points, which is under five percent relative.
*Watch out:* A change in a *rate* is measured in percentage points. Saying "conversion is up three percent" when you mean three points understates the move by a factor of eight, and finance people will notice.

**Pattern 4:** `{HEDGE}, {NUMBER}.` where hedge is one of *directionally / roughly / on the order of / call it / ballpark*.
- Directionally, we're ahead — call it five to seven percent.
- Roughly two thousand applications, give or take a hundred.
- On the order of half a million rupees a month, if the pattern holds.
*Watch out:* One hedge per number. "Maybe approximately around roughly two thousand or so, I think" is four hedges and zero credibility. Pick the strongest one and stop.

**Pattern 5:** `{NUMBER}. One caveat — {LIMITATION}. It doesn't change {THE CONCLUSION}.`
- We're up nine percent. One caveat — the last week is partial. It doesn't change the direction.
- Forty-two percent. One caveat — that's self-reported. It doesn't change which program ranks first.
- Cost per lead is down to 940. One caveat — vendor invoices lag a month. The trend is still real.
*Watch out:* Caveat *then reassert*. Non-native speakers often end on the caveat, which leaves the listener holding doubt instead of a decision. Never let the limitation be your last sentence.

**Pattern 6:** `{A} and {B} move together, but {ALTERNATIVE EXPLANATION} could be driving both.`
- Attendance and grades move together, but motivated students probably drive both.
- Campaign spend and applications rose together, but the deadline moved in the same week.
- Portal logins track completion, but that's likely the same effort showing up twice.
*Watch out:* Don't say "correlation does not imply causation" to a non-technical stakeholder. It sounds like a lecture. Name the *specific* third thing that could explain it — that's what actually persuades.

**Pattern 7:** `That's based on {N}. The difference is {D} points and the error bar is {E}.`
- That's based on 43 responses. The difference is two points and the error bar is four.
- That's 180 students across three cohorts — enough to see a big effect, not a subtle one.
- We have 12 in that segment. I'd treat anything from it as a hypothesis, not a finding.
*Watch out:* Give the base *before* the interpretation, not after. "It's up twelve percent... based on nine people" feels like a retraction; "based on nine people, it looks up twelve percent" is honest framing from the start.

**Pattern 8:** Reading large numbers aloud — `{DIGITS}` → `{SPOKEN}`
- 1,355 → "one thousand three hundred fifty-five" (US) or "one thousand three hundred and fifty-five" (UK/EU); in fast speech, "thirteen fifty-five."
- 24,800 → "twenty-four thousand eight hundred," or casually "just under twenty-five thousand."
- 1,240,000 → "one point two four million," or "roughly one and a quarter million."
*Watch out:* Never read digit by digit ("one three five five") unless it's an ID, a phone number, or a year. And the US convention drops the "and": Americans say "two hundred fifty," not "two hundred and fifty."

**Pattern 9:** Decimals, percentages and ranges aloud — `{VALUE}` → `{SPOKEN}`
- 2.4% → "two point four percent." Never "two comma four," even with European colleagues.
- 0.8% → "zero point eight percent" (US) or "nought point eight percent" (UK); "point eight of a percent" also works.
- 12–15% → "twelve to fifteen percent," or "somewhere in the twelve to fifteen range."
*Watch out:* Written European convention uses a comma as the decimal separator (2,4%) and a dot as the thousands separator (1.240). Read it aloud in English regardless: "two point four percent," "one thousand two hundred forty." Say the number the English way even when the slide is formatted the German way.

**Pattern 10:** Currency aloud — `{SYMBOL}{AMOUNT}` → `{SPOKEN}`
- $25,000 → "twenty-five thousand dollars," or in fast US speech, "twenty-five K."
- €2,500,000 → "two and a half million euros" — currency after the number, not before.
- ₹18,00,000 → "eighteen lakh rupees" internally; externally, "about two hundred thousand dollars."
*Watch out:* Don't say "dollar twenty-five thousand" or "rupees eighteen lakh" when speaking English — that's the written symbol order leaking into speech. The unit follows the number: *twenty-five thousand dollars*.

**Pattern 11:** `{INDIAN UNIT} is {WESTERN UNIT} — I'll use {UNIT} for this conversation.`
- Twelve lakh is one point two million — I'll use millions from here.
- Four crore is forty million, or about four point eight million dollars.
- We track this in lakhs internally; I've converted everything on this slide to thousands.
*Watch out:* Lakh and crore are invisible to US and European listeners — not "less familiar," genuinely unparsed. Convert before you speak, not after they frown. And if you slip, correct in the same breath (phrase #121) rather than explaining the Indian numbering system.

**Pattern 12:** Dates and fiscal periods aloud — `{PERIOD}, which is {PLAIN DATES}.`
- Q3, which for us is January to March — our fiscal year runs April to March.
- FY26, meaning the year ending March 2026.
- End of Q2, so end of September on the calendar.
*Watch out:* "Q3" means nothing until you say whose fiscal year. And 09/08 is September eighth to an American and the ninth of August to a European — say the month by name, always.

**Pattern 13:** `Both are right — mine is {SCOPE A}, yours is {SCOPE B}.`
- Both are right — mine is all programs, yours is filtered to Executive Education.
- Both are right — mine is as of last night, your export ran Tuesday.
- Both are correct — you're counting applicants, the dashboard counts applications.
*Watch out:* Lead with "both are right." If you open with "your number is wrong," the rest of the conversation is about status, not data — and you will win the point and lose the stakeholder.

**Pattern 14:** `What decision is this feeding? If the answer were {X} rather than {Y}, what changes?`
- What decision is this feeding? If churn were 8 percent instead of 12, what would you do differently?
- Before I build it — what changes if the answer comes back flat?
- Who's the audience? That drives how much rigor this needs.
*Watch out:* Ask this as curiosity, not as a gate. "Why do you need this?" sounds like an interrogation; "what decision is this feeding?" sounds like you're trying to be useful. Same question, opposite reception.

**Pattern 15:** `I published {WRONG NUMBER} on {DAY}. The correct figure is {RIGHT NUMBER}. {CAUSE, one line}. {IMPACT}. {FIX}.`
- I published 1,482 on Tuesday. The correct figure is 1,284 — a duplicate join. It doesn't change the ranking. I've added a row-count check.
- The cost-per-lead figure in yesterday's deck was wrong: 640, not 940. Currency conversion applied twice. It strengthens the case rather than weakening it.
- Yesterday's completion rate was overstated by two points. Withdrawals weren't excluded. The decision stands; the margin is thinner than I said.
*Watch out:* Five short sentences, in that order, no throat-clearing. Every extra word of apology before the corrected number makes people trust the correction less, not more.

## 9.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 9.1 — "This number looks wrong"

**Setting:** Slack call with the Director of Admissions, who has just opened the weekly dashboard and doesn't believe the application count.

**Director:** This number looks wrong. There's no way we only had 1,240 applications last month.

**You:** Let me look. Where are you seeing that? [#37]

**Director:** Top left tile on the admissions dashboard. Last month I'd have said we were well over fourteen hundred.

**You:** Okay — so that tile is counting applications, not applicants. [#42] If someone applies to two programs, that's one applicant and two applications. Which number are you carrying in your head?

**Director:** Honestly, I think I've been quoting the applicant number from the program office file.

**You:** That would explain most of it. Send me the file you're looking at [#39] and I'll reconcile the two and come back to you. [#46] My guess is the gap is about forty records, and I know roughly where they are. [#47]

**Director:** So which one's right?

**You:** Both are correct; they answer different questions. [#160] For capacity planning you want applications. For your conversion rate you want applicants, otherwise the denominator's inflated.

**Director:** Fine. But I still feel like we're down on last year.

**You:** We are, slightly. Down two percent year over year [#4] on a like-for-like basis. [#72] It dipped in week two and recovered [#10] — it's not a collapse, it's a slow start.

**Director:** Alright, that I can work with.

**You:** I'll document the definition on the tile so we stop relitigating it. [#119]

**Why this worked:**
- No defensiveness in the first turn. "Let me look" and "where are you seeing that?" treat the challenge as information, which keeps the stakeholder on your side of the table.
- "Both are correct; they answer different questions" resolved the dispute without anyone being wrong — the single most valuable sentence in reconciliation work.
- The conversation ended with a structural fix, not just an answer. Labeling the tile means this call doesn't happen again next month.

### Scenario 9.2 — The dashboard doesn't match the spreadsheet

**Setting:** Video call with a Finance Manager who maintains her own workbook and has found a 3 percent variance.

**Finance Manager:** Your dashboard says 4.8 million in program revenue. My workbook says 4.65. That's a material difference.

**You:** Agreed, that's worth chasing. What date range are you on? [#40]

**Finance Manager:** April through March, full fiscal year.

**You:** Same here. Which report are you pulling from? [#38]

**Finance Manager:** The export you sent me on the twelfth.

**You:** That's part of it — your export ran before the refresh [#44] that picked up March's late invoices. But that's maybe half a percent, not three. Let me flag something else. [#91] Are you including the Executive Education short courses?

**Finance Manager:** No, those sit in a different cost center for us.

**You:** Then that's the rest of it. Both are right — mine is all programs, yours excludes ExecEd. [#160] Yours is filtered, mine isn't. [#162]

**Finance Manager:** So the dashboard is overstating from my point of view.

**You:** From your point of view, yes — it's answering a different question. I'll add a cost-center filter so you can toggle to your view directly. And I'll say it in millions on the tile so we're not converting live. [#123]

**Finance Manager:** Can you confirm the split in writing?

**You:** I'll send it today. Executive Education is on the order of 150,000 [#14] — I'll give you the exact figure rather than the round one. And it cross-checks with your ledger to within one percent [#115] once ExecEd is stripped out.

**Why this worked:**
- The variance was decomposed into two separate causes — a timing difference and a scope difference — instead of being explained away by one.
- Nothing was framed as her error. "Both are right" plus a product change (the filter) converted a complaint into a feature request.
- The commitment was specific: today, in writing, with the exact figure. Reconciliation promises without a date are how variances become quarterly arguments.

### Scenario 9.3 — The result contradicts the leadership narrative

**Setting:** Pre-read session with the Associate Dean, two days before a board update. Your analysis shows the flagship scholarship program has no measurable effect on yield.

**You:** Before Thursday, I want to walk you through something, because this result cuts against what we've been saying publicly. [#181]

**Associate Dean:** Go on.

**You:** The headline is, scholarship recipients convert at 61 percent and comparable non-recipients convert at 59. [#1] That's two points, and the error bar is four. [#147] Statistically, that difference isn't real. [#145]

**Associate Dean:** We've told the board that program is the reason yield held up.

**You:** I know. And I've tried hard to make this say something else; it doesn't. [#182] I ran it three ways — matched on GMAT band, on region, and on program. Two independent methods, same answer. [#116]

**Associate Dean:** Could it be the sample?

**You:** Fair question. We're at 340 recipients over three years. With this sample, I can't separate a two-point effect from zero. [#148] What I can say is there's no large effect. If it were ten points, I'd have found it.

**Associate Dean:** So the scholarship does nothing?

**You:** The data doesn't support that either. [#164] It says we can't detect a yield effect. There may be effects we don't measure — diversity of the cohort, alumni giving later. I'd be guessing on those, and a guess here is expensive. [#169]

**Associate Dean:** This is awkward two days out.

**You:** I'd rather tell you now than in the board meeting. [#156] My recommendation is that we change the story, not the number [#184] — say the scholarship is an access commitment rather than a yield lever. That's defensible and it's true.

**Associate Dean:** And if I want to keep the yield line in?

**You:** Then I'd want the caveat in the same sentence. [#186] Something like "yield held up, and scholarships are part of a broader access strategy." I'm not comfortable with a slide that claims the causal link. [#185]

**Associate Dean:** Understood. Draft me the wording.

**Why this worked:**
- The collision was named in the first sentence. Burying a narrative-breaking result until turn eight makes it look like you were hiding it.
- "I've tried hard to make this say something else" signals loyalty to the institution *and* to the number simultaneously — it pre-empts the suspicion that you enjoy being contrarian.
- The refusal was scoped precisely: not "don't say that," but "don't claim the causal link, and here's alternative wording." Offering the replacement sentence is what turns a blocker into an advisor.

### Scenario 9.4 — Retracting a number you published yesterday

**Setting:** You sent a cost-per-enrolled-student figure to a leadership distribution list yesterday. This morning you found a double-counted vendor invoice. You're calling the Chief of Staff before sending the correction.

**You:** I published a number yesterday that was wrong [#171] and I want you to hear it from me before the email lands.

**Chief of Staff:** Which one?

**You:** Cost per enrolled student. I said 94,000 rupees. The correct figure is 78,000. [#173] Here's what happened, in one line: [#174] one vendor's invoices were loaded twice in February.

**Chief of Staff:** How bad is this?

**You:** It moves in our favor, which is a small mercy. It doesn't change the decision [#175] on the channel mix — the ranking of channels is identical. It does change the headline you were planning to use in the town hall.

**Chief of Staff:** Who has the wrong number?

**You:** Eleven people on the distribution list. Everyone who got the original will get the correction [#177] in the next hour, same thread, subject line starting "Correcting a figure I sent Tuesday." [#172]

**Chief of Staff:** How did it get through?

**You:** That one's on me. [#179] I didn't have a duplicate check on the vendor load. I've added a row-count reconciliation so this can't happen silently again. [#178] It'll fail the pipeline rather than publish.

**Chief of Staff:** You could have quietly fixed it in the dashboard.

**You:** I'd rather own it now than have it found later. [#180] If someone quotes 94 in a meeting next month and finance says 78, we lose more than we lose today.

**Chief of Staff:** Fair. Send it.

**Why this worked:**
- The corrected number appeared in the third sentence. No preamble, no apology stack — the fastest possible route from "something's wrong" to "here is the right figure."
- Impact was stated in decision terms, not emotional ones: what changes, what doesn't, who's affected, how many people.
- Owning it came bundled with a permanent control. "That's on me" alone is confession; "that's on me and here's the check I added" is engineering.

### Scenario 9.5 — Pushing back on a badly-scoped request, on a US call

**Setting:** A US-based partnerships director pings you on a Thursday call asking for "all the engagement data" for a partner review on Monday.

**Partnerships Director:** Can you pull me all the engagement data for the corporate partners? I need it for Monday.

**You:** Sure — that request is broader than it sounds, though. [#107] Let me make sure I'm solving the right problem. [#105] What decision is this feeding? [#57]

**Partnerships Director:** We're deciding which three partners to renew.

**You:** That helps a lot. Tell me what "engagement" means in your head [#106] — is that headcount enrolled, sessions attended, or spend?

**Partnerships Director:** Honestly, probably spend and headcount. Attendance would be nice.

**You:** Attendance is the expensive one — it's in a different system with no clean partner key. Done properly it's two weeks; quick and dirty, two days. [#110] Spend and headcount I can have you by Friday afternoon.

**Partnerships Director:** Let's do spend and headcount. Ballpark, how much are we talking per partner?

**You:** For the top three, on the order of two hundred thousand dollars a year [#14] each, and headcount in the low forties. [#129] I'll confirm exact figures Friday.

**Partnerships Director:** And year over year?

**You:** Year over year, not month over month [#23] — yes, I'll show both years on a comparable basis. [#71] One flag: we changed how we count "enrolled" in April. [#67] Before April it included deferrals, [#68] so the older numbers run a little high. I'll restate last year on the new basis [#70] so it's like for like. [#72]

**Partnerships Director:** Perfect. And can you use dollars? Half my slide is in rupees and it's confusing people.

**You:** I'll use dollars throughout so we're not converting live. [#124] Anything in lakhs I'll convert before it reaches you.

**Why this worked:**
- The scope pushback arrived as a question about the decision, not a complaint about workload — so it read as service, not resistance.
- The two-week versus two-day tradeoff let the stakeholder choose the cut themselves, which means the descope was theirs, not yours.
- The definition change was volunteered before delivery. Surfacing a series break *after* someone builds a slide with it is how analysts lose stakeholders permanently.

## 9.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| The number is increased by nine percent. | Applications are up nine percent year over year. | Natural verb, plus the comparison basis nobody can guess. |
| Conversion increased by three percent, from 12 to 15. | Conversion went from 12 to 15 — three points. | Points versus percent. The wrong unit undermines every later number. |
| The value is fluctuating up and down. | It's noisy week to week, but flat over the quarter. | Names the noise and the underlying signal separately. |
| There is a huge increase in the month of April. | April spiked, then fell straight back — that's the deadline effect. | Right verb for a temporary jump, plus the cause in the same breath. |
| Maybe approximately it could be around forty percent, I think. | Roughly forty percent. | One hedge, not four. Stacked hedges read as no confidence at all. |
| I am not fully sure about this number. | It's directional, but the direction is clear. | Separates what you're unsure about from what you're sure about. |
| This data is having some quality issues. | About four percent of rows are missing region — I'd still use it for this. | Quantifies the defect and tells them what to do about it. |
| The report is not matching, I will check. | Both are right — mine is all programs, yours is filtered to ExecEd. | Diagnoses instead of deferring, and nobody is wrong. |
| Your number is wrong. | Send me the number you have and I'll show you where they diverge. | Wins the reconciliation without spending stakeholder goodwill. |
| Since attendance increased, grades also increased. | Attendance and grades move together, but motivated students likely drive both. | Names the specific confounder rather than lecturing about causation. |
| Correlation does not imply causation. | That's a reasonable hypothesis, but it's not what we're seeing. | Same point without the classroom tone that makes people defensive. |
| The sample is very less. | That's based on 43 responses — too small to conclude from. | "Very less" is a giveaway; the count does the persuading anyway. |
| This result is significant. | The difference is two points and the error bar is four — so it's not real. | Avoids the word people misread as "important." |
| As per the requirement given by you, I have prepared this. | Here's the cut you asked for — spend and headcount, both years. | Peer register. "As per" and "kindly" date you instantly. |
| Why do you need this data? | What decision is this feeding? | Curiosity instead of interrogation. Same question, opposite reception. |
| It is not possible to do this by Monday. | Done properly it's two weeks; quick and dirty, two days. Your call. | Gives them the tradeoff instead of a wall, and they own the descope. |
| The data is not refreshed due to some technical issue. | The upstream feed came in late — you'll have it by eleven. | Cause plus a commitment. "Some technical issue" sounds like you don't know. |
| Twelve lakh applications-wise we are seeing. | One point two million applications. | Converted before they have to ask, and the word order is English. |
| Rupees eighteen lakh is the cost. | About two hundred thousand dollars, all in. | Unit follows the number, and it's in the listener's currency. |
| In Q3 we are expecting the improvement. | In Q3 — January to March for us — I'd expect it to recover. | "Q3" is meaningless until you say whose fiscal year. |
| Two comma four percent of the cohort. | Two point four percent of the cohort. | European written convention read aloud in English is always "point." |
| I think so the number is correct. | I've checked it three ways; it holds. | Removes the "I think so" tic and replaces it with method. |
| Sorry sorry, actually I gave the wrong figure yesterday. | Correcting a figure I sent Tuesday: it's 1,284, not 1,482. | Correction in the first sentence. Apology stacking dilutes the fix. |
| This conclusion cannot be drawn from the data, sorry. | I can't get from this data to that claim — here's what we'd need. | Blocks the claim and opens a path, so you're not just the obstacle. |
| Leadership will not like this result. | This cuts against what we've been saying, and I want to be straight about it. | Names the collision as a professional issue rather than a political one. |

## 9.6 Practice Drills

**Drill 1 — The 10× rep.** Anchor phrase: *"The headline is..."* Say each aloud, standing, at conversational pace. The target is a falling tone at the end of each — a rising tone turns a statement into a request for permission.

1. The headline is, applications are up nine percent year over year.
2. The headline is, we're flat — and flat is worse than it sounds this late in the cycle.
3. The headline is, yield is down four points on last year's cohort.
4. The headline is, the drop is entirely one program.
5. Short version — we're ahead of last year, and it's driven by the early deadline.
6. Let me give you the number first: cost per enrolled student is 78,000.
7. The headline is, nothing in this dataset supports the scholarship claim.
8. The one number that matters here is 61 percent, and it hasn't moved since March.
9. The headline is, two of the three channels are working; the third isn't.
10. Before the detail — the recommendation is that we hold the current mix.

**Drill 2 — Slot swap.** Pattern: `{METRIC} {MOVEMENT VERB} {AMOUNT} {PERIOD}, and the reason is {CAUSE}.` Speak all eight. Force yourself to use a different movement verb each time.

1. applications / ticked up / two percent / last week / the reminder email
2. completion rate / slid / from 88 to 84 / over the semester / the new capstone requirement
3. enquiries / spiked / by a factor of three / the day after the deadline / an alumni newsletter
4. yield / plateaued / around 61 percent / since March / capacity, not demand
5. cost per lead / dipped / to 940 / in Q2 / a cheaper channel mix
6. attrition / held steady / at six percent / all year / nothing changed upstream
7. portal logins / climbed / roughly forty percent / since the redesign / mobile traffic
8. international applications / fell off / by a fifth / this cycle / visa processing times

**Drill 3 — Cold open.** One spoken sentence each. No "that's a good question," no preamble.

1. "This number looks wrong — we definitely had more than 1,240 applications."
2. "So the scholarship program is driving the yield increase, right?"
3. "Can you pull me all the engagement data by Monday?"
4. "Finance has 4.65 million. You have 4.8. Which one do I put in the deck?"
5. "You sent me 94,000 yesterday and now you're saying 78,000. What happened?"

**Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt.** Paste this into an AI chat. Speak your answers aloud — including every number — rather than typing them. The numbers-aloud feedback is the point of this drill.

```
You are Marcus Bell, a US-based Director of Finance at a global education
group, dialing in from Chicago. You are numerate, fast, slightly impatient,
and you have never heard the words "lakh" or "crore" in your life. You do not
know anything about Indian fiscal years. You interrupt when a number is
unclear.

I am the senior program analytics lead, based in Hyderabad. I am about to
walk you through cost-per-enrolled-student figures for the last fiscal year
and explain a variance between my dashboard and your ledger.

Run this as a live call, one turn at a time. Do this:
1. Open by telling me my dashboard number doesn't match your ledger, and
   give me your figure.
2. Every time I say a number in a way that is ambiguous or non-standard for
   a US listener - lakhs, crores, "one two four zero", a bare "Q3" with no
   fiscal year, a date like 09/08, "two comma four percent" - STOP me and
   say "Sorry, say that again?" Do not explain why. Make me self-correct.
3. At some point, confuse percent with percentage points on purpose and see
   whether I correct you.
4. Ask me whether the difference between two segments is "significant" and
   push me on what that word means.
5. Near the end, tell me you're putting my number in a board pack and ask
   me to confirm it, with no caveats attached.

Stay in character. Two or three sentences per turn. Do not coach me during
the call.

When I write "END SCENE", stop and give me feedback in this format:
- Every number I said aloud, written as you heard it, with a note on whether
  a US listener would have parsed it first time.
- Every time I used an Indian numbering unit without converting. Quote me.
- Did I lead with the headline number or with the method? Quote my opening.
- Count my hedges. Flag any sentence with more than one.
- Did I correct your percent/percentage-point error? If not, what should I
  have said?
- How did I handle "confirm it with no caveats"? Rate 1-5 and quote me.
- Rewrite my three worst spoken numbers the way a US finance director would
  have wanted to hear them.
```

## 9.7 Master these 10 first

1. **The headline is, applications are up nine percent.** [#1] — Number first, method on request.
2. **That's three percentage points, not three percent.** [#21] — The unit error senior people notice.
3. **Directionally, we're fine.** [#17] — Confidence without inventing false precision.
4. **Both numbers are correct; they answer different questions.** [#160] — Ends reconciliation fights without blame.
5. **The two move together, but that's not causation.** [#52] — Blocks the most common analytical error.
6. **That's based on 43 responses.** [#50] — Base rate before interpretation, always.
7. **What decision is this feeding?** [#57] — Scopes the request without sounding obstructive.
8. **Twelve lakh is one point two million.** [#122] — Convert before they have to ask.
9. **The data doesn't support that conclusion.** [#164] — Your single most important refusal.
10. **I published a number yesterday that was wrong.** [#171] — Owning it fast protects everything else.


---

## Part V — The Influence Layer


---

# Chapter 10 — Disagreement

> **One-line promise:** You'll be able to disagree with anyone — a peer, your manager, a Dean, a vendor — at exactly the strength the moment needs, without damaging the relationship or losing the argument by accident.

## 10.1 Why this chapter matters

- Disagreement is the single place where non-native speakers get misread most. The literal translation of a polite Hindi or Marathi hedge often lands in English as either flat contradiction ("No, this is wrong") or as such a soft murmur that nobody registers you disagreed at all. Both cost you: the first makes you "difficult," the second makes you "quiet."
- English disagreement is **graded**. There are at least five distinct strength levels, and native-level professionals switch between them constantly and deliberately. "I'd push back on that slightly" and "I can't sign off on this" are not the same sentence with different words — they are different acts with different consequences. Using level 4 language for a level 2 problem is how careers stall.
- The most valuable move in this chapter isn't a phrase, it's a redirection: **disagree with the assumption, the method, or the data — never with the person**. "Your analysis is wrong" starts a fight. "What's the baseline on that?" wins the same argument and leaves the other person intact.
- Written disagreement is a separate skill. Slack and email strip out your tone, your face, and your pauses, so a message you wrote as "mild curiosity" arrives as "public attack." Knowing when to stop typing and pick up the phone is a senior behaviour, and it's learnable in one sentence.
- The endgame matters as much as the opening. Most people can start a disagreement; far fewer can concede cleanly, hold a position under pressure without becoming rigid, or execute "disagree and commit" so convincingly that nobody can tell you lost. That's the part that gets you into the room where decisions are made.
- In an analytics role you are structurally the person who has to say the uncomfortable thing about the numbers. If you can't do that fluently, you become a reporting service instead of an advisor.

## 10.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)

Use these until they're automatic. Nearly all of these are level 1–2 on the strength scale in 10.3 — soft, everyday, low-cost.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | I see it a bit differently. | Opening any disagreement safely | Neutral |
| 2 | Hmm, I'm not sure about that one. | First, mildest signal of doubt | Casual |
| 3 | Can I push back on that slightly? | Asking permission to disagree | Neutral |
| 4 | I'd challenge that a little. | Light, confident objection | Neutral |
| 5 | Yes and no. | Partial agreement, in two words | Casual |
| 6 | I agree with the first half, less with the second. | Splitting a claim you half-buy | Neutral |
| 7 | That's fair, but — | Conceding one inch, taking a mile | Neutral |
| 8 | I hear you, but I'm not fully there yet. | Acknowledging without agreeing | Neutral |
| 9 | Let me offer a different read on that. | Introducing your interpretation | Neutral |
| 10 | Actually, I'd argue the opposite. | Direct but still collegial | Neutral |
| 11 | I'm not convinced yet. | Honest, low-drama holdout | Neutral |
| 12 | Can I ask what that's based on? | Questioning evidence, not judgment | Neutral |
| 13 | Where's that number coming from? | A figure appears from nowhere | Neutral |
| 14 | What's the assumption behind that? | Attacking the premise politely | Neutral |
| 15 | Help me understand the logic there. | Sounds curious, works as disagreement | Neutral |
| 16 | I might be missing something, but — | Classic softener before an objection | Neutral |
| 17 | Sorry — just so I follow, why that approach? | Questioning method without insult | Neutral |
| 18 | Is that definitely the case? | Testing a confident claim | Neutral |
| 19 | Do we know that for certain? | Separating fact from assumption | Neutral |
| 20 | That doesn't quite match what I'm seeing. | Your data says otherwise | Neutral |
| 21 | My numbers say something different. | Direct factual conflict | Neutral |
| 22 | The data's pointing somewhere else, I think. | Softened version of the same | Neutral |
| 23 | I'd want to check that before we commit. | Slowing a fast decision | Neutral |
| 24 | Can we test that assumption? | Turning belief into an experiment | Neutral |
| 25 | I take a slightly different view. | Formal-ish, safe in any room | Formal |
| 26 | I'm with you on the goal, not on the method. | Separating what from how | Neutral |
| 27 | Same objective, different route. | Compact version of the above | Casual |
| 28 | Would it be worth considering another option? | Opening the door to alternatives | Neutral |
| 29 | What if we did it the other way round? | Proposing an inversion | Casual |
| 30 | Have we thought about — ? | Raising an overlooked path | Neutral |

| 32 | There's another way to look at this. | Reframing a shared fact | Neutral |

| 34 | My only hesitation is ... | Narrowing your disagreement | Neutral |
| 35 | The bit I'm stuck on is ... | Naming the exact sticking point | Neutral |
| 36 | That's where I'd disagree. | Pinpointing, not blanket-rejecting | Neutral |
| 37 | I don't think that follows. | The logic jumps a step | Neutral |
| 38 | That's true, but it doesn't change the outcome much. | Conceding a fact, refusing the conclusion | Neutral |
| 39 | I'd be careful with that one. | Warning without forbidding | Neutral |
| 40 | I wouldn't go that far. | Someone overstated the finding | Neutral |
| 41 | It's a bit stronger than I'd put it. | Toning down a claim | Neutral |
| 42 | I'd soften that slightly. | Editing someone's wording | Neutral |
| 43 | Not sure I'd read it that way. | Alternative interpretation | Casual |
| 44 | Can I offer a counterpoint? | Formal opening in a group | Neutral |
| 45 | Playing devil's advocate for a second — | Disagreeing without owning it | Casual |
| 46 | Just to test that a bit — | Prefacing a probing question | Neutral |
| 47 | Let me stress-test that. | Pressure-checking a proposal | Neutral |
| 48 | What breaks if we're wrong? | Forcing a downside conversation | Neutral |
| 49 | What would have to be true for that to work? | Elegant, non-confrontational challenge | Neutral |
| 50 | I'd want to see the numbers first. | Refusing to agree on vibes | Neutral |
| 51 | That feels early to me. | Decision is premature | Casual |
| 52 | I think we're jumping ahead. | Skipping a step in the logic | Neutral |
| 53 | Can we slow down on that one? | Buying analysis time | Neutral |
| 54 | I'd like to understand it better before I agree. | Honest deferral | Neutral |
| 55 | I'm not saying no — I'm saying not yet. | Distinguishing refusal from timing | Neutral |
| 56 | Fair enough, but here's my worry. | Concede-then-object structure | Neutral |
| 57 | I'll take your word on that part, but — | Accepting one claim, not the next | Neutral |
| 58 | Let's park the conclusion and check the input. | Redirecting to the data | Neutral |
| 59 | Good point — my concern is what happens next quarter. | Agreeing short-term, objecting long-term | Neutral |

### Tier 2 — Weekly (phrases 61–120)

Structured disagreement: challenging a method, proposing an alternative, holding your line, conceding well, and handling it in writing.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |

### Tier 4 — Rare but high-impact (phrases 171–200)

Blocking language. Every one of these is a level 4 or 5 move. Use them once a year at most — and mean it when you do.

| 189 | This crosses a line for me. | Ethical, not technical, objection | High-stakes |
