| 191 | I can't defend that publicly if I'm asked. | Warning of exposure | High-stakes |
| 192 | I won't present a number I don't believe. | Refusing to be the messenger | High-stakes |
| 193 | If we publish this, we'll have to retract it. | Predicting the consequence | High-stakes |
| 194 | I need to withdraw my earlier agreement — new information. | Reversing your own sign-off | High-stakes |
| 195 | I was wrong, and the recommendation has to change. | Public self-correction, high cost | High-stakes |
| 196 | I've reconsidered, and I can no longer support this. | Changing sides mid-project | High-stakes |
| 197 | I'd like a formal review before we proceed. | Invoking process to stop momentum | Formal |
| 198 | Let's pause the release until this is resolved. | Blocking a launch | High-stakes |
| 199 | I'm asking for this decision to be revisited. | Reopening a closed decision | Formal |
| 200 | If we're proceeding anyway, I want the risk owner named. | Accepting defeat, assigning accountability | High-stakes |

## 10.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

### The strength scale — know what level you're firing at

Before the patterns, calibrate. Every disagreement in English sits on a five-point scale. The commonest career mistake is firing level 4 language at a level 2 problem — or level 1 language when the situation genuinely needs a block.

| Level | What it does | Signature phrases | Typical phrases from the ladder | Cost if you're wrong |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 — Mild signal | Registers doubt, changes nothing | "Hmm, I'm not sure about that one." / "That feels early to me." | 1, 2, 5, 11, 43, 51 | None. Free to use. |
| 2 — Probe | Disagrees by questioning | "What's the assumption behind that?" / "Where's that number coming from?" | 12, 13, 14, 17, 48, 49, 86 | Very low. Reads as rigour. |
| 3 — Stated position | Puts an alternative on the table | "I'd rather we ran it the other way, and here's why." / "I think we're solving the wrong problem." | 61, 62, 63, 79, 85, 89 | Low. You now own a position. |
| 4 — Formal concern | Goes on the record; slows the decision | "I'd like to register a concern for the record." / "I want to be on record as having raised this." | 128, 129, 145, 171, 184, 185 | Real. Costs political capital. |
| 5 — Blocking | Withholds approval; stops the thing | "I can't sign off on this." / "Let's pause the release until this is resolved." | 173, 174, 192, 198, 181 | Very high. Once or twice a career. |

Rule of thumb: **start one level below where you think you need to be.** If level 2 doesn't move the room, escalate to 3. Almost nothing that starts at level 5 ends well, and if you use level 5 twice in a year, people stop hearing it.

---

**Pattern 1:** `I see it a bit differently — [YOUR READ].`
- I see it a bit differently — I think the drop is a reporting artefact, not a real fall in applications.
- I see it a bit differently — the model's fine, it's the training window I'd change.
- I see it a bit differently — that cohort was already declining before the intervention.
*Watch out:* Don't stack softeners. "I may be wrong but I see it a bit differently, just my small opinion only" undoes all the authority the phrase gives you. One softener, then your point.

**Pattern 2:** `I agree with [PART A], but [PART B] is where I'd push back.`
- I agree with the diagnosis, but the timeline is where I'd push back.
- I agree that retention is the right metric, but the definition is where I'd push back.
- I agree we need a dashboard, but building it in Power BI is where I'd push back.
*Watch out:* The part you agree with must be real and specific. A generic "I agree with everything you said, but..." reads as a fake courtesy and native speakers hear it as sarcasm.

**Pattern 3:** `What's the assumption behind [CLAIM]?`
- What's the assumption behind the 15% uplift?
- What's the assumption behind treating those two programs as comparable?
- What's the assumption behind the forecast holding through admissions season?
*Watch out:* Ask it flat and curious, not slow and pointed. Drawn out with heavy stress on "assumption," this becomes an accusation, and you'll get a defensive answer instead of a real one.

**Pattern 4:** `Help me understand [THE STEP I DON'T BUY].`
- Help me understand how we get from the survey scores to the retention forecast.
- Help me understand why we dropped the 2023 cohort.
- Help me understand what changed between last month's number and this one.
*Watch out:* In US English this is standard and warm. Said too flatly it can sound like a manager auditing a junior, so keep your intonation rising and friendly if you're speaking to a peer or upward.

**Pattern 5:** `I'm with you on [GOAL], not on [METHOD].`
- I'm with you on getting this to the Dean by Friday, not on skipping the reconciliation.
- I'm with you on simplifying the report, not on dropping the segment breakdown.
- I'm with you on the ambition, not on doing it in one sprint.
*Watch out:* Keep the goal half genuinely warm. If it's clipped, the sentence becomes "you're wrong" with extra words.

**Pattern 6:** `The [CONCLUSION] may well be right — my issue is [THE METHOD].`
- The conclusion may well be right — my issue is that we're inferring it from 40 responses.
- The recommendation may well be right — my issue is the control group.
- The direction may well be right — my issue is that the baseline moved halfway through.
*Watch out:* This is the safest way to criticize someone's work. Never invert it into "your method is wrong so your conclusion is worthless" — that leaves the author nowhere to stand.

**Pattern 7:** `What would have to be true for [PROPOSAL] to work?`
- What would have to be true for the pipeline to hold at three times the volume?
- What would have to be true for that vendor timeline to be realistic?
- What would have to be true for us to hit 90% completion by March?
*Watch out:* Present tense in the "to be true" clause, always. "What would have to be true" — not "what would have had to be true," which shifts it to a post-mortem about the past.

**Pattern 8:** `I'd rather [ALTERNATIVE], because [REASON].`
- I'd rather run it as a two-week pilot, because we can't undo a full rollout.
- I'd rather fix the source table, because we'll be patching this every month otherwise.
- I'd rather present ranges than a point estimate, because the confidence interval is wide.
*Watch out:* "I'd rather" needs a bare infinitive: "I'd rather run," not "I'd rather to run." This is the single most common slip in this pattern for Indian-English speakers.

**Pattern 9:** `Can I have five minutes before [PUBLIC MOMENT]?`
- Can I have five minutes before the steering group tomorrow?
- Can I have five minutes before you send that to the Dean?
- Can I have five minutes before we lock the vendor decision?
*Watch out:* Say what it's about in the same breath ("— it's about the enrolment number"). A bare "can we talk" makes your manager assume you're resigning.

**Pattern 10:** `I want to flag [RISK], and then I'll back whatever you decide.`
- I want to flag the data-quality risk, and then I'll back whatever you decide.
- I want to flag that the timeline assumes no leave in December, and then I'll back whatever you decide.
- I want to flag one dependency on the IT team, and then I'll back whatever you decide.
*Watch out:* Only use this if it's true. If you say it and then keep re-arguing the point in the next three meetings, you'll be labelled as someone whose word doesn't hold.

**Pattern 11:** `Disagree and commit: [YOUR POSITION], [YOUR COMMITMENT].`
- I'd have gone the other way, but the decision's made and I'll deliver it properly.
- My view hasn't changed, but I'm not going to undercut it — let's make it work.
- I lost that one. I'm building it the way we agreed.
*Watch out:* Never deliver this with an audible sigh or a "well, if that's what we're doing." Half-hearted commitment reads worse than open dissent.

**Pattern 12:** `To be clear, I'm disagreeing with [THE THING], not with [THE PERSON].`
- To be clear, I'm disagreeing with the methodology, not with the team that built it.
- To be clear, I'm questioning the number, not anyone's effort here.
- To be clear, my problem is the process, not your call.
*Watch out:* This is a written-channel workhorse. In speech it can sound over-explained — face and tone do the job for you, so use it sparingly out loud.

**Pattern 13:** `This is easier on a call — [OFFER A TIME].`
- This is easier on a call — free at 3?
- This is easier on a call — I'll ping you after standup.
- This is easier on a call — got ten minutes now?
*Watch out:* Send it before the thread turns cold, not after. Once someone has typed a long defensive paragraph, moving to a call feels like you're pulling them out of the room to be told off.

**Pattern 14:** `That's a fair challenge — [YOUR EVIDENCE].`
- That's a fair challenge — the filter excludes withdrawals, and here's the row count either way.
- That's a fair challenge — I ran it both ways and the gap is under a point.
- That's a fair challenge — let me show you the query rather than argue about it.
*Watch out:* Say "fair challenge" or "fair question," not "good question." "Good question" is the phrase people use when they're buying time, and senior audiences hear it that way.

**Pattern 15:** `I can't [APPROVE/SIGN/PRESENT] [THING] — [ONE-LINE REASON].`
- I can't sign off on this — the reconciliation hasn't been done.
- I can't put my name to that number — I don't believe the denominator.
- I can't present that chart — it implies causation we haven't shown.
*Watch out:* Exactly one reason, and stop talking. Piling on three justifications makes a firm refusal sound negotiable, and someone will start negotiating.

## 10.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 10.1 — Your manager proposes a method you know is wrong

**Setting:** Your Associate Director wants to measure program impact by comparing this year's placement rate to last year's — with no adjustment for a cohort that grew 40%.

**You:** Before we lock this, can I push back on that slightly? [#3] I'm with you on the goal, not on the method. [#26]

**Manager:** We've always done the year-on-year comparison. It's what the Dean expects.

**You:** Understood. My issue isn't the conclusion, it's how we got there. [#65] If we change the baseline, the story changes — [#71] the cohort's 40% bigger, so the same absolute number of placements shows up as a decline.

**Manager:** So you're saying the number is wrong?

**You:** I'm saying the comparison isn't like-for-like. What's the assumption behind treating the two years as comparable? [#14] If it's that intake is stable, that's the bit I'm stuck on. [#35]

**Manager:** Hmm. What would you do instead?

**You:** One alternative would be to report rate per 100 eligible students and show both years' denominators on the slide. [#31] I'd rather present it that way, because the Dean will ask about intake anyway and I'd like us to have the answer first.

**Manager:** That's more work two days before the review.

**You:** It's about three hours — I've already got the query half-written. Let me walk you through why I'd do it differently, [#79] and if you still want the straight comparison, I'll make it work. [#127]

**Manager:** Fine, show me at four.

**You:** Perfect. And either way I'll flag the intake change in the footnote so nobody's surprised.

**Why this worked:**
- The disagreement stayed at level 2 and 3 throughout — probing and proposing, never blocking. No formal language was needed because the technical argument did the work.
- Separating goal from method (#26, #65) let the manager change position without conceding anything personal.
- Ending with "if you still want it, I'll make it work" (#127) removed the threat entirely and made saying yes cheap.

### Scenario 10.2 — A peer publicly challenges your analysis

**Setting:** Monthly program review, twelve people including two Deans. A peer from Admissions says your retention figure "doesn't look right" and that his team has a different number.

**Peer:** I have to say, that 87% doesn't match anything we've got. Ours is 91%. I'd be careful using that in front of leadership.

**You:** That's a fair challenge — here's what's behind it. [#152] Both numbers can be right. I think we're both right, different definitions. [#157]

**Peer:** They're both retention. How different can it be?

**You:** Mine excludes students who deferred; if I read your dashboard correctly, yours counts a deferral as retained. That's roughly four points on this cohort. Let's find where our two numbers diverge [#156] — do you want to reconcile them live, or shall I send you the row-level comparison this afternoon?

**Peer:** Send it. But I still think 87 understates it.

**You:** Possibly. I'd rather you challenge it now than after it ships. [#154] What I'd suggest is we agree the definition before the board pack, not after.

**Dean:** Which one goes in the pack?

**You:** I'd like to answer that properly rather than quickly. [#150] Give me until Thursday and you'll get one number with the definition written on the slide.

**Dean:** Good. Do that.

**Peer:** Works for me.

**You:** And Rahul — nobody's questioning your work here. [#161] Two teams, two definitions. That's a governance fix, not a data fix.

**Why this worked:**
- Reframing "you're wrong" into "different definitions" (#157) converted a public credibility attack into a shared technical problem in one sentence.
- Refusing to produce an answer on the spot (#150) protected the number without looking evasive, because a date was attached.
- The closing reassurance (#161) mattered more than the analysis — it ensured the peer had no reason to relitigate it in the corridor afterwards.

### Scenario 10.3 — Overruled, and now you have to support the decision

**Setting:** Leadership has chosen an external dashboarding vendor over your internal build. You argued against it and lost. Now your team is asking you what you think.

**Director:** So the vendor decision is final. I know it wasn't your recommendation.

**You:** It wasn't. I've made my case; I'm not going to relitigate it. [#135] The decision's made, and I'm not going to undercut it. [#147]

**Director:** I appreciate that. Anything you want on the record?

**You:** One thing. I'd like the assumption we're betting on written down [#131] — that the vendor can hit a two-week turnaround on schema changes. If that slips, our monthly reporting slips with it.

**Director:** Fair. I'll put it in the decision note.

**You:** Then I'm in. Disagree and commit. [#146] I'll support it publicly, and I'd like my reservations noted internally [#145] so that if we review this in a year, the reasoning is visible rather than reconstructed.

**Director:** That's reasonable. How will you brief your team?

**You:** Straight. I'll tell them I argued the other way, I lost the argument, and I'm fine with that [#148] — and then I'll tell them exactly what good execution looks like. If I hedge, they'll hedge, and the vendor will get a half-committed client.

**Director:** That's the right call.

**You:** Last thing — can we agree a review point at six months? Not to reopen it, just to check the turnaround assumption. [#143]

**Director:** Yes. Put it in the calendar.

**Why this worked:**
- Dissent was converted into a documented assumption (#131) rather than a lingering grievance, which is the only form of dissent that survives a decision usefully.
- "Disagree and commit" (#146) was made credible by the explicit promise about how the team would be briefed — the words alone would have been cheap.
- Asking for a review point (#143) preserved the possibility of being proved right without reopening a closed decision.

### Scenario 10.4 — A Slack disagreement that's about to escalate

**Setting:** A shared #program-analytics channel. A colleague has posted a chart from your data with a conclusion you think is wrong. Six people are watching.

**Colleague (Slack):** Numbers are in — Cohort B is clearly outperforming A. Recommending we scale the B format. @you can confirm.

**You (Slack):** Genuine question, not a dig [#114] — is that gap adjusted for the intake difference? B started with 60 students, A with 210.

**Colleague (Slack):** The percentages are percentages. 12 points is 12 points.

**You (Slack):** Right, but small numbers make that look bigger than it is [#74] — on 60 students, four people moving flips it. To be clear, I'm disagreeing with the read, not with the work. [#111]

**Colleague (Slack):** So you're saying the analysis is useless?

**You (Slack):** Not at all, and reading my message back, my tone may be sharper than I meant it. [#110] This is easier on a call — do you have five minutes? [#112]

**Colleague (Slack):** Sure, dialling.

*(Ten minutes later, back in channel.)*

**You (Slack):** Quick summary for everyone else — Vikram and I went through it. The direction holds, but the confidence interval on B is wide because of the sample. We're going to rerun with a pooled baseline before recommending a scale-up. Vikram's charting it, I'm supplying the denominators.

**Colleague (Slack):** Agreed. Should have flagged the n on the chart — adding it now.

**You (Slack):** And for the record, the underlying pull was clean. The question was only about what we conclude from it.

**Why this worked:**
- The first message disagreed by asking (#114 plus a probe), which is the only safe opening move in a public written channel.
- The moment the exchange turned personal ("so you're saying it's useless"), the escalation was stopped by owning tone (#110) and leaving the channel (#112) — two messages, no audience damage.
- Returning to the channel with a joint summary and explicit credit repaired the public record, which a private resolution alone would not have done.

### Scenario 10.5 — Refusing to sign off

**Setting:** A quality committee meeting. You are being asked to approve an accreditation submission containing a completion metric you believe is calculated wrongly.

**Chair:** Last item — we need sign-off on the submission pack today.

**You:** I have to stop us here. [#178] I can't sign off on this. [#173]

**Chair:** That's a strong position. What's the issue?

**You:** The completion rate on page nine counts students who transferred out as completers. That's not a judgment call — that's a factual error. [#177] I'd be misrepresenting the data if I agreed to that. [#176]

**Chair:** The deadline is Friday. Can we submit with a note?

**You:** If we publish this, we'll have to retract it [#193] — the accrediting body has our raw enrolment file, so the inconsistency is discoverable. I'd rather we fix a number than explain one.

**Chair:** How long to fix?

**You:** A day, and I'll do it myself. Everything else in the pack I'm comfortable with — this is one metric, not the whole submission.

**Chair:** And if the committee decides to proceed as drafted?

**You:** Then I'd want my dissent minuted, [#172] and I'd ask for the risk owner to be named. [#200] I won't present that number if I'm asked about it in the review. [#192]

**Chair:** Understood. Let's take the day. Revised pack by Thursday noon.

**You:** Thursday noon. Thank you — and thank you for not making me argue for it twice.

**Why this worked:**
- The block came first and the reason second. Level 5 language needs no run-up; a long preamble would have made the refusal sound negotiable.
- The objection was scoped tightly to one metric, which made it easy for the chair to accommodate without the committee losing face.
- The fallback position (#172, #200, #192) was stated calmly and only when asked, showing this was a professional line rather than a threat.

## 10.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| No, that is wrong. | I see it a bit differently — can I show you what I'm looking at? | Opens an investigation instead of a verdict. |
| I don't agree with you. | I'd push back on that slightly. | Targets the idea, not the person. |
| Your analysis is incorrect. | The conclusion may well be right — my issue is the method. | Lets the author keep their footing while you fix the work. |
| This is not possible. | That's going to be difficult with the current pipeline — here's why. | A reason turns a refusal into information. |
| I have a doubt on your logic. | Help me understand the step from the survey to the forecast. | "Doubt" reads as suspicion; this reads as engagement. |
| You are misunderstanding the data. | I think we're reading the same chart differently. | Makes it mutual instead of accusatory. |
| It is not correct as per my knowledge. | That doesn't match what I'm seeing — let's reconcile the two. | Offers a next step instead of a standoff. |
| Sir, I beg to differ. | I take a slightly different view. | Current register; the older phrase now sounds theatrical. |
| Why did you do it like this? | What was the thinking behind this approach? | Same question, no implied blame. |
| That is a very bad idea. | I'd flag one significant risk with that. | Specific and survivable; the original ends the conversation. |
| You should have checked the numbers. | Let's agree who reconciles the numbers before it goes out. | Forward-looking; fixes the process, not the blame. |
| I already told you this last week. | We've been round this twice — can we decide today? | Pushes for closure without scoring a point. |
| Anyway, do as you like. | I disagree, but it's your call — and I'll back it. | Passive aggression versus visible professionalism. |
| I am not happy with this decision. | I'd like to register a concern for the record. | Turns an emotion into a procedural fact. |
| This is your mistake, not mine. | Let's find where the two numbers diverge. | Removes ownership of blame from the sentence entirely. |
| As I mentioned earlier... | Coming back to the intake point — I don't think it's been answered. | Drops the scolding tone, keeps the substance. |
| With due respect, you are not correct. | With respect, that's not what the data shows — can I take you through it? | "Due respect" signals attack; the revision signals evidence. |
| Kindly reconsider the same. | Would you be open to revisiting that? | Natural modern English; "the same" as a pronoun is dated. |
| I told you it would fail. | The assumption we flagged in March is the one that's biting us. | Makes the point without the victory lap. |
| Sorry, sorry, maybe I am wrong, but possibly... | I might be missing something, but — | One softener is credible; four sound like an apology. |
| Please do not take it personally. | To be clear, I'm disagreeing with the approach, not with you. | Names the boundary instead of pre-blaming their reaction. |
| Let us not fight on this. | Can we take the temperature down a notch? | Doesn't label the exchange a fight, which escalates it. |
| Okay okay, fine, we will do it your way. | Fair — you've convinced me on that point. | Concedes with authority instead of surrender. |
| I will do it but I don't agree. | I'll go along with it, with one caveat I'd like noted. | Converts sulking into a documented position. |
| Ultimately it is not my responsibility. | If we're proceeding anyway, I'd want the risk owner named. | Escalates accountability without washing your hands. |

## 10.6 Practice Drills

### Drill 1 — The 10× rep

**Anchor phrase:** "I see it a bit differently."

Say all ten aloud, back to back, at normal speaking pace. Notice how the strength climbs without the volume changing.

1. I see it a bit differently.
2. I see it slightly differently, actually.
3. I'd read that a bit differently.
4. I see it differently — can I explain?
5. I'd look at it the other way round.
6. My read on that is different.
7. I'd take a different view on that one.
8. I see it differently, and I think the data backs me up.
9. I see it very differently, to be honest.
10. I see this quite differently, and I'd like to explain why before we decide.

### Drill 2 — Slot swap

**Pattern:** `The [CONCLUSION] may well be right — my issue is [THE METHOD].`

Speak each one out loud, without pausing between the dash and the second half:

1. The conclusion may well be right — my issue is that it rests on 38 responses.
2. The recommendation may well be right — my issue is there's no comparison group.
3. The direction may well be right — my issue is the baseline changed in April.
4. The forecast may well be right — my issue is it assumes intake stays flat.
5. The headline may well be right — my issue is the two cohorts aren't comparable.
6. The vendor's claim may well be right — my issue is we haven't tested it at our volume.
7. The finding may well be right — my issue is the survey went out during exam week.
8. The scoring may well be right — my issue is who decided the weights.

### Drill 3 — Cold open

One sentence each. Say it aloud within three seconds of reading the prompt.

1. Your manager says, in front of six people, "The dashboard shows enrolment is down 12%." You know that number includes a duplicated feed. Respond in one sentence.
2. A vendor says their model "will definitely improve accuracy by 20%." Respond in one sentence that questions the claim without insulting them.
3. A peer messages on Slack: "Your query's wrong, I've got different numbers." Respond in one written sentence that de-escalates and moves to a call.
4. You've just been overruled on a tooling decision in a leadership meeting. The Dean asks, "Are you on board?" Respond in one sentence.
5. You're asked to approve a report containing a metric you know is miscalculated, and the deadline is tomorrow. Respond in one sentence.

### Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt

Paste this into an AI assistant and run it twice — once where you stay at level 2, once where you're forced up to level 4.

```
You are Margarethe, a 52-year-old Dutch Director of Institutional Research at a
European business school. You are direct to the point of bluntness, you have 20
years of experience, and you are visibly impatient with hedging. You believe the
year-on-year completion comparison you have prepared is correct and you have
already circulated it to the Dean's office.

I am a senior program analytics manager who believes your comparison is invalid
because the intake grew 40% and you have not adjusted the denominator.

Roleplay a 12-turn one-to-one conversation. Rules:
- Do not concede easily. Push back at least three times.
- Twice, misinterpret something I say as a personal criticism of your team, and
  react to that.
- Once, put me under time pressure ("the pack goes out in an hour").
- Stay in character. Give me only your lines, one turn at a time, and wait for
  my reply.

After the 12 turns, break character and give me feedback on:
1. Which strength level (1-5) I opened at, and whether it was calibrated right.
2. Any sentence where I attacked the person rather than the method or the data.
3. Where I over-hedged - list the exact filler words I stacked.
4. Whether I gave you a face-saving route to change your position, and if not,
   the exact sentence I could have used.
5. How I closed: did I secure a next step, or just end the argument?
```

Afterwards, re-run the same prompt but change Margarethe to a warm, conflict-avoidant Singaporean program director who agrees with everything you say in the meeting and then does the opposite. Disagreeing with someone who never openly disagrees back is a different skill, and most people are worse at it.

## 10.7 Master these 10 first

1. **I see it a bit differently.** — Universal safe opener, any room.
2. **What's the assumption behind that?** — Attacks the premise, not the person.
3. **I'm with you on the goal, not on the method.** — Separates the what from how.
4. **The conclusion may well be right — my issue is the method.** — Criticize work without wounding its author.
5. **I'd rather [X], because [Y].** — Turns objection into a proposal.
6. **Can I have five minutes before this goes out?** — Moves disagreement out of public view.
7. **I want to flag a risk, then I'll back your decision.** — Full upward dissent in one sentence.
8. **That's a fair challenge — here's what's behind it.** — Absorbs a public attack calmly.
9. **This is easier on a call — do you have five minutes?** — Stops written disputes from escalating.
10. **I can't sign off on this.** — Your one real block; spend it rarely.


---

# Chapter 11 — Negotiation

> **One-line promise:** You'll be able to turn an impossible request into a choice the other person has to make — without ever saying the word "no."

## 11.1 Why this chapter matters

- The most common failure mode for a senior data professional is the **silent yes**. Someone asks for a two-week report in two days, you feel the pressure, you say "I'll try" — and now you own a failure that was never yours. "I'll try" is not a negotiation. It's a slow no with your name on it.
- The second failure mode is the opposite: flat refusal. "That is not possible" is grammatically perfect and professionally expensive. It reads as obstruction, it ends the conversation, and it teaches people to route around you next time.
- The move that separates senior people from delivery people is **trade language**: *if we do X, then Y has to move.* You don't refuse the request — you hand back the consequence and let the other person choose. The decision stays with them, the constraint becomes visible, and you stop being the bottleneck in the story.
- Non-native speakers often over-apologize into a weak position ("Sorry sir, actually it is very difficult from our side") or over-formalize into a cold one ("Kindly note the same is not feasible"). Both cost you the middle register — calm, specific, unbothered — which is where negotiating power actually lives.
- Negotiation isn't only about money. In a week you will negotiate a deadline, a scope, a definition, a queue position, a license renewal, and somebody's expectation of your team. If you only practice negotiating in the salary conversation once a year, you'll be bad at it in the one conversation that pays.
- What changes when you fix this: you stop absorbing other people's planning failures, your team stops burning weekends, and your "yes" starts meaning something — because people have watched you decline things cleanly and survive.

## 11.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)

Use these until they're automatic.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | What's driving the deadline? | Testing whether a date is real | Neutral |
| 2 | Help me understand the priority here. | Two things arrive at once | Neutral |
| 3 | If we do that, something else has to move. | The core trade sentence | Neutral |
| 4 | Let me make sure I understand what you need. | Before you agree to anything | Neutral |
| 5 | What does "done" look like for you? | Scope is vague | Neutral |
| 6 | Is this a hard deadline or a target? | The single highest-value question | Neutral |
| 7 | Can you tell me what it's for? | Purpose changes the effort | Neutral |
| 8 | I can do that — here's what it costs. | Accepting with a price attached | Neutral |
| 9 | Which of the two do you want first? | Forcing a choice, politely | Neutral |
| 10 | I'd rather get you something useful than something fast. | Pushing back on speed | Neutral |
| 11 | Let me be straight with you about the timeline. | Opening an honest reset | Neutral |
| 12 | Two weeks is realistic; two days isn't. | Naming the gap plainly | Neutral |
| 13 | What would a good outcome look like on your side? | Finding their real need | Neutral |
| 14 | You'll have a first cut by Thursday. | Offering a smaller commitment | Neutral |
| 15 | Would a partial answer help in the meantime? | Buying time with value | Neutral |
| 16 | Let me talk you through the trade-off. | Making the cost visible | Neutral |
| 17 | That's doable if we drop the segmentation. | Conditional yes | Neutral |
| 18 | Happy to give you less, sooner, with the gaps flagged. | Offering a scoped-down version | Neutral |
| 19 | Can we agree on scope first, then dates? | Stopping a date-first conversation | Neutral |
| 20 | Who else is depending on this? | Mapping the real pressure | Neutral |
| 21 | What happens if this lands a week later? | Testing the consequence | Neutral |
| 22 | Let me check with my team before I commit. | Never commit in the room | Neutral |
| 23 | Give me an hour and I'll come back with options. | Buying thinking time | Neutral |
| 24 | By end of day you'll have a plan from me. | Committing to a process, not a result | Neutral |
| 25 | I don't want to promise something I can't hold. | Declining softly | Neutral |
| 26 | Here's what I can commit to today. | Moving to firm ground | Neutral |
| 27 | Is there any flex on the date? | Probing for movement | Neutral |
| 28 | How firm is that number? | Probing a price or figure | Neutral |
| 29 | Let's separate what's urgent from what's important. | Reframing a pile of requests | Neutral |
| 30 | I hear you — and I've got a constraint. | Acknowledging without folding | Neutral |
| 31 | That's fair. Here's my problem with it. | Conceding then pushing | Neutral |
| 32 | I'm not saying no — I'm saying not by Friday. | The single best "no" | Neutral |
| 33 | Yes, if we can move the pipeline work. | Conditional acceptance | Neutral |
| 34 | Happy to do it, with one condition. | Attaching a price | Neutral |
| 35 | Can we start with the version that's cheap to build? | Steering to a small first step | Neutral |
| 36 | What's the minimum you need to make the decision? | Cutting scope by purpose | Neutral |
| 37 | Would 80% on Tuesday beat 100% next month? | Trading precision for speed | Neutral |
| 38 | Let me show you what's in the queue right now. | Making capacity visible | Neutral |
| 39 | The team is fully committed through month-end. | Stating capacity as fact | Neutral |

## 11.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `If we [DO X], then [Y] has to move.`
- If we pull the placement report forward, then the data-quality fixes have to move.
- If we add the European cohort, then the go-live has to move to the fifteenth.
- If we do this in two days, then the reconciliation step has to come out.
*Watch out:* Name a specific victim, not a vague one. "Then something else will be affected" is noise; "then the faculty dashboard slips to the 20th" is a decision the other person now has to make.

**Pattern 2:** `I can protect [A] or [B] — not both. Which one?`
- I can protect the date or the accuracy — not both. Which one matters more on Wednesday?
- I can protect the scope or the weekend — not both. Which do you want?
- I can protect the model quality or the demo date — not both. Your call.
*Watch out:* Ask the question and then stop talking. If you fill the silence with "but I'll try my best", you've handed the choice back to yourself and rebuilt the trap you just escaped.

**Pattern 3:** `Yes, if [CONDITION].`
- Yes, if I can drop the segment-level breakdown.
- Yes, if you can get Ravi to sign off on the definition by tomorrow.
- Yes, if the March refresh moves out by a week.
*Watch out:* Lead with "Yes". The word does enormous work — it makes the whole sentence sound cooperative even though it is, structurally, a refusal of the original request.

**Pattern 4:** `Is [DATE] a hard deadline, or a target? What's driving it?`
- Is Friday a hard deadline, or a target? What's driving it?
- Is the 30th hard, or is that when the deck gets built?
- Is that date coming from the board meeting, or from us?
*Watch out:* Ask this in the first two minutes, before you've said anything about feasibility. Once you've said "that's very tight", the other person hears the question as bargaining rather than scoping.

**Pattern 5:** `Taking this on means [CONSEQUENCE]. What would you like me to deprioritize?`
- Taking this on means the cohort refresh doesn't happen this week. What would you like me to deprioritize?
- Saying yes to this means Anjali stops the migration testing. What should come off the list?
- If this jumps the queue, the accreditation numbers wait. Which one do you want?
*Watch out:* Say "deprioritize", not "cancel" or "drop". Cancel sounds like a threat; deprioritize sounds like planning, and people answer planning questions.

**Pattern 6:** `That's [REAL EFFORT], not [ASSUMED EFFORT].`
- That's about three days of work, not three hours.
- That's a rebuild, not a filter change.
- That's a new data source, not a new column.
*Watch out:* Give the real number without the sigh. Effort correction works because it's factual; the moment it sounds aggrieved, it reads as complaining and people discount it.

**Pattern 7:** `Would [PARTIAL] by [EARLY DATE] beat [COMPLETE] by [LATE DATE]?`
- Would headline numbers on Wednesday beat the full breakdown on the 20th?
- Would a directional estimate today beat a validated one next week?
- Would three programs by Friday beat all eleven by month-end?
*Watch out:* Make the early option genuinely useful, not a token. If your partial version can't answer the question they actually have, they'll say no and you'll have burned the move.

**Pattern 8:** `What would it take to [OUTCOME]?`
- What would it take to get the license down to last year's rate?
- What would it take to get to the top of the band?
- What would it take to have this signed off by Thursday?
*Watch out:* This is a question, so stop at the question mark. Adding "because I really can't go higher" turns an open probe into a confession about your own limits.

**Pattern 9:** `I'm not saying no — I'm saying [SPECIFIC ALTERNATIVE].`
- I'm not saying no — I'm saying not by Friday.
- I'm not saying no to the upgrade — I'm saying not this renewal.
- I'm not saying we can't build it — I'm saying not with one analyst.
*Watch out:* The second half must be narrower than the first. "I'm not saying no, I'm saying let's discuss" is a non-answer, and experienced people will read it as a yes.

**Pattern 10:** `Option A gets you [X]. Option B gets you [Y]. Which risk would you rather carry?`
- Option A gets you the number Tuesday with a caveat. Option B gets you a clean number on the 22nd. Which risk would you rather carry?
- Option A is the vendor's platform in eight weeks. Option B is ours in five months and cheaper.
- Option A is the model as-is. Option B is two more weeks of tuning and a better recall number.
*Watch out:* Two options, never three. Three options is a menu, and people order the one that's cheapest for them and hardest for you.

**Pattern 11:** `That's a move from my side — I'd want [RETURN] in exchange.`
- That's a move from my side — I'd want the requirements frozen after Friday.
- Happy to bring the date in — I'd want the second analyst for those two weeks.
- I'll take the extra cohort — I'd want the QA sign-off moved to your team.
*Watch out:* Ask for the return in the same breath. A concession you give now and invoice for later is a favor, and favors don't show up in anyone's ledger but yours.

**Pattern 12:** `Before we talk [PRICE / DATE], can we talk [SCOPE / PURPOSE]?`
- Before we talk price, can we talk about what's actually included?
- Before we talk dates, can we talk about what this report needs to answer?
- Before we talk headcount, can we talk about what we're taking off the roadmap?
*Watch out:* Use this early or not at all. Once a number is on the table, everything you say is measured against it, and you've lost control of the frame.

**Pattern 13:** `Based on [EVIDENCE], I'm looking for [ASK].`
- Based on the two systems I took over this year, I'm looking for a move to the senior band.
- Based on what comparable roles are paying, I'm looking for something in the 18 to 22 range.
- Based on the delivery record last cycle, I'm looking for a second analyst before we commit to the roadmap.
*Watch out:* Evidence first, ask second. The reverse order — ask then justification — sounds defensive, and the justification gets heard as an apology for the ask.

**Pattern 14:** `If I can solve [BLOCKER], do we have an agreement?`
- If I can get the security review done by the 10th, do we have an agreement?
- If I can free up Anjali for a week, are you happy with the 22nd?
- If procurement approves the three-year term, are we agreed on the rate?
*Watch out:* Only use this when you can genuinely solve the blocker. Asking it and then failing to deliver costs you the credibility that made the move work in the first place.

**Pattern 15:** `So to confirm — [WHAT THEY GET], [WHAT YOU GET], by [DATE]. I'll put that in writing.`
- So to confirm — you get the headline numbers Wednesday, I get till the 22nd for the full cut. I'll write that up today.
- So to confirm — we hold the current rate for two years, you get the signature this week.
- So to confirm — the accreditation pack goes first, the placement dashboard moves to April. I'll send that round.
*Watch out:* Say the whole trade, both halves, out loud. If you only confirm what they get, that's the half that ends up in everyone's memory of the meeting.

## 11.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 11.1 — A two-week report, wanted in two days

**Setting:** Thursday afternoon. The Program Director stops by your desk and asks for a full placement-outcomes report by Monday morning. Done properly, it's two weeks of work.

**Program Director:** I need the full placement outcomes report by Monday morning. All programs, three-year trend, split by sector.

**You:** Let me make sure I understand what you need. [#4] What's driving Monday? [#1]

**Program Director:** The Dean's meeting the advisory board on Tuesday.

**You:** Understood. Is Monday a hard deadline, or is that when the deck gets built? [#6]

**Program Director:** The deck gets built Monday evening. So Monday, effectively.

**You:** Okay. I'll be straight with you about the timeline. [#11] The full version — three years, all programs, sector splits — is about two weeks of work. The sector mapping alone isn't clean in the source system; that's five days on its own. Two weeks is realistic; two days isn't. [#12]

**Program Director:** I can't tell the Dean that.

**You:** You won't have to. What's the minimum the advisory board needs to have the conversation? [#36]

**Program Director:** Honestly? Headline placement rate, median salary, and whether we're up or down on last year.

**You:** Then that's a different job. Those three numbers, all programs, one year of comparison — that I can have with you Monday morning. The sector splits and the three-year trend come later. Would that beat waiting for the complete version? [#37]

**Program Director:** That would work. Can I get the sector split too?

**You:** If we add the sector split, then the Monday date has to move. [#3] The mapping is the expensive part. Here are two options — neither is perfect. [#136] Option A: three headline numbers Monday, sector detail on the 20th. Option B: everything together, on the 20th. Which risk would you rather carry going into Tuesday? [#138]

**Program Director:** A. Give me the headlines Monday.

**You:** Done. So to confirm — you get placement rate, median salary and year-on-year on Monday morning; the sector and three-year work lands on the 20th. [#53] I'll put that in writing so nothing gets lost. [#52]

**Why this worked:**
- Two clarifying questions before any resistance — the deadline got tested [#1, #6] before you spent any credibility arguing about feasibility.
- "What's the minimum you need to make the decision?" [#36] converted an impossible scope into a possible one by going after the *purpose*, not the request.
- The trade sentence [#3] was followed immediately by two structured options, so the director made the cut himself and left owning the decision.

### Scenario 11.2 — Two directors, one week of capacity

**Setting:** Both the Accreditation Director and the Marketing Director have asked for your team's full attention next week. You're on a three-way call you convened.

**You:** Thanks, both. I asked for fifteen minutes because you're both asking for the same week of my team's time. [#64] I want to be upfront rather than quietly disappoint one of you.

**Accreditation Director:** The submission window closes on the 30th. That's not movable.

**Marketing Director:** And the campaign numbers are useless to me after the intake cycle starts.

**You:** Right — so let me put the capacity math on the table. [#143] Next week I have Anjali and Ravi, that's it. The accreditation data pack is four days of Anjali's time. The campaign attribution rebuild is five days of Ravi's, and it needs Anjali for the join logic on at least two of them. That's one and a half people of work, and I have one. [#144]

**Marketing Director:** Can't some of it run in parallel?

**You:** Some of it. Not the part that needs the same person. If we run the attribution rebuild in that week, then the accreditation pack slips past the 30th. [#3]

**Accreditation Director:** That's a regulatory deadline.

**Marketing Director:** And mine's a business one.

**You:** I'm not going to pick between the two of you — that's not my call to make, and either way one of you finds out from an email. [#123] I'd rather you two agree than have me guess. [#67] I'll do whichever you decide — I just need a decision today. [#68]

**Accreditation Director:** Can marketing take a week's delay?

**Marketing Director:** A week, maybe. Not three.

**You:** Then here's a proposal. Accreditation goes first, Anjali is on it Monday to Thursday. Ravi starts the attribution scaffolding in that same week — the parts that don't need her — and the join logic lands the following Monday. You'd have campaign numbers by the 6th instead of the 1st. That's a move from my side on sequencing; what I'd want in return is no new requests into either workstream until the 6th. [#111]

**Marketing Director:** The 6th works.

**Accreditation Director:** Agreed.

**You:** Good. Anything else before we call this agreed? [#167] I'll send a short note confirming the three points this afternoon. [#118]

**Why this worked:**
- Naming the collision out loud [#64] moved the conflict from your inbox to the room where it could actually be resolved.
- The capacity math [#143, #144] made the constraint arithmetic rather than opinion — nobody argues with "one and a half people and I have one."
- The concession came with an explicit price [#111] — a request freeze — so a compromise didn't quietly become a new baseline.

### Scenario 11.3 — The salary conversation

**Setting:** Annual review. Your manager has just told you the increment is 6%, in line with the standard band.

**Manager:** So it's 6% this year, which is the standard increment. I know it's not dramatic, but it's what the band allows.

**You:** Thanks for being straight about it. Before I react to the number, help me understand how the band was set. [#96]

**Manager:** It's the mid-band for a senior analyst, adjusted for the school's overall pool.

**You:** That's useful. Let me walk you through what I've taken on this year, [#100] because I think the role has changed underneath the band. I took over the accreditation reporting pipeline in March after Deepak left — that was never in my scope. The faculty dashboards moved from a monthly manual build to an automated refresh, which gave the program office about six days a month back. And I've been the one in the room with the vendor on the license renegotiation. Based on that, I think 6% is low. [#97]

**Manager:** I don't disagree with any of that. But the pool is the pool.

**You:** Understood — and I'd like to talk about the level, not just the number. [#101] If the band is the constraint, then the conversation is whether I'm still in the right band. What would it take to get to the top of the band, or to the next one? [#98]

**Manager:** Realistically, the next band needs formal ownership of a function, not just delivery.

**You:** That's clear, and it's fair. So what would you need to see from me? [#104] If it's ownership of the analytics function end to end, I'd want to know what that looks like in writing, and by when it gets reviewed.

**Manager:** Give me a month. Let me talk to the Associate Dean about scope.

**You:** I'd like to close this before the cycle ends. [#105] Can we agree the principle now — that the level gets reviewed on the ownership question — and work the detail over the month? [#106]

**Manager:** I can agree to that.

**You:** Then here's my ask, and here's the reasoning. [#154] I'll take the 6% for this cycle. What I want in exchange is a written scope for the function-ownership role and a decision on band by the end of Q3. That's a concession from my side on the number; I'd want the timeline commitment back. [#111]

**Manager:** That's reasonable. Put it in an email and I'll confirm.

**You:** I'll send it today so we're both working off the same text. [#169]

**Why this worked:**
- The first move was a question, not a reaction [#96] — it bought information and kept the number from becoming the whole conversation.
- Evidence came before the ask [#100, #97], and the ask widened from money to level [#101], which is where the real leverage sits when the pool is genuinely fixed.
- The close traded a soft concession for a hard, dated commitment [#111, #105] and moved it into writing the same day [#169].

### Scenario 11.4 — The vendor and the upgrade you don't need

**Setting:** Renewal call with your BI platform vendor. The account manager is pushing an enterprise tier that costs 40% more.

**Account Manager:** So renewal's due next month, and I'd strongly recommend moving to the enterprise tier. You get advanced governance, the AI assistant, and unlimited row-level security policies.

**You:** Before we talk price, can we talk scope? [#77] Walk me through how you got to the enterprise recommendation. [#78]

**Account Manager:** Your usage has grown — you're at 180 users now, and enterprise is really designed for organizations at your scale.

**You:** We're at 180 named users, of whom about 60 log in monthly. What breaks if we don't upgrade? [#90]

**Account Manager:** Nothing breaks immediately. But you'd be capped on RLS policies at 25, and you're at 19.

**You:** So the real constraint is six policies of headroom.

**Account Manager:** Roughly, yes. And governance becomes harder to manage at scale.

**You:** We're not feeling that pain today. [#91] Our policies map to program groups and we've added two in eighteen months. Let's take the enterprise tier off the table for now [#88] and talk about the standard renewal. What's the price on a straight renewal at current volume?

**Account Manager:** It'd be a 9% uplift on last year, in line with our list movement.

**You:** That's higher than I was expecting. [#79] What would it take to hold the current rate? [#81]

**Account Manager:** Holding flat is difficult. There might be something on a multi-year.

**You:** Then let's look at that. A longer term is possible if the price comes down. [#87] If you can hold the current rate flat for two years, I can get this signed this month rather than dragging it into the next quarter. [#86]

**Account Manager:** Two years flat is a stretch. I could do flat year one, 4% year two.

**You:** Halfway between us is flat year one and 2% year two — can we work with that? [#109] And I'd want the RLS cap lifted to 40 as part of it, since that was your case for enterprise in the first place.

**Account Manager:** Let me check on the cap. The pricing I think I can do.

**You:** Take your time — I'm happy to wait. [#159]

**Account Manager:** ...I can probably get the cap to 35.

**You:** That works. If you can confirm the cap at 35 and flat-then-2%, do we have an agreement? [#166]

**Account Manager:** I'd say yes, subject to my director signing off.

**You:** Good. I'd like that in the contract, not in an email. [#93] Send me the redlined terms and I'll route them through procurement this week.

**Why this worked:**
- The upgrade was dismantled by testing consequence, not price [#90] — once "nothing breaks" was said out loud, the enterprise tier had no argument left.
- Removing the upgrade from the table [#88] shrank the negotiation to one variable, where a two-year term could be traded for a flat rate [#86, #87].
- The pause after the counter [#159] did the last piece of work; the account manager improved his own offer into the silence.

### Scenario 11.5 — Asking for headcount you actually need

**Setting:** Roadmap planning with the Associate Dean. She's just presented six initiatives for next year and asked you to commit to all of them.

**Associate Dean:** So that's the six. Can your team commit to delivering these next year?

**You:** Let me tell you what saying yes to all six would mean, [#69] and then you can decide. Two of them — the admissions funnel model and the alumni outcomes platform — are each a full quarter of two people. The other four are roughly a quarter between them. That's about five quarters of work in four, with the team I have.

**Associate Dean:** Can we not be efficient about it?

**You:** We can be efficient about the four small ones — a lot of that is shared plumbing. The two big ones don't compress; they're new data sources, not new reports. [Pattern 6] That's one and a half people of work, and I have one. [#144]

**Associate Dean:** So which one drops?

**You:** That's one option, and I'd rather offer you a better one. Give me one more analyst and I'll commit to the full roadmap. [#141] Without that hire, this is the list I can deliver: [#142] the four smaller initiatives and the admissions model, with the alumni platform starting in Q4 and landing the following year.

**Associate Dean:** Headcount is genuinely hard this cycle.

**You:** Then let's talk about the alternative shapes. A twelve-month contractor for the alumni platform costs less than the delay does — that initiative is tied to the fundraising cycle, and a two-quarter slip pushes it past the campaign. [#146]

**Associate Dean:** How confident are you in that estimate?

**You:** Confident on the shape, less on the tail. Let me show you the capacity math [#143] rather than ask you to take my word for it — I'll send the breakdown by initiative this afternoon.

**Associate Dean:** Do that. If the numbers hold, I'll take the contractor case to the budget meeting.

**You:** That's all I'd ask. And to be clear about the fallback — if the contractor doesn't land, I'll come back with the five-initiative version rather than quietly running six and missing four. [#131]

**Associate Dean:** Appreciated.

**You:** I'll send the breakdown today, and unless I hear otherwise, we'll plan against the five-initiative version until the budget meeting. [#120]

**Why this worked:**
- The answer to "can you commit to all six" was neither yes nor no — it was the consequence of yes [#69], stated as arithmetic.
- The headcount ask came bundled with a delivery commitment [#141] and a concrete fallback list [#142], which turns a request into a business case.
- The default was set in writing [#120], so silence from the budget meeting works in your favor instead of against it.

## 11.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| It is not possible. | Not by Friday — here's what is possible by Friday. | Replaces a wall with a door; keeps you in the conversation |
| I will try my best. | Here's what I can commit to today. | "I'll try" is a slow no; a commitment is a real answer |
| That is very difficult from our side. | That's about three days of work, not three hours. | Facts beat feelings; effort in days can't be argued with |
| Kindly note the timeline is not feasible. | Let me be straight with you about the timeline. | Drops dated register, keeps the same message |
| Sir, we are already overloaded. | The team is fully committed through month-end. | States capacity as fact, not as a complaint |
| Why do you need it so fast? | What's driving the deadline? | Same question, no defensiveness in it |
| Okay, I'll manage somehow. | Yes, if the March refresh moves out a week. | Turns silent absorption into a visible trade |
| Please give more time. | Would headline numbers Wednesday beat the full cut on the 20th? | Offers a solution instead of asking for mercy |
| We cannot do both. | I can protect the date or the scope — not both. Which one? | Ends with a question, so the decision moves to them |
| You decide, I'll do whatever. | I'll do whichever you decide — I just need a decision today. | Same flexibility, with a deadline attached |
| This is not my priority. | Let's separate what's urgent from what's important. | Depersonalizes the pushback |
| That's too expensive. | That's higher than I was expecting. What's included at that price? | Keeps negotiating instead of closing the topic |
| Give me your best price. | What would it take to hold the current rate? | Invites a path to yes rather than a single number |
| We don't need this upgrade. | What breaks if we don't upgrade? | Makes them dismantle their own pitch |
| I deserve a raise. | Based on what I've taken on this year, that's low. | Evidence-anchored, not entitlement-anchored |
| I am not happy with this increment. | I'd like to talk about the level, not just the number. | Moves to the variable that can actually move |
| What is my salary going to be? | I'm looking for something in the range of X to Y. | Anchors first instead of waiting to be anchored |
| Can you please consider increasing it? | What would it take to get to the top of the band? | Turns a plea into a question about criteria |
| I need more people. | Give me one more analyst and I'll commit to the full roadmap. | Trades a resource for a delivery guarantee |
| We'll see how it goes. | Let's write down what we've agreed while it's fresh. | Closes the loop before memory diverges |
| As discussed earlier, kindly confirm. | Correct me if I've got any of this wrong. | Invites objection now instead of a dispute later |
| I already told you this last month. | Help me understand what's changed since we agreed this. | Holds the line without accusing anyone |
| Fine, do whatever you want. | I'd like my objection on record, and then I'm behind the decision. | Keeps your standing while losing the point |
| If you don't agree, we'll go elsewhere. | If there's no movement on that, I'll look at alternatives. | Same leverage, no aggression to react to |
| Let me know your decision soon. | If the answer's no, I'd rather know now. | Creates urgency without sounding needy |

## 11.6 Practice Drills

**Drill 1 — The 10× rep**

Anchor phrase: **"If we do that, something else has to move."** [#3]

Say each variation out loud, twice, with a two-second pause after it. Do not add "but I'll try."

1. If we do that, something else has to move.
2. If we pull this forward, the pipeline work slips two weeks.
3. If we add the sector split, then Monday has to move.
4. If this jumps the queue, the accreditation pack waits.
5. If I take that on, nothing else moves this week.
6. If we hold the date, the segment detail comes out.
7. If we bring in the European cohort, go-live moves to the fifteenth.
8. If Anjali goes to your workstream, the migration testing stops.
9. If we keep all six initiatives, the alumni platform lands next year.
10. If we compress the timeline, the reconciliation step is the thing that goes.

**Drill 2 — Slot swap**

Pattern: `I can protect [A] or [B] — not both. Which one?`

Speak all eight. Then speak them again and stay silent for a full four seconds after each question.

1. the date / the accuracy
2. the scope / the go-live
3. the model quality / the demo date
4. all eleven programs / the Friday deadline
5. the automated refresh / the ad-hoc requests
6. the price / the two-year term
7. Anjali's time / the testing window
8. the full trend history / the Monday delivery

**Drill 3 — Cold open**

One sentence only, out loud, within three seconds of reading each prompt. No preamble, no apology.

1. A director says: "It's just a small change, can you squeeze it in by tomorrow?" — and it's two days of work.
2. Your manager says: "6% is what the band allows." — and you've absorbed a departed colleague's entire scope.
3. A vendor says: "This offer expires Friday." — and you haven't finished evaluating the alternative.
4. Two stakeholders are cc'd on the same email, both saying "this is the priority."
5. The Dean says: "Can your team commit to all six of these next year?" — and it's five quarters of work in four.

**Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt**

```
You are Marcus Hale, a 51-year-old regional sales director for an
enterprise analytics vendor. You have been selling software for
twenty-six years. You are warm, funny, extremely likeable, and you use
that warmth as a pressure tool. You never sound aggressive; you sound
like a friend who is disappointed. Your quarter closes in nine days and
you need this renewal at the enterprise tier.

I am a senior analytics lead at a business school in India. Our BI
platform renewal is due next month. We have 180 named users, about 60
active monthly, and 19 row-level-security policies against a cap of 25.
The enterprise tier costs 40% more than what we pay now. I do not
believe we need it.

Run the call in character, one turn at a time. Do the following:
- Open with two minutes of genuine relationship warmth before any pricing.
- Use scarcity at least twice ("this pricing is only available until...").
- When I push back on the upgrade, agree with me sympathetically and
  then reframe the same upgrade as risk mitigation.
- If I ask for a flat renewal, quote a 9% uplift and call it "list movement".
- At least once, go quiet and wait for me to fill the silence.
- Near the end, try to get a verbal commitment without written terms.

Stay in character until I write "END SCENE".

After END SCENE, drop character and give me feedback on:
1. Whether I tested the upgrade's necessity or argued about its price —
   and which one I should have done first.
2. Every point where I conceded something without asking for anything back.
3. How I handled your silence — did I fill it, and what did filling it cost me?
4. Whether my final close was specific enough to survive procurement,
   with a rewritten version of my closing lines.
5. One phrase I used that sounded either apologetic or aggressive, and
   the neutral version I should drill before my next vendor call.
```

## 11.7 Master these 10 first

1. **If we do that, something else has to move.** [#3] — The whole chapter in one sentence.
2. **Is this a hard deadline or a target?** [#6] — Most deadlines fail this test.
3. **I'm not saying no — I'm saying not by Friday.** [#32] — Refuses without ever refusing.
4. **What would you like me to deprioritize?** [#41] — Hands the cut back upward.
5. **I can protect the date or the scope — not both.** [#62] — Forces one clean choice.
6. **What's the minimum you need to make the decision?** [#36] — Shrinks impossible scope fast.
7. **What would it take to get there?** [#81] — Opens a path to yes.
8. **That's a concession from my side — I'd want something back.** [#111] — Stops one-way generosity.
9. **Take your time — I'm happy to wait.** [#159] — Silence does your negotiating.
10. **So to confirm — you get X, I get Y.** [#53] — Closes it before memory drifts.


---

# Chapter 12 — Leadership

> **One-line promise:** You'll be able to set direction, delegate, give real feedback, absorb bad news and hand work away — in language that sounds like someone people want to work for, whether or not anyone reports to you.

## 12.1 Why this chapter matters

- Leadership language is the one area where being *too polite* does as much damage as being too blunt. "Please try to improve the quality" is so soft that the person walks out thinking the conversation went fine. Three months later they're blindsided at appraisal time, and that's your failure, not theirs.
- The most common non-native pattern here is issuing instructions instead of giving context. "Do the reconciliation by Friday" produces a reconciliation. "Finance is questioning our conversion numbers in Wednesday's board pack, so I need the reconciliation clean by Friday" produces someone who catches the thing you didn't think to ask for.
- Positive feedback fails for the opposite reason — it's generic. "Good job", "well done", "keep it up" costs you nothing and buys you nothing. One specific sentence naming what they did and what it changed is worth more than a month of "well done".
- Checking in and micromanaging use almost the same words. The difference is whether you name why you're asking and what you're offering. "Where are we on this?" reads as chasing. "Not chasing — I just want to know if you need anything from me before Thursday" reads as cover.
- The senior moves in this chapter are the ones people notice: giving credit away in a leadership review, taking blame that could have been pushed downward, saying no on behalf of your team, and stepping back so someone else leads. These are rare in any given week and they define how you're seen over a year.
- You don't need direct reports for any of it. Analysts lead vendors, peers, interns, cross-functional squads and their own managers. The language is the same.

## 12.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)

Use these until they're automatic.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 1 | Here's what we're trying to get to. | Framing a goal before the work | Neutral |
| 2 | Let me give you the context first. | Before you hand over any task | Neutral |
| 3 | The outcome I care about is the clean number. | Naming the goal, not the method | Neutral |
| 4 | I'll leave the how to you. | Delegating with real autonomy | Neutral |
| 5 | Does that give you enough to start? | Checking your brief actually landed | Neutral |
| 6 | What would you need from me to make that work? | Removing blockers before they appear | Neutral |
| 7 | That one's yours to run. | Handing over ownership cleanly | Neutral |
| 8 | You don't need to check with me on that. | Widening someone's authority | Casual |
| 9 | Take a first pass and we'll look at it together. | Delegating a draft, not a deliverable | Neutral |
| 10 | Where are you with this? | Light, neutral check-in | Neutral |
| 11 | How's it going with the pipeline rebuild? | Opening a status check warmly | Casual |
| 12 | Anything blocking you? | Standard check-in question | Neutral |
| 13 | What's in your way? | Same idea, more active | Neutral |
| 14 | Do you have what you need? | Support check, not a status check | Neutral |
| 15 | Shout if you get stuck. | Closing a handover | Casual |
| 16 | Nice work on that. | Quick positive feedback in passing | Casual |
| 17 | That was a really clean piece of work. | Praising quality specifically | Neutral |
| 18 | That saved us a lot of time. | Naming the impact of good work | Neutral |
| 19 | I noticed you rebuilt the join logic — that mattered. | Specific recognition, low effort | Neutral |
| 20 | Thanks for picking that up. | Someone took initiative unprompted | Casual |
| 21 | That's exactly what I was hoping for. | Confirming a good output | Neutral |
| 22 | You called that early — good instinct. | Praising judgment, not just output | Casual |
| 23 | What's your read on it? | Coaching instead of answering | Neutral |
| 24 | What would you do if I weren't here? | Pushing back an unnecessary escalation | Neutral |
| 25 | Talk me through your thinking. | Opening a coaching conversation | Neutral |
| 26 | What have you tried so far? | Before you offer any help | Neutral |
| 27 | What are the options as you see them? | Forcing structured thinking | Neutral |
| 28 | Which one would you go with? | Making them commit to a recommendation | Neutral |
| 29 | Go with that. | Fast approval, no ceremony | Casual |
| 30 | I'm happy either way — you pick. | Delegating a low-stakes call | Casual |
| 31 | Let's make sure we're aligned on priorities. | Start of a heavy week | Neutral |
| 32 | If everything's a priority, nothing is. | Forcing a trade-off | Neutral |
| 33 | What would you drop to make room for this? | New work lands mid-sprint | Neutral |
| 34 | Right now, this is the one that matters. | Setting a single focus | Neutral |
| 35 | Let's park the rest until this ships. | Narrowing the team's scope | Neutral |
| 36 | I want to be clear about what done looks like. | Preventing rework before it starts | Neutral |
| 37 | By when can you have a first version? | Asking them to set the date | Neutral |
| 38 | Let's put a date on it. | Turning intent into commitment | Neutral |
| 39 | Can I hold you to Thursday? | Confirming a commitment out loud | Casual |
| 40 | I'll check in with you Wednesday. | Scheduled follow-up, not a surprise one | Neutral |
| 41 | Not chasing — just want to know if you need anything. | Softening a check-in | Casual |
| 42 | Tell me if that changes. | Building an early-warning habit | Neutral |
| 43 | I'd rather hear it early than late. | Inviting bad news | Neutral |
| 44 | Bad news doesn't get better with time. | Encouraging fast escalation | Casual |
| 45 | That's a fair call. | Backing someone's decision | Neutral |
| 46 | I'd have done the same. | Reassuring after a judgment call | Casual |
| 47 | You've got my backing on that. | Giving cover explicitly | Neutral |
| 48 | If anyone pushes back, send them to me. | Protecting someone from friction | Casual |
| 49 | Let me take that one. | Absorbing a painful task yourself | Neutral |
| 50 | I'll handle the conversation with leadership. | Shielding the team from a hard room | Neutral |
| 51 | That's on me, not on the team. | Taking responsibility in public | Neutral |
| 52 | I should have caught that earlier. | Owning your own miss | Neutral |
| 53 | One thing I'd offer — | Opening light corrective feedback | Neutral |
| 54 | Can I give you some feedback on that? | Asking permission before correcting | Neutral |
| 55 | Small thing, but worth flagging. | Low-stakes correction | Casual |
| 56 | Next time, I'd try it the other way round. | Forward-looking correction | Neutral |
| 57 | How do you think it went? | Opening any debrief | Neutral |
| 58 | What would you do differently next time? | Prompting self-assessment | Neutral |
| 59 | Let's do a quick debrief while it's fresh. | Right after a launch or review | Neutral |
| 60 | Good — let's build on that. | Closing a positive check-in | Casual |

### Tier 2 — Weekly (phrases 61–120)

The one-on-one, the structured feedback, the recognition, the trade-off conversation.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 61 | How are you doing — actually? | Opening a real one-on-one | Neutral |
| 62 | This is your time, not mine. | Framing a one-on-one properly | Neutral |
| 63 | What's on your list for today? | Letting them set the agenda | Neutral |

| 65 | What's the most interesting thing you're working on? | Career-adjacent opener | Casual |
| 66 | Where do you want to be a year from now? | Development conversation | Neutral |
| 67 | What kind of work do you want more of? | Growth question that gets answers | Neutral |
| 68 | Let's talk about what's next for you. | Career discussion opener | Neutral |
| 69 | I want to make sure you're not carrying too much. | Workload check | Neutral |

| 73 | Here's the impact it had. | Closing the SBI loop | Neutral |
| 74 | You probably couldn't see that from where you were sitting. | Softening feedback without weakening it | Neutral |
| 75 | That's not the version of you I usually see. | Naming a drop in quality kindly | Neutral |

| 77 | Help me understand what's going on. | Inviting explanation before judgment | Neutral |

| 92 | You should present this yourself. | Giving someone the stage | Neutral |
| 93 | I'll introduce it and then hand over to you. | Sharing a leadership slot | Neutral |

| 95 | I'll be in the room, but it's your call. | Backstopping without taking over | Neutral |

| 97 | If I answer this one, you'll bring me the next one too. | Naming the dependency pattern | Casual |
| 98 | Bring me a recommendation, not a question. | Raising the bar on escalations | Neutral |
| 99 | What does your gut say? | Drawing out judgment they already have | Casual |
| 100 | You know this data better than I do. | Building confidence with truth | Neutral |

| 103 | What's the assumption underneath that? | Deepening someone's analysis | Neutral |
