### Tier 3 — Situational (phrases 121–170)

Unpopular decisions, ambiguity, underperformance, blame, trust.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 121 | I wanted you to hear this from me first. | Announcing bad news to the team | High-stakes |
| 122 | The decision's been made, and I want to explain it properly. | Delivering an unpopular call | High-stakes |
| 123 | I'm not going to pretend I'm thrilled about it. | Honest framing of a decision | Neutral |
| 124 | I argued for a different outcome and I lost that one. | Being transparent about your role | High-stakes |
| 125 | Here's the reasoning as I understand it. | Explaining a decision made above you | Neutral |
| 126 | What I can tell you is this. What I can't tell you yet is that. | Managing partial information | Neutral |
| 127 | I don't have an answer for you yet, and I won't invent one. | Honesty under pressure | High-stakes |
| 128 | The moment I know, you'll know. | Committing to transparency | Neutral |
| 129 | I'd rather give you uncertainty than false comfort. | Leading through ambiguity | High-stakes |
| 130 | The work isn't wasted — here's where it goes next. | After a cancellation | Neutral |
| 131 | I know that's hard after six months on it. | Acknowledging sunk effort | Neutral |
| 132 | Take today to be annoyed. Tomorrow we'll talk about what's next. | Giving the team space to react | Casual |
| 133 | Let's separate the decision from how we respond to it. | Steadying a frustrated team | Neutral |
| 134 | I need everyone pulling in the same direction on this. | Requiring visible alignment | Neutral |
| 135 | Disagree and commit — I need the commit part now. | After an argument you lost together | Neutral |
| 136 | This is where I need you to back me publicly. | Asking for loyalty explicitly | High-stakes |
| 137 | If you have concerns, bring them to me, not to the corridor. | Shutting down side-channel grumbling | High-stakes |
| 138 | We're in a stretch where the answers keep changing. | Naming ambiguity honestly | Neutral |
| 139 | Let's focus on the part we can actually control. | Refocusing during a reorg | Neutral |
| 140 | Plan for the direction, not for the details. | Leading through change | Neutral |
| 141 | I'd like to give you an honest picture of where you stand. | Opening a serious performance talk | High-stakes |
| 142 | This isn't where I need it to be. | Stating the gap plainly | High-stakes |
| 143 | I want to be clear that this is a performance conversation. | Removing all ambiguity | High-stakes |
| 144 | Here's specifically what has to change, and by when. | Setting a performance expectation | High-stakes |
| 145 | I'll do everything I can to help you get there. | Pairing pressure with support | Neutral |
| 146 | I don't want you to be surprised by what comes next. | Warning of consequences fairly | High-stakes |
| 147 | Let's write down what we've agreed so we're both clear. | Documenting a hard conversation | Written |
| 148 | I think the role grew faster than the support we gave you. | Framing a mismatch generously | High-stakes |
| 149 | This may not be the right seat for you, and I'd rather say it out loud. | Role-fit conversation | High-stakes |
| 150 | I've thought about this a lot, and I don't think it's working. | Reaching a hard conclusion | High-stakes |
| 151 | That one's on me — I gave you a brief that wasn't clear enough. | Owning your part of a failure | Neutral |
| 152 | I set you up to fail there, and I'm sorry. | Full ownership of a bad setup | High-stakes |
| 153 | We got it wrong, and I signed it off. | Taking a failure publicly | High-stakes |
| 154 | I'm accountable for this, whatever happened underneath. | Standard leadership accountability | High-stakes |
| 155 | Let's fix it first and work out the why afterwards. | Sequencing an incident properly | Neutral |
| 156 | Nobody's looking for someone to blame here. | Setting a blameless tone | Neutral |
| 157 | I want the post-mortem to be about the process, not the person. | Framing a review | Neutral |
| 158 | I trust you with this. | Saying the quiet part out loud | Neutral |
| 159 | You've earned the benefit of the doubt. | Building trust explicitly | Neutral |
| 160 | If I'm honest with you, I expect the same back. | Establishing a two-way norm | Neutral |
| 161 | You can tell me things I don't want to hear. | Inviting hard truths | Neutral |
| 162 | Tell me where I'm wrong. | Inviting dissent directly | Casual |
| 163 | I'd rather be challenged in the room than agreed with and ignored. | Encouraging real debate | Neutral |
| 164 | What am I missing? | The humility move that works | Neutral |
| 165 | I got that wrong last quarter and I'm trying not to repeat it. | Modeling self-correction | Neutral |
| 166 | I'm going to step back on this one. | Deliberately stepping aside | Neutral |
| 167 | You don't need me in that meeting. | Removing yourself from a room | Casual |
| 168 | Take me off the copy list for these. | Reducing your own oversight | Casual |
| 169 | Run it your way — I'll step in only if you ask. | Full delegation, stated clearly | Neutral |
| 170 | My job is to make sure you don't need me. | Naming your leadership stance | Neutral |

### Tier 4 — Rare but high-impact (phrases 171–200)

You may use these four times a year. They're the ones people remember.

| # | Phrase | Use it when | Register |
|---|--------|-------------|----------|
| 171 | I've decided, and I'll own the consequences. | Closing a debate decisively | High-stakes |
| 172 | This is a call I'm going to make on my own. | Ending consultation | High-stakes |
| 173 | I hear you, and the answer's still no. | Firm refusal after genuine listening | High-stakes |
| 174 | I'm going to ask you to trust me on this one. | Asking for faith without full evidence | High-stakes |
| 175 | I can't tell you everything yet, but I'm not hiding anything from you. | Confidentiality without breaking trust | High-stakes |
| 176 | If this goes wrong, it's my name on it. | Owning risk in front of the team | High-stakes |
| 177 | I'll take the hit on that publicly. | Absorbing blame for someone's mistake | High-stakes |
| 178 | That criticism belongs to me, not to my team. | Redirecting blame upward, to yourself | High-stakes |
| 179 | I'd like to push back on how that was framed to my team. | Defending your team's dignity | High-stakes |
| 180 | I'm not going to ask my team to do that. | Refusing an unreasonable demand | High-stakes |
| 181 | That request costs us people, not just hours. | Naming the human cost of a demand | High-stakes |
| 182 | I have to protect this team's credibility on numbers. | Defending a professional standard | High-stakes |
| 183 | We can hit the date or hit the quality bar — you choose. | Forcing an executive trade-off | High-stakes |
| 184 | Let me be honest about what this is going to cost us. | Truth-telling upward | Formal |
| 185 | Here's what I'd do if it were my decision to make. | Advising where you have no authority | Neutral |
| 186 | I'm not the right person to decide this, but my recommendation is clear. | Influencing without owning | Formal |
| 187 | This team delivered under conditions I wouldn't wish on anyone. | Public recognition, high stakes | Formal |
| 188 | I want the record to show who actually did this work. | Formal credit in a review | Formal |
| 189 | She's ready for more than I can give her in this role. | Advocating for a promotion | Formal |
| 190 | I'd hire him again tomorrow. | The strongest short reference there is | Neutral |
| 191 | Losing her would cost us more than the salary difference. | Making a retention argument | High-stakes |
| 192 | I've been getting this wrong, and here's what I'm changing. | Public self-correction | High-stakes |
| 193 | I owe you an apology, and here it is. | Direct repair after damage | High-stakes |
| 194 | I let this run too long before I said something. | Owning delayed feedback | High-stakes |
| 195 | We're not doing this again — here's the guardrail. | Turning a failure into a rule | Neutral |
| 196 | I'm handing this over properly, not just walking away from it. | Structured handover | Neutral |
| 197 | From today, Priya is the decision-maker on this. | Transferring authority publicly | Formal |
| 198 | I'll be available, but I won't be involved. | A clean step-back | Neutral |
| 199 | The most useful thing I can do now is get out of the way. | Stepping back with grace | Neutral |
| 200 | If this team runs better without me in the loop, that's the result I wanted. | Framing a leadership legacy | Formal |

## 12.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

The first four patterns are the feedback structures. Learn those before anything else in this section — they carry more weight than the other eleven combined.

**Pattern 1 — Situation-Behavior-Impact (corrective):** `In [SITUATION], when [BEHAVIOR], [IMPACT]`
- In Tuesday's steering call, when the two totals didn't reconcile on screen, the room stopped trusting the rest of the slide.
- In the vendor review last week, when the SLA question got answered with an estimate, they came back asking for everything in writing.
- In yesterday's stand-up, when the pipeline failure came up on day three rather than day one, we lost two days we didn't have.
*Watch out:* The whole point is that all three parts are observable. "When you were careless" is not a behavior, it's a verdict — and the moment you use a verdict, the person defends their character instead of fixing the work.

**Pattern 2 — Situation-Behavior-Impact (positive):** `In [SITUATION], when [BEHAVIOR], [IMPACT]`
- In the dean's review, when you flagged the definition change before he asked, it saved us a correction email to forty people.
- When the extract failed on Sunday night and you rebuilt it before Monday, nobody downstream even noticed.
- In the vendor call, when you asked for the retention clause in writing, that's what gave us leverage six weeks later.
*Watch out:* Praise uses the identical structure, and most people skip the impact half. "Nice work on the extract" dies in the air. "Nobody downstream even noticed" is the part they'll repeat to their spouse.

**Pattern 3 — The "one thing I'd offer" frame:** `One thing I'd offer — [OBSERVATION]. [SUGGESTION FOR NEXT TIME]`
- One thing I'd offer — the methodology slide came before the answer. Next time lead with the number and keep the method in the appendix.
- One thing I'd offer — you apologized twice for the delay. Once is enough, and then move to the fix.
- One thing I'd offer — the caveats were all correct but there were six of them. Pick the two that would change a decision.
*Watch out:* Use this frame for one item only. Stacking three "one thing I'd offer"s in a row turns a light-touch note into an ambush, and the frame loses its power permanently with that person.

**Pattern 4 — Permission before feedback:** `Can I give you some feedback on [SPECIFIC THING]?`
- Can I give you some feedback on how the deck landed with finance?
- Can I offer a thought on how that call went?
- Mind if I share an observation about the way the escalation was worded?
*Watch out:* Ask about a specific thing, not about the person. "Can I give you some feedback?" with no object makes people's stomachs drop and they hear nothing for the next thirty seconds.

**Pattern 5 — Context before instruction:** `Here's the context: [WHY IT MATTERS]. What I need is [OUTCOME]. How you get there is up to you.`
- Here's the context: finance is challenging our conversion numbers in the board pack. What I need is a reconciliation we can defend line by line. How you do it is up to you.
- Context first: the dean wants to compare three cohorts in one view. What I need is a single chart that survives a hostile question. The design is yours.
- The background is that our vendor contract renews in March. What I need is a usage picture we can negotiate from. Pull it however you like.
*Watch out:* Keep the outcome and the method separate in two sentences. If you merge them, people hear a method instruction and stop thinking, which is exactly what you were trying to avoid.

**Pattern 6 — Coaching redirect:** `Before I answer — [QUESTION BACK TO THEM]?`
- Before I answer — what would you do if I were on leave this week?
- Before I answer that — what's your read on which of the two definitions is right?
- Let me turn that round: what have you already ruled out?
*Watch out:* You must actually wait after asking. Three seconds of silence feels like an hour, and if you fill it with your own answer you've taught them that waiting you out works.

**Pattern 7 — Check-in without micromanaging:** `Not chasing — [REASON YOU'RE ASKING]. [WHAT YOU'RE OFFERING]`
- Not chasing — I've got the dean on Thursday and I want to know what to say. Anything you need from me before then?
- I'm not chasing this. I just want to know whether the DBA has come back to you, because I can escalate if not.
- Genuinely not a nudge — is there anything I can take off you this week?
*Watch out:* Only say "not chasing" when it's true. If you say it and then ask for a delivery date in the same breath, you've simply added a lie to the chase.

**Pattern 8 — Accountability without aggression:** `We agreed [COMMITMENT] by [DATE]. Where are we?`
- We agreed the reconciliation would be with finance by Friday. Where are we on that?
- Last month we said the manual step would be gone by the end of the quarter. Where did that land?
- You were going to own the data dictionary by mid-June. Talk me through where it stands.
*Watch out:* Say "we agreed", not "you promised". The first restates a shared commitment; the second is an accusation, and the conversation becomes about tone rather than the work.

**Pattern 9 — The trade-off no:** `We can do [X], but [Y] moves. Which do you want?`
- We can build the new dashboard this month, but the migration slips to August. Which do you want?
- I can get you the placement analysis by Wednesday, but then the accreditation pack waits a week. Your call.
- Both are possible, just not both by the fifteenth. Tell me which one you'd rather have first.
*Watch out:* Always hand the choice back. If you stop at "but Y moves", you've delivered a complaint. Ending on a question turns it into a decision the other person has to make.

**Pattern 10 — The unpopular decision:** `The decision is [X]. Here's the reasoning. Here's what it means for you.`
- The project's been stopped. The reasoning is that the funding moved to accreditation work. What it means for you is that the next two weeks are about closing down cleanly, not about finding a workaround.
- We're consolidating onto one reporting platform. The reasoning is licensing cost. For this team it means the SSRS work stops at the end of the month.
- The hiring freeze holds through Q3. The reason is the enrolment shortfall. Practically, we're running with four people rather than six until October.
*Watch out:* Deliver all three parts in one go. If you give the decision and stop, people fill the silence with a worse version of the reasoning than the real one.

**Pattern 11 — Owning a failure:** `That's on me — [YOUR SPECIFIC PART]. [WHAT YOU'RE CHANGING]`
- That's on me — I approved the number without checking the denominator. From now on anything going to the board gets a second pair of eyes.
- That one's mine. I gave a brief with no success criteria in it. I'll write them down in future rather than assuming they're obvious.
- My mistake — I knew about the dependency and didn't flag it. I'm adding it to the weekly risk note.
*Watch out:* Name your actual contribution. A vague "that's on me, sorry everyone" reads as performance; a specific admission plus a change reads as leadership.

**Pattern 12 — Public credit:** `[NAME] led [SCOPE] — [SPECIFIC CONTRIBUTION]`
- Priya led the reconciliation end to end — the definition fix that closed the gap was hers.
- Sameer built the whole pipeline rewrite. I mostly stayed out of the way.
- The intake model is Anjali's work. I'd want her in the room for any questions on it.
*Watch out:* Give the scope, not just the name. "Priya helped a lot" is worth almost nothing in a leadership review; "Priya led it end to end" changes how she's seen for a year.

**Pattern 13 — Leading through ambiguity:** `What I know is [X]. What I don't know yet is [Y]. You'll hear from me by [WHEN].`
- What I know is that the restructure is happening. What I don't know is where reporting sits afterwards. You'll hear from me the day I hear anything.
- I know the budget is being cut. I don't yet know whether it touches contractor spend. I'll have an answer by the end of next week.
- Confirmed: the platform decision is made. Unconfirmed: the timeline. I'll update you Friday either way, even if the update is "still nothing".
*Watch out:* Attach a date to the third part even when you have no news to promise. "I'll update you Friday even if there's nothing" is what stops the corridor speculation.

**Pattern 14 — Stepping back:** `From here, [PERSON] owns [SCOPE]. I'll [YOUR LIMITED ROLE].`
- From here, Priya owns the vendor relationship. I'll join the quarterly review and nothing else.
- Sameer's the decision-maker on the schema now. I'll back whatever he decides.
- The dashboard roadmap is Anjali's from this month. I'll be available if she wants a sounding board.
*Watch out:* Say it in front of the people who will need to accept it, not just to the person you're promoting. Authority you hand over privately gets routed around within a week.

**Pattern 15 — Performance escalation:** `[GAP]. What has to change is [SPECIFIC]. By [DATE]. Here's the support.`
- The last three deliverables have needed rework before they could go out. What has to change is that they're checked against the definitions before I see them. I want that from this week. I'll set up a session with Sameer on the definitions so you're not doing it cold.
- Right now the turnaround is roughly double what the role needs. What has to change is the estimate coming to me upfront rather than the deadline slipping quietly. Starting Monday. I'll cut two of your standing meetings to make the room.
- The quality is fine but nothing's arriving on the agreed date. What has to change is the date holding, or a heads-up at least two days out. Over the next month. Come to me weekly and we'll look at the load together.
*Watch out:* Never end on the support sentence alone or you've softened the whole message into a nice chat. Gap, change, date, support — in that order, and don't reverse it.

## 12.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 12.1 — A one-on-one with someone whose quality has slipped

**Setting:** Your weekly thirty minutes with Ravi, a senior analyst who's been strong for two years. His last three deliverables have needed rework. This is the first time you're naming it.

**You:** How are you doing — actually? [#61]

**Ravi:** Fine. Busy. The usual.

**You:** Okay. I want to use some of today for something specific. Can I give you some feedback on the last few deliverables? [#54]

**Ravi:** Sure.

**You:** Let me tell you what I'm seeing. [#71] In Tuesday's steering call, when the two totals didn't reconcile on screen, the room stopped trusting the rest of the slide — and I had to spend ten minutes rebuilding credibility that we'd normally get for free. [#72] Same thing on the placement pack two weeks before that. The last two or three deliverables haven't been at your usual standard. [#76]

**Ravi:** I know the Tuesday one was messy. I ran it late.

**You:** That's not the version of you I usually see. [#75] So help me understand what's going on. [#77] Is something making this harder than it should be? [#78]

**Ravi:** Honestly, I'm sitting in about eleven hours of meetings a week that I don't think I need to be in. I'm doing the actual analysis after six.

**You:** That's useful, and I didn't know it. Which of those eleven hours would you drop if I said you could? [#33]

**Ravi:** The vendor sync and the two governance calls. I'm not adding anything in any of them.

**You:** Take yourself off all three from this week. I'll square it with governance — if anyone pushes back, send them to me. [#48]

**Ravi:** That would help a lot.

**You:** Good. And I want to be equally clear about the other half. Let's agree what good looks like from here [#80] — every deliverable that goes to a leadership audience gets checked against the definitions doc before it reaches me. I need to see that consistently, not just once. [#81] Let's check in on it in two weeks. [#82]

**Ravi:** That's fair.

**You:** One more thing — I'd rather tell you this now than at appraisal time. [#84] None of this should be a surprise in March. [#85]

**Why this worked:**
- The feedback was one specific situation with one specific consequence, so there was nothing to argue with and nothing to take personally.
- Asking what was making the work harder surfaced a fixable cause in under a minute — the manager owned the meeting load, which was actually his problem, not Ravi's.
- The support and the standard were delivered together. Removing the meetings without restating the bar would have read as an excuse being accepted.

### Scenario 12.2 — Telling the team a project has been cancelled

**Setting:** Six people on a call. The intake-forecasting model they've built over five months has just been stopped — the funding moved to an accreditation program. You found out forty minutes ago.

**You:** I've pulled everyone together because I wanted you to hear this from me first. [#121] The intake model has been stopped. The decision was made this morning and it's final. [#122]

**Anjali:** Stopped as in paused, or stopped as in stopped?

**You:** Stopped. The funding's been moved to the accreditation work, which has a hard external deadline in November. Here's the reasoning as I understand it [#125] — that deadline carries a regulatory risk and ours doesn't. I don't fully agree with the weighting, and I argued for a different outcome. I lost that one. [#124]

**Sameer:** Five months.

**You:** Five months, and I'm not going to pretend I'm happy about it. [#123] I know that's hard after that long on it. [#131]

**Anjali:** So we just bin it?

**You:** No. The work isn't wasted — here's where it goes. [#130] The feature pipeline and the cleaned application history become the base layer for the accreditation reporting; that's about sixty percent of what you built. Sameer, the reusable part is genuinely reusable, and I'll be saying that upward with your name on it. [#89]

**Sameer:** And the model itself?

**You:** Documented and archived properly, not abandoned mid-branch. If the funding comes back in the next cycle, whoever picks it up should be able to start where you finished. That's the fortnight's work I'm asking for.

**Anjali:** It's hard not to be annoyed.

**You:** Then be annoyed. Take today for it — tomorrow we'll talk about what's next. [#132] What I'd ask is that we separate the decision from how we respond to it. [#133] The decision's above us. How this team closes it down is entirely ours, and that part people will remember.

**Sameer:** What happens to us in the reshuffle?

**You:** Honest answer: what I know is that the accreditation work needs three analysts from January. What I don't know yet is who, or what happens to the other three roles. I won't invent an answer for you. [#127] The moment I know, you'll know [#128] — and I'll update you Friday either way, even if the update is that there's nothing.

**Why this worked:**
- Saying "I argued for a different outcome and lost" bought honesty without disloyalty — the decision still stood, and nobody was left thinking the manager was a mouthpiece.
- Naming exactly which sixty percent survives turned an abstract loss into a concrete continuation, which is the only thing that reduces the sting.
- The unknown was stated as an unknown with a date attached. That single move is the difference between a team that waits and a team that speculates.

### Scenario 12.3 — Giving credit in a leadership review

**Setting:** Quarterly review with the associate dean and two program directors. Your team closed a six-week reconciliation gap that had been embarrassing the school in board reporting. The dean is pleased and is looking at you.

**Associate Dean:** This is the first quarter the numbers have held up under questioning. Whatever you did, it worked.

**You:** Thank you — I'll pass that on, because the team did the heavy lifting on it. [#88] To be specific: Priya led the reconciliation end to end. [#87] The insight that closed the gap was hers — we were counting deferred applicants in one system and not the other, and she found it by reading four years of admission policy nobody had opened since 2021.

**Program Director:** That's the deferral issue we've been arguing about since March?

**You:** The same one. And I want to call out Sameer's work on the pipeline side [#89] — the reason the fix has held for six weeks rather than breaking on the next load is that he rebuilt the ingestion to fail loudly instead of silently.

**Associate Dean:** Good. And your part?

**You:** Mostly staying out of the way, honestly. Credit for that one sits with the team, not with me. [#90] Where I did add something was getting the deferral definition ratified by registry, which took three meetings that Priya shouldn't have had to sit in.

**Associate Dean:** Well, pass on the thanks.

**You:** I'd rather do better than that, if I may. Priya's presenting the methodology at the data governance forum next month — she should present this here too, next quarter. [#92] I'll introduce it and hand over to her. [#93]

**Associate Dean:** Fine by me.

**You:** One thing for the record while we're here: this team delivered that under a hiring freeze, two people down. [#187] I want the record to show who actually did the work. [#188] Priya's ready for more than I can give her at her current level [#189] — I'll come to you separately about that.

**Associate Dean:** Send me something before the next cycle.

**Why this worked:**
- Credit was specific and scoped — "led it end to end" plus the actual insight, which is what makes a name stick in a senior person's memory.
- Naming his own contribution honestly (and modestly) kept it credible. Total self-erasure reads as false and makes the praise sound scripted.
- The credit was converted into something durable: a presenting slot and an opened promotion conversation, rather than warm words that evaporate by Friday.

### Scenario 12.4 — Coaching an analyst who keeps asking you for the answer

**Setting:** Nikhil, six months in, bright, and at your desk for the third time this week with a question he can answer himself. You have a full afternoon.

**Nikhil:** Quick one — for the retention chart, should I use the cohort start date or the enrolment date?

**You:** Before I answer — what's your read on it? [#23]

**Nikhil:** I mean, I could see it either way. That's why I'm asking.

**You:** Fair. So talk me through your thinking. [#25] What have you already ruled out? [#26]

**Nikhil:** Well, enrolment date is cleaner in the data. Fewer nulls. But cohort start is what the program directors talk about in meetings.

**You:** Keep going. What's the assumption underneath choosing the cleaner field? [#103]

**Nikhil:** That data quality matters more than matching how people think about it. Which... probably isn't right for a chart that goes to program directors.

**You:** So which one would you go with? [#28]

**Nikhil:** Cohort start. And I'd footnote the null rate so nobody's surprised.

**You:** That's exactly what I'd have done. [#46] Now — notice what just happened. You had the answer before you walked over. [#100]

**Nikhil:** I wanted to check.

**You:** I know, and I'd rather you check than guess on something that matters. But here's the thing: if I answer this one, you'll bring me the next one too [#97], and in six months you'll still be walking over here for calls you're better placed to make than I am. You're closer to this data than anyone in the building. [#101]

**Nikhil:** So don't ask?

**You:** Ask — but bring me a recommendation, not a question. [#98] "I'm going with cohort start because of X, tell me if you disagree" takes me ten seconds and it's yours if I say nothing. That's a completely different conversation from "which one should I use".

**Nikhil:** And if I'm wrong?

**You:** Then you'll be wrong on something reversible, which is how this works. What's the worst case if you pick the wrong date field on a chart? [#105]

**Nikhil:** We redo the chart.

**You:** Right. You don't need my permission for that. [#107]

**Why this worked:**
- Three coaching questions in a row got him to his own answer in under ninety seconds — faster than explaining it would have taken.
- The pattern was named out loud rather than tolerated. Without that, the same conversation happens weekly for a year.
- He was given a concrete replacement behavior — recommendation instead of question — so "stop asking me" didn't land as "stop talking to me".

### Scenario 12.5 — Saying no on behalf of the team

**Setting:** A program director wants a new weekly dashboard built in eight days. Your team is mid-migration with a fixed cutover date. Two of your five people are already working late.

**Program Director:** I need a weekly applicant-quality dashboard by the twenty-sixth. It's for the strategy offsite.

**You:** Let me make sure I've got the shape of it — weekly refresh, applicant quality, live for the offsite on the twenty-sixth. Is that right?

**Program Director:** That's it.

**You:** Then I'll be straight with you [#83] — we can't take that on right now. [#115] Not in that window.

**Program Director:** It's one dashboard.

**You:** It's one dashboard on top of a migration with a cutover date that finance and registry are both planning around. We can do it, but something else moves. [#117] If you want it by the twenty-sixth, the cutover slips to September and I'd need you in the room when I tell registry.

**Program Director:** That's not really an option.

**You:** Agreed. So here's what I'd offer instead. Two of my five are already working past seven most nights, and I'm going to protect the team's focus on the migration [#116] because if that goes wrong it costs us a quarter, not a week. What I can get you by the twenty-sixth is a static cut of the applicant-quality numbers — same figures, no live refresh, about a day's work. The live version lands three weeks after cutover.

**Program Director:** The offsite would probably survive a static version.

**You:** Then let's do that. And I'd rather do one of these properly than both badly. [#119] Give me until tomorrow and I'll come back with what's realistic on the live one. [#120]

**Program Director:** Can your team not just push a bit harder for two weeks?

**You:** I'm not going to ask them to. [#180] That request costs us people, not just hours [#181] — I've lost two analysts to that exact pattern before and replacing one takes four months. If it becomes a genuine priority above the migration, tell me and I'll re-plan the whole thing openly. But I won't quietly absorb it into evenings.

**Program Director:** Understood. Static version it is.

**Why this worked:**
- The no came with a trade-off and a decision handed back, so it read as planning rather than obstruction.
- A real alternative arrived within thirty seconds of the refusal, which is what stops "no" from becoming a relationship problem.
- The human-cost argument was made with a number attached — four months to replace someone beats any abstract appeal to team wellbeing.

## 12.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| Good job. | Nobody downstream even noticed the failure — that's because of how you rebuilt it. | Names the impact, so the praise is worth keeping. |
| Do the reconciliation by Friday. | Finance is challenging our numbers in the board pack, so I need the reconciliation clean by Friday. | Context lets them catch what you forgot to ask for. |
| Kindly improve the quality of your work. | The last two deliverables haven't been at your usual standard — let me show you what I mean. | Specific, dated, and answerable instead of a character note. |
| You are careless in your analysis. | When the totals didn't reconcile on screen, the room stopped trusting the slide. | Behavior and impact, not a verdict on the person. |
| Any update? | Not chasing — I've got the dean Thursday and want to know what to say. | Explains why you're asking, so it isn't a nudge. |
| Why is this not done? | We agreed this would be with finance by Friday. Where are we? | Restates a shared commitment instead of accusing. |
| I am not satisfied with your performance. | This isn't where I need it to be, and I want to be specific about why. | Points at the work and promises detail. |
| This is my team's mistake. | We got it wrong, and I signed it off. | Leaders absorb blame outward and hand credit inward. |
| Management has decided, so we have to do it. | The decision's been made and I want to explain the reasoning properly. | Keeps you a leader rather than a messenger. |
| I also did not want this to happen. | I argued for a different outcome and I lost that one. | Honest about your role without undermining the call. |
| Don't worry, everything will be fine. | I'd rather give you uncertainty than false comfort. | Reassurance you can't back up destroys trust later. |
| It is not possible in eight days. | We can do it, but the cutover moves to September. Which do you want? | Turns a refusal into a decision they must make. |
| My team is already overloaded. | That request costs us people, not just hours. | Makes an abstract complaint concrete and hard to wave off. |
| Please do it as I told you. | The outcome I need is X — how you get there is up to you. | Buys their thinking rather than their compliance. |
| You should have informed me earlier. | I'd rather hear this kind of thing early than late. | Builds the habit instead of punishing the report. |
| Everyone worked hard on this. | Priya led it end to end — the definition fix was hers. | Named, scoped credit is the only credit that travels. |
| I will let you know. | I'll update you Friday, even if the update is that there's nothing. | A date stops speculation better than a promise does. |
| As per me, you need to change your approach. | One thing I'd offer — lead with the number and keep the method in the appendix. | Light frame, single item, concrete next step. |
| Do you have any doubts? | What's still unclear? | "Doubts" reads as suspicion to native ears. |
| I want a status update on everything. | Where are you with the migration, and what's in your way? | Two questions beat a demand for a report. |
| Sorry to disturb, just checking. | Quick one — anything you need from me on this? | Drops the apology and offers something instead. |
| That is not my responsibility. | I'm not the right person to decide this, but my recommendation is clear. | Declines ownership while still adding value. |
| You must trust me on this. | I'm going to ask you to trust me on this one. | An ask, not an order — and people grant asks. |
| I have given him full freedom. | From today, Sameer is the decision-maker on the schema. | Transfers real authority in words others will honor. |
| Let us discuss your performance in the appraisal. | I'd rather tell you now than at appraisal time. | Feedback delayed to March is feedback wasted. |

## 12.6 Practice Drills

**Drill 1 — The 10× rep**

Anchor phrase: **"One thing I'd offer —"** The tone target is level and unhurried, with a small pause after "offer". Say each twice. If your voice rises at the end, you're asking permission; if it drops hard, you're delivering a verdict. Aim for flat.

1. One thing I'd offer — lead with the number next time.
2. One thing I'd offer — the caveats were correct, but there were six of them.
3. One thing I'd offer — you apologized twice; once is plenty.
4. One thing I'd offer — the methodology belongs in the appendix.
5. One thing I'd offer — send it the night before rather than in the room.
6. One thing I'd offer — say "I'll confirm" instead of guessing the figure.
7. One thing I'd offer — the chart had four messages in it, and it needed one.
8. One thing I'd offer — you went to the vendor before you came to me.
9. One thing I'd offer — the email was three paragraphs where two lines would do.
10. One thing I'd offer — you had the answer already; back yourself next time.

**Drill 2 — Slot swap**

Pattern: `In [SITUATION], when [BEHAVIOR], [IMPACT]`

Speak all eight as complete sentences, out loud, no notes. Force yourself to make the impact half the longest part.

1. Tuesday's steering call / the totals didn't reconcile on screen / the room stopped trusting the slide
2. the vendor review / the SLA question got an estimate rather than a number / they asked for everything in writing after that
3. the dean's session / you flagged the definition change before he asked / we avoided a correction email to forty people
4. Monday's stand-up / the pipeline failure came up on day three / we lost two days we didn't have
5. the accreditation walkthrough / you took the hostile question calmly / the panel moved on in thirty seconds
6. last week's handover / the runbook wasn't updated / Sameer spent a Sunday reverse-engineering it
7. the offsite / you pushed back on the enrolment assumption / the target got revised before it was published
8. the migration cutover / you called the go/no-go early / registry had a full day to prepare rather than an hour

**Drill 3 — Cold open**

One sentence only, out loud, within three seconds of reading each prompt.

1. Your best analyst has just delivered the third late deliverable in a row and is standing in front of you.
2. The dean says "great work" about something you had almost nothing to do with, in front of eight people.
3. A junior analyst asks you a question for the fourth time this week that she can answer herself.
4. You have to tell six people that the project they've built for five months is cancelled, starting with the first sentence.
5. A director asks whether the team can "push a bit harder for a couple of weeks" to absorb unplanned work.

**Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt**

```
You are Ravi Menon, a 34-year-old senior data analyst at a business school
in Hyderabad. You have been in the team two years and were, until recently,
the strongest person on it. Over the last two months your output quality has
dropped: two deliverables went out with reconciliation errors and one was
three days late. You know it, and you're defensive about it, because the
real cause is that you're sitting in eleven hours a week of meetings you
consider useless and doing your actual analysis after 6pm. You have not
said this to anyone. You are also quietly wondering whether you should be
at a bigger company by now.

I am your manager. This is our weekly one-on-one, thirty minutes, just the
two of us on a video call.

Run the conversation in character, one turn at a time. Do the following:
- Open guarded. Answer "how are you doing" with "fine, busy" and nothing more.
- If I give vague feedback ("quality needs to improve"), agree blandly and
  give me nothing. Only open up if I name a specific situation and its impact.
- Push back once on whether the errors were really my fault.
- Do not volunteer the meeting-load problem unless I ask an open question
  about what's making the work harder.
- If I offer sympathy without restating a standard, relax and treat the
  conversation as over.
- Near the end, ask me directly whether this is going to affect my rating.

Stay in character until I write "END SCENE".

After END SCENE, drop character and give me feedback on:
1. Whether my feedback was situation-behavior-impact or a character judgment,
   quoting the exact line where it tipped either way.
2. How long it took me to ask an open diagnostic question, and what I asked
   before that which I should have skipped.
3. Whether I held the standard after offering support, or let the support
   replace the standard.
4. How I answered the rating question — evasive, brutal, or honest and calm.
5. Two phrasings I used that sounded either too soft to register or too blunt
   for a two-year relationship, with a better version of each.
```

## 12.7 Master these 10 first

1. **Let me give you the context first.** [#2] — Buys thinking, not just compliance.
2. **The outcome I care about is X.** [#3] — Separates the goal from the method.
3. **Not chasing — just want to know if you need anything.** [#41] — Check in without micromanaging.
4. **One thing I'd offer —** [#53] — Lightest reliable frame for correction.
5. **In [situation], when [behavior], [impact].** [#72] — The feedback structure that never fails.
6. **Help me understand what's going on.** [#77] — Finds the cause before judging.
7. **Bring me a recommendation, not a question.** [#98] — Ends dependency without ending access.
8. **We can do it, but something else moves.** [#117] — Converts refusal into their decision.
9. **Priya led this end to end.** [#87] — Credit that actually travels upward.
10. **That's on me, not on the team.** [#51] — Blame outward, credit inward, always.


---

# Chapter 13 — Executive Communication

> **One-line promise:** You'll be able to give a senior person the answer, the "so what" and the ask inside two minutes — and hold your ground when they interrupt, challenge or ask something you can't answer.

## 13.1 Why this chapter matters

- Executives don't read your work — they read your first sentence. A dean, a VP or a steering committee chair arrives with no context, five minutes, and three other problems already in their head. If your first sentence is background, you've spent your entire budget before you said anything.
- The dominant non-native failure here is chronological order. "So, in April we started the migration, then the vendor changed the API, then we had a data issue in June, and so now…" is how the work actually happened, and it is exactly the wrong order to say it in. Senior people want the ending first and the plot only if they ask.
- The second failure is the missing ask. Long, competent, well-evidenced updates that end with nothing — no decision requested, no approval sought, no "here's what I need from you". The executive nods, the meeting ends, nothing moves, and you conclude that leadership doesn't listen. They listened; you never asked.
- Politeness inflation makes it worse. "I was just wondering whether it might perhaps be possible to consider approving the budget" reads to a senior ear as someone who doesn't believe their own case. The confident version is shorter and flatter: "The ask is eighteen lakh over two quarters, and here's what it buys."
- Being interrupted is not rudeness and not failure — it's the format. When someone cuts you off on slide two and says "what's the bottom line", the correct response takes four seconds and contains no apology. Most people spend twenty seconds recovering, and that's what actually costs them credibility.
- Knowing when to stop is the rarest skill in this chapter. The update that ends at ninety seconds with "that's it from me" reads as command of the material. The same update padded to four minutes reads as someone who wasn't sure which part mattered.
