| 195 | A chicken-and-egg problem | Each side needs the other first | Neutral |
| 196 | A needle in a haystack | Nearly impossible to find | Neutral |
| 197 | Bells and whistles | Non-essential extra features | Neutral |
| 198 | Nuts and bolts | The practical mechanical details | Neutral |
| 199 | Money left on the table | Value available but not captured | Neutral |
| 200 | A rounding error | So small it doesn't affect the total | Neutral |

> **Caution — Tier 4.** These are the highest-risk idioms for a non-native speaker, not because they're rude but because a small slip is very audible. **Fall on your sword** and **the buck stops here** are near-theatrical — using either casually makes an ordinary problem sound like a resignation. The cricket idioms (**176**, and "knocked for six", which didn't make this list) are invisible to Americans. **Circle the wagons** carries an American-frontier image that some colleagues find dated. Understand all thirty. Say maybe four: *herding cats*, *ducks in a row*, *garbage in, garbage out*, and *chicken-and-egg* are safe everywhere and genuinely useful in data work.

## 16.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `We're {IN THE WEEDS / ON TRACK / DOWN TO THE WIRE} on {DELIVERABLE}.`
- We're a bit in the weeds on the enrollment reconciliation logic.
- We're on track for the Thursday dashboard release.
- We're down to the wire on the accreditation data pull.
*Watch out:* It's "on" the deliverable, not "about" or "with". "In the weeds about the join" is a giveaway.

**Pattern 2:** `Let's {PARK IT / TAKE IT OFFLINE / PUT A PIN IN IT} and {NEXT ACTION}.`
- Let's take it offline and I'll walk you through the schema after.
- Let's park it and pick it up once Finance confirms the headcount.
- Let's put a pin in the model choice and come back after the data audit.
*Watch out:* Always attach the next action. "Let's take it offline" alone sounds like you're shutting the person down.

**Pattern 3:** `{THING} is a bit of a {NEGATIVE NOUN IDIOM}.`
- That legacy ETL job is a bit of a house of cards.
- The vendor's pricing model is a bit of a black box.
- Getting all four departments to agree is a bit of a herding-cats exercise.
*Watch out:* "A bit of a" is the softener that makes criticism survivable. Drop it and "that pipeline is a house of cards" becomes an accusation.

**Pattern 4:** `Just a heads-up — {FACT}. {IMPLICATION}.`
- Just a heads-up — the source system is down till noon. The refresh will be late.
- Just a heads-up — Priya's on leave Friday. She's the only one with prod access.
- Just a heads-up — the dean asked for the numbers a week early.
*Watch out:* A heads-up is information, not a request. If you want something, add the ask explicitly; otherwise people file it and do nothing.

**Pattern 5:** `Can you {LOOP ME IN / KEEP ME POSTED / RUN IT BY ME} {WHEN / BEFORE}?`
- Can you loop me in when the vendor replies?
- Can you keep me posted if the load time gets worse?
- Can you run it by me before it goes to the leadership deck?
*Watch out:* "Loop me in" needs a trigger event; "keep me posted" needs a topic. Bare "keep me posted" with no subject sounds vague and gets ignored.

**Pattern 6:** `That's {ABOVE MY PAY GRADE / IN OUR WHEELHOUSE / NOT MY CALL}, but {WHAT YOU CAN DO}.`
- That's above my pay grade, but I can get you the numbers behind it by tomorrow.
- Pipeline work is squarely in our wheelhouse — the survey design isn't.
- That's not my call, but I'd flag that we'd need two more weeks.
*Watch out:* Never end on the refusal. Without the "but" clause, "above my pay grade" reads as ducking.

**Pattern 7:** `The {RED FLAG / ELEPHANT IN THE ROOM / REAL BOTTLENECK} here is {X}.`
- The red flag for me is that the row count dropped twelve percent overnight.
- The elephant in the room is that nobody owns this dataset.
- The real bottleneck here is the approval step, not the query.
*Watch out:* Singular with "the", plural without: "the red flag is X" but "I'm seeing red flags in the vendor's numbers." Mixing them sounds off.

**Pattern 8:** `{IDIOM}, {so to speak / for want of a better phrase / if that makes sense}.`
- We've been putting out fires all week, so to speak.
- It's a bit of a chicken-and-egg problem, for want of a better phrase.
- We're spinning our wheels on this one, if that makes sense.
*Watch out:* Use this hedge once or twice, not habitually. Constant hedging signals you don't trust your own English, which invites people to talk over you.

**Pattern 9:** `Not exactly {POSITIVE IDIOM}. / Hardly {POSITIVE IDIOM}.`
- Migrating fourteen years of history isn't exactly low-hanging fruit.
- A three-week turnaround is hardly a slam dunk.
- This isn't a quick win — it's a quarter of work.
*Watch out:* Negating an idiom is the most natural-sounding move in this chapter and almost nobody learns it. It also lets you decline gracefully without saying no.

**Pattern 10:** `{PERSON} {THREW ME UNDER THE BUS / HAS MY BACK / WENT OVER MY HEAD}.`
- I don't want to throw anyone under the bus, but the spec changed twice.
- Ravi's had my back on this since day one.
- She went over my head to the associate dean, which I only found out afterwards.
*Watch out:* These split around the object: *throw me under the bus*, never "throw under the bus me". And the disclaimer "I don't want to throw anyone under the bus, but…" is what you say immediately before doing exactly that — everyone knows this, so use it only when you genuinely name a process, not a person.

**Pattern 11:** `We can {BITE THE BULLET AND X} or {KICK THE CAN AND Y}.`
- We can bite the bullet and rebuild the model, or patch it and revisit in Q3.
- We either draw a line in the sand on scope now, or we accept a March release.
- We can hedge our bets and run both, but that doubles the compute cost.
*Watch out:* Present both branches with costs attached. An idiom without a number behind it sounds like an opinion, not an analysis.

**Pattern 12:** `Before we {PULL THE TRIGGER / GO LIVE}, let's {SANITY-CHECK / KICK THE TIRES ON} {X}.`
- Before we pull the trigger on the vendor, let's kick the tires on their API limits.
- Before this goes to the dean, let's sanity-check the denominator.
- Before we sign off, I want one more apples-to-apples comparison against last year.
*Watch out:* "Sanity check" is a verb and a noun — "let's sanity-check it" and "let's do a sanity check on it" are both correct. "Let's sanity it" is not.

**Pattern 13:** `It's {PAR FOR THE COURSE / a first} for {CONTEXT}.`
- Late survey responses are par for the course in week one.
- Vendor slippage in December is par for the course, unfortunately.
- Three schema changes mid-sprint is not par for the course — that's new.
*Watch out:* "Par for the course" is resigned, not neutral. Saying it about your own team's misses signals you've accepted them, which is not the impression you want in a review.

**Pattern 14:** `I'd rather {ERR ON THE SIDE OF X} than {RISK Y}.`
- I'd rather err on the side of caution and hold the release a day.
- I'd rather over-communicate than have Finance find this in the board pack.
- I'd rather be honest about the confidence interval than oversell the model.
*Watch out:* "Err" rhymes with "her" in US English and with "air" in UK English — both are accepted, so pick one and stay consistent rather than switching mid-sentence.

## 16.4 Scenarios — see the idioms working

### Scenario 16.1 — The release that's slipping

**Setting:** Wednesday stand-up. The program dashboard was meant to ship Friday. The upstream admissions feed changed shape without warning.

**You:** Quick heads-up before we go round the room [#9] — the admissions feed changed schema overnight, so the Friday release is at risk.

**Engineering lead (US):** How bad? Are we down to the wire [#43] or are we already past it?

**You:** Down to the wire, not past it. Two of the six tables are fine. The other four need remapping, and that's the heavy lifting [#33].

**Engineering lead:** What's the actual bottleneck [#21] — the mapping, or getting someone to confirm the new definitions?

**You:** Honestly, the confirmation. The remapping is maybe a day. Getting the registry team to sign off on [#28] what "active enrollment" now means could take three.

**Product manager:** Can we ship the two clean tables Friday and follow with the rest?

**You:** We could. It's not a quick win [#30] though — we'd need a banner on the dashboard saying four metrics are stale, and that's the part leadership actually looks at.

**Engineering lead:** So it's a judgment call [#134]. Ship partial with a caveat, or hold everything a week.

**You:** That's how I'd frame it. My gut feel [#39] is hold. A dashboard people half-trust is worse than one that's late — and I'd rather err on the side of caution [#130] with the dean's numbers.

**Product manager:** Fine. Let's take the definitions question offline [#6] with the registry team — I'll set up thirty minutes today. Everything else stays on track [#20].

**You:** Works for me. I'll keep you posted [#47] as the remapping lands, and I'll loop in [#4] Priya since she owns the semantic layer.

**Why this worked:**
- Every idiom carried information, not decoration. "Down to the wire, not past it" is a precise status in five words.
- The negation move — "it's *not* a quick win" [Pattern 9] — declined the easy option without ever saying no.
- Naming the real bottleneck as a *person-approval* problem rather than a *technical* problem reframed the whole conversation in one sentence.

### Scenario 16.2 — Vendor escalation

**Setting:** Call with a data vendor whose nightly file has arrived late four times this month. Their account manager is on; so is their delivery lead.

**You:** Thanks for making time. I want to cut to the chase [#100] — we've had four late files in three weeks, and it's starting to look less like bad luck and more like a pattern.

**Account manager:** We're aware, and we're on it. There have been some teething problems [#180] since the platform migration.

**You:** I appreciate the candour. Here's my concern: those late files are a canary in the coal mine [#138] for us. If the SLA slips in a quiet month, I don't want to find out what happens during admissions season.

**Delivery lead:** Fair. The root cause is a queueing job that doesn't scale past a certain volume. We've got a workaround [#115] running now.

**You:** Is that a workaround or a band-aid fix [#116]? I'm asking seriously — I need to know what to tell my leadership.

**Delivery lead:** Honestly, it's a band-aid. The permanent fix is in the pipeline [#53] for next quarter.

**You:** That's useful, and I'd rather have that than a polished answer. So let me draw a line in the sand [#133] on our side: from the first of next month, we need the file by 04:00 or an automated alert by 04:15. Not a fix by then — just visibility.

**Account manager:** That we can commit to.

**You:** Good. Then the ball's in your court [#66] on the alerting, and I'll hold off on escalating internally. Can you close the loop [#61] with me in writing by Friday?

**Account manager:** You'll have it Thursday.

**Why this worked:**
- "Canary in the coal mine" did work no plain sentence could: it made a small recurring annoyance into a forward-looking risk without any accusation.
- Challenging "workaround" versus "band-aid fix" forced an honest answer by offering the vendor a socially acceptable way to admit the truth.
- The ask was deliberately smaller than the fix — visibility, not resolution — which is why it got agreed on the call.

### Scenario 16.3 — Leadership review of a model

**Setting:** Quarterly review with the associate dean. You're presenting a student-attrition risk model. She is smart, busy, and not technical.

**Associate dean:** Give me the big picture [#14] first. Does this thing work?

**You:** In a nutshell [#103] — yes, with a caveat I want to be upfront about. It correctly flags about seven in ten students who eventually withdraw. It also flags some who don't.

**Associate dean:** How many false alarms?

**You:** Roughly one in four flags is a false positive. That's not a rounding error [#200], so I don't want to gloss over it.

**Associate dean:** What's the practical implication?

**You:** It depends entirely on what we do with a flag. If a flag means a conversation with an advisor, a false positive costs twenty minutes. If a flag means a note on a student's record, the calculation changes completely.

**Associate dean:** So the model isn't really the decision.

**You:** Exactly. The model is the easy part — the intervention design is the heavy lifting [#33]. And I'd flag [#86] one more thing: this model is only as good as the attendance data feeding it. Garbage in, garbage out [#193] is not a cliché here, it's the main risk.

**Associate dean:** Is the attendance data reliable?

**You:** In two departments, yes. In the other three, it's held together with duct tape [#143] — manual uploads, inconsistent timing. I'd want to fix that before we scale this.

**Associate dean:** So what are you asking me for?

**You:** A go/no-go [#75] on a pilot in the two clean departments this term. Not a full rollout. If it holds up, we come back in March with real numbers rather than a promise.

**Associate dean:** Do that. Keep it tight and keep me posted [#47].

**Why this worked:**
- Volunteering the weakness before being asked — "that's not a rounding error, so I don't want to gloss over it" — bought credibility for everything that followed.
- "Garbage in, garbage out" landed a technical dependency on a non-technical stakeholder in four words, and the follow-up sentence stopped it sounding glib.
- The close converted an open-ended review into a specific, small, reversible decision. Idioms framed it; the scope did the work.

### Scenario 16.4 — Corridor conversation, London office

**Setting:** Coffee area at a partner institution in London. You're chatting with a counterpart, Ellie, two days after a reorg announcement.

**Ellie:** So — how are you finding all this? Bit of a week.

**You:** Slightly on the back foot [#175], if I'm honest. We found out about the reporting change the same morning everyone else did.

**Ellie:** Same here. The whole analytics function got moved under Finance and nobody was consulted. Classic.

**You:** Is your team out of the loop [#5] on what happens to the existing roadmap, or has someone said something?

**Ellie:** Officially, "no change". Unofficially, the writing's on the wall [#157] for at least two of the reporting projects.

**You:** That's a shame. The student-journey one was genuinely good work.

**Ellie:** It was. Between us, I think it became a bit of a political football [#191] — everyone wanted their name on it, nobody wanted to fund it.

**You:** I've seen that film. Are you going to push back [#26] on the roadmap cuts, or pick your battles [#150]?

**Ellie:** Pick my battles, definitely. I'll keep my powder dry [#186] until the new director's actually in the chair. No point rocking the boat [#152] with someone who isn't there yet.

**You:** Sensible. If it's useful, our team's been through the same restructure two years ago — happy to talk shop [#64] properly over lunch sometime rather than in a coffee queue.

**Ellie:** I'd like that. Ping me [#51] a couple of dates.

**Why this worked:**
- The register dropped correctly for a corridor: contractions, short turns, no framing language. Idioms carry most of the meaning because nobody is spelling anything out.
- British-flavoured idioms — *on the back foot*, *keep my powder dry* — were matched, not imported. You used one; you let her use the rest.
- The close converted informal sympathy into a concrete, low-pressure offer, which is how professional relationships actually get built.

## 16.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too blunt / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| Kindly do the needful. | Can you take it from here? | The first is dead business English; the second is what people actually say. |
| Please revert back to me. | Get back to me when you can. | "Revert" means "return to a previous state" to a native ear. |
| It is raining cats and dogs. | (say nothing about weather) | Textbook idiom nobody has said in decades; it marks you instantly. |
| Let us not beat a dead horse. | I think we've covered this — let's park it. [#87] | The plainer version is current and less aggressive. |
| We are facing so many problems. | There are a lot of moving parts on this one. [#32] | Names complexity without sounding like you're drowning. |
| This is very difficult work. | This is where the heavy lifting is. [#33] | Signals effort as valuable rather than as complaint. |
| I do not know the exact number. | Off the top of my head, around forty thousand. [#56] | Gives them something usable and flags the uncertainty. |
| The project will be late. | We're down to the wire on this. [#43] | Same information, but it reads as urgency rather than surrender. |
| I have too much work. | I don't have the bandwidth this week. [#10] | Capacity language is neutral; "too much work" sounds personal. |
| You did not tell me about this. | I think I was out of the loop on that one. [#5] | Removes the accusation while still making the point. |
| He made a mistake and it caused this. | Something fell through the cracks between us. [#113] | Names the process gap instead of a person. |
| That is wrong. | That's a red flag for me — can we check the denominator? [#15] | Turns a verdict into an investigation. |
| Your requirements keep changing. | The goalposts have moved a couple of times on this. [#67] | Idiom depersonalizes what would otherwise be a complaint. |
| We must finish everything. | Let's not try to boil the ocean — pick the top three. [#110] | Redirects scope without refusing anything. |
| I want to explain everything in detail. | I'll keep it high level and we can deep-dive after. [#7] | Respects the room's time and offers the detail as an option. |
| We should study this deeply first. | Let's kick the tires on it before we commit. [#140] | Lighter, faster, and doesn't sound like a delay tactic. |
| This decision is not mine. | That's above my pay grade, but here's what I'd flag. [#118] | Declines the decision while still adding value. |
| I am not sure, maybe yes maybe no. | I'm genuinely on the fence — here's what would tip me. [#72] | Turns indecision into a request for the missing input. |
| Please do it fast. | This one's crunch time — can you drop everything else today? [#42, #45] | Explicit about the trade-off instead of just applying pressure. |
| We will discuss later. | Let's take it offline and I'll set up thirty minutes. [#6] | Attaching the next action stops it sounding like a brush-off. |
| Everything is fine now. | We're not out of the woods yet, but the worst is behind us. [#148] | Honest optimism reads as more credible than blanket reassurance. |
| It is a very old system with many problems. | Frankly, it's held together with duct tape. [#143] | Vivid, memorable, and gets budget conversations started. |
| This solution will fix all issues. | There's no silver bullet here, but this closes the biggest gap. [#112] | Pre-empts the objection everyone in the room is already forming. |
| Sorry to disturb you. | Quick one — do you have two minutes? | "Disturb" is over-apologetic; the second is how colleagues actually open. |
| As per our discussion, please find attached. | Following up on our chat — the file's attached. | Modern written register; the first belongs in a 1998 fax. |

## 16.6 Practice Drills

### Drill 1 — The 10× rep

Anchor idiom: **"down to the wire" [#43]**. Say each of these aloud, twice. The goal is for the phrase to arrive without you assembling it.

1. We're down to the wire on the Friday release.
2. It's going to be down to the wire, but we'll make it.
3. We went down to the wire last quarter and I'd rather not repeat it.
4. If the vendor file slips again, we're down to the wire.
5. This is down to the wire territory now.
6. I don't want to be down to the wire on the dean's numbers.
7. Every accreditation cycle comes down to the wire.
8. We're down to the wire, so I'm cutting scope rather than quality.
9. It came right down to the wire — the last table landed at 4:50.
10. We're not down to the wire yet, but we will be by Wednesday.

### Drill 2 — Slot swap

Pattern: `Let's not {OVERREACH IDIOM} — let's {NARROW ACTION} first.`

Speak all eight:
1. Let's not boil the ocean — let's fix the two dashboards people actually open.
2. Let's not reinvent the wheel — let's see what the Finance team already built.
3. Let's not go down a rabbit hole on attribution — let's get the totals right first.
4. Let's not move the goalposts mid-sprint — let's log it for the next cycle.
5. Let's not bite off more than we can chew — let's pilot in two departments.
6. Let's not put a band-aid on this — let's find the actual root cause.
7. Let's not spin our wheels on the naming convention — let's ship and rename later.
8. Let's not gold-plate the pilot — let's get something in front of users this month.

### Drill 3 — Cold open

Respond aloud in exactly one sentence, using at least one idiom from the ladder. No preamble.

1. Your director asks in a corridor: "Is the migration going to be a problem?"
2. A US colleague says on a call: "Can you take the lead on the reconciliation piece?" — you can't, you're at capacity.
3. In a review, someone says: "Why didn't we catch this earlier?"
4. A vendor asks: "Are you happy to proceed to contract this week?" — you're not.
5. A junior analyst says: "The numbers look wrong but I can't work out why."

### Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt

```
You are Maria Kowalski, a 52-year-old operations director at a European
university system, based in Warsaw. English is your third language and you
speak it precisely but without idiom — you learned it from documents, not
from offices. You have been dealing with British and American partners for
fifteen years and you find their idiomatic English genuinely irritating.

I am a data lead at a partner institution. We are on a 25-minute video call
about a joint reporting project that is running three weeks late.

Run the conversation in character, one turn at a time. Do the following:
- Whenever I use an idiom, either ask me flatly what it means, or —
  occasionally — misinterpret it literally and act on the wrong meaning.
  ("Park it? We are not parking anything. Where would we park it?")
- Do not soften this. You are not being difficult; you genuinely do not
  follow, and you have stopped pretending to.
- Use exactly two idioms yourself, both slightly wrong ("we are on the
  same book", "this is the elephant in the corner"). Do not acknowledge
  the error.
- Push hard on the timeline. Ask twice for a specific date.
- If I explain an idiom clearly and then restate the point in plain
  English, warm up noticeably and become collaborative.
- Near the end, ask me to send a written summary — and say you would
  prefer it "without the expressions".

Stay in character until I write "END SCENE".

After END SCENE, drop character and give me feedback on:
1. Every idiom I used, and whether it survived translation to a non-native
   listener or created friction. Quote the line.
2. Which idioms I could have replaced with a plain phrase at zero cost,
   and which ones genuinely carried meaning nothing else would.
3. Whether I explained an idiom when challenged, or just repeated it
   louder.
4. How my register changed once you warmed up — did I over-correct into
   stiff, formal English?
5. Three sentences from the call rewritten so they'd work equally well
   with a native speaker in Chicago and with you.
```

## 16.7 Master these 10 first

1. **On the same page** [#1] — Fastest way to confirm alignment.
2. **A heads-up** [#9] — Warns people without creating alarm.
3. **In the weeds** [#16] — Names over-detail without insulting anyone.
4. **A red flag** [#15] — Raises risk with no accusation attached.
5. **The heavy lifting** [#33] — Makes hard work visible and valued.
6. **Down to the wire** [#43] — Precise, non-panicked deadline status.
7. **Take it offline** [#6] — Ends a derailing thread politely.
8. **Fall through the cracks** [#113] — Blames the process, never the person.
9. **Above my pay grade** [#118] — Declines a decision, keeps your credibility.
10. **Garbage in, garbage out** [#193] — Explains data dependency to non-technical leaders.

## 16.8 Idioms to understand but never say

You will hear all of these. Recognize them instantly, and let someone else be the one who says them. Some are dated, some are corporate parody, some are actively offensive — and as a non-native speaker you get none of the ironic cover a native speaker might claim.

| Idiom | What it means | Why you should never say it |
|---|---|---|
| Open the kimono | Share information openly | Orientalist and sexualized in origin; a genuine HR risk in US and UK offices. Say "be fully transparent". |
| Drink the Kool-Aid | Accept the company line uncritically | References the 1978 Jonestown mass deaths. Widely used, but the origin makes it ugly once known. Say "bought into it completely". |
| Long pole in the tent | The task determining the timeline | Opaque US military-consultant jargon, and a common source of snickering. Say "the critical path". |
| Put lipstick on a pig | Improve the surface of something bad | Insulting to whoever built the thing; it's an attack disguised as a joke. Say "that's cosmetic, not structural". |
| Eat our own dog food | Use your own product internally | Unappetizing image, and "dogfooding" as a verb is now pure tech in-group speech. Say "we use it ourselves first". |
| Synergy / synergize | Combined value exceeding the parts | The single most parodied word in corporate English. It signals you have nothing specific to say. Name the actual overlap instead. |
| Think outside the box | Be creative | Exhausted since roughly 2005. Asking for it produces nothing. Say "what would we do if the constraint didn't exist?". |
| Take it to the next level | Improve significantly | Means nothing measurable. Managers who say it are usually stalling. Say what the next level actually is. |
| Run it up the flagpole | Test an idea with senior people | Dated 1950s-advertising idiom; sounds like a costume. Say "let me sound out the director". |
| Let's double-click on that | Examine in more detail | Peak consultant-speak, and now openly mocked. Say "let's go one level deeper". |
| Give 110% | Try extremely hard | Mathematically silly and reads as motivational-poster language. Say what specific effort you're asking for. |
| Peel the onion | Uncover successive layers of a problem | Overused to the point of self-parody, and it implies crying. Say "there are a few layers here". |
| Net-net | The final summarized outcome | Banking slang that leaked into everything; sounds like an impression of a CFO. Say "the bottom line is". |
| Rock star / ninja / guru | An exceptionally skilled person | Now considered exclusionary in job descriptions and slightly embarrassing in speech. Say "she's exceptional at this". |
| CYA (cover your ass) | Protect yourself from blame | Crude, and admitting to it out loud damages you more than the risk it describes. Say "let's make sure this is documented". |


---

# Chapter 17 — Phrasal Verbs

> **One-line promise:** You'll swap the stiff Latinate verbs that mark you as a non-native speaker for the 200 phrasal verbs your colleagues actually use, and you'll place the object in the right slot every time.

## 17.1 Why this chapter matters

- Phrasal verbs are the single fastest fluency signal in corporate English. "I will revert with the analysis" and "I'll get back to you with the analysis" carry identical information, but the first one tells everyone in the room that English is your second language, and — unfairly — nudges them toward explaining things to you more slowly.
- Indian English education rewards the Latinate register: *commence, intimate, furnish, revert, elucidate, ascertain*. Those words are correct. They are also, in a 2020s Slack thread or a stand-up call, quietly wrong — they read as either legalistic or dated. Native-level professionals reach for the two-word Germanic version almost every time: *kick off, let know, send over, get back, walk through, find out*.
- The mistakes non-native speakers make with phrasal verbs are rarely about meaning. They're about **placement** and **preposition**. "Run by me the numbers" is understood but jarring. "I'll follow up you" and "reach out him" are the two most common errors in Indian corporate English, and each one is a single-word fix.
- Phrasal verbs are how English encodes **degree and stage**. Ramp up, scale back, phase out, roll out, roll back, wind down — these aren't decorations, they're the vocabulary of project status. Without them you're stuck saying "increase" and "decrease" while everyone else describes a trajectory.
- Most of them nominalize, and the noun form is where written English trips people: *follow-up*, *handover*, *rollout*, *kickoff*, *sign-off*, *workaround*, *backup*, *turnaround*. Get the hyphen rule right once and your emails stop looking translated.
- In an analytics role you talk about data movement all day — pulling, pushing, breaking out, drilling down, filtering out, backing up, spinning up. This is the part of the language your job is literally made of.

## 17.2 The Frequency Ladder — 200 phrases, most-used first

### Tier 1 — Daily driver (phrases 1–60)

Use these until they're automatic. You will hear every one of these in a normal working week, most of them several times a day.

| # | Phrasal verb | Means | Example in use |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | follow up | Chase something to its next step | "I'll follow up with the registrar's office and let you know." |
| 2 | check in | Ask for a quick status update | "Just checking in on the enrollment dashboard — where are we?" |
| 3 | reach out | Make first contact with someone | "I've reached out to the vendor about the API limits." |
| 4 | get back to | Reply later with an answer | "Let me confirm the numbers and get back to you by noon." |
| 5 | catch up | Sync informally, or cover missed ground | "Can we catch up for fifteen minutes before the review?" |
| 6 | circle back | Return to a topic later | "Let's park the schema question and circle back tomorrow." |
| 7 | loop in | Add someone to a thread or discussion | "I'll loop Priya in — she owns the pipeline." |
| 8 | run by | Get a quick opinion before you commit | "Can I run the methodology by you before I publish?" |
| 9 | walk through | Explain step by step, at the listener's pace | "I'll walk you through how the retention metric is built." |
| 10 | run through | Cover a list quickly, item by item | "Let's run through the open tickets and then wrap up." |
| 11 | kick off | Start something formally | "We kick off the data-quality audit on Monday." |
| 12 | wrap up | Finish and close out | "I'm wrapping up the cohort report tonight." |
| 13 | sign off on | Give final approval | "Finance still hasn't signed off on the numbers." |
| 14 | roll out | Release to users, usually in stages | "We're rolling the dashboard out to program directors first." |
| 15 | set up | Configure or arrange | "I've set up a nightly refresh at 2 a.m." |
| 16 | figure out | Work out the answer to something | "We still need to figure out why Tuesday's load failed." |
| 17 | find out | Discover a fact | "Let me find out who owns that table." |
| 18 | work out | Resolve, calculate, or end well | "We worked out a fix with the DBA team." |
| 19 | look into | Investigate | "I'll look into the duplicate applicant records." |
| 20 | go over | Review something together | "Can we go over the model assumptions once more?" |
| 21 | go through | Examine in detail, start to finish | "I went through every row in the reject file." |
| 22 | break down | Split into component parts | "Let me break the churn number down by program." |
| 23 | drill down | Move to a finer level of detail | "Drill down into the Q3 bar — the spike is one cohort." |
| 24 | sort out | Fix a messy problem | "We sorted out the duplicate IDs this morning." |
| 25 | come up with | Produce an idea or a solution | "Can you come up with two options by Thursday?" |
| 26 | put together | Assemble or prepare | "I've put together a one-pager for the Dean." |
| 27 | take on | Accept work or responsibility | "I can't take on another dashboard this sprint." |
| 28 | hand over | Transfer ownership to someone else | "I'll hand the pipeline docs over before I go on leave." |
| 29 | hold off | Delay deliberately | "Let's hold off on the release until QA clears it." |
| 30 | push back | Resist, or resist a date | "I pushed back on the Friday deadline — it isn't realistic." |
| 31 | bring up | Raise a topic | "Someone brought up data privacy in the steering call." |
| 32 | point out | Draw attention to something | "She pointed out that the filter was excluding transfers." |
| 33 | lay out | Present clearly and in order | "The deck lays out three options with costs." |
| 34 | touch base | Make brief contact | "Let's touch base Friday on the vendor contract." |
| 35 | pick up | Take over a task, or notice something | "Can you pick up the ticket while I'm out?" |
| 36 | move on | Go to the next thing | "Agreed — let's move on to staffing." |
| 37 | follow through | Finish what you promised | "The plan was fine; we just didn't follow through." |
| 38 | fill in | Substitute for someone, or supply missing detail | "I'll fill in for Raj at the governance meeting." |
| 39 | fill out | Complete a form | "Everyone needs to fill out the access request." |
| 40 | write up | Produce a written version | "I'll write the findings up tonight." |
| 41 | clean up | Tidy data or a mess | "We cleaned the campus field up — it had nine spellings." |
| 42 | back up | Save a copy, or support a claim | "Back the database up before you run the migration." |
| 43 | speed up | Make faster | "Partitioning sped the query up by a factor of ten." |
| 44 | slow down | Reduce the pace | "Applications slowed down in July, as expected." |
| 45 | ramp up | Increase gradually and deliberately | "We're ramping up reporting ahead of admissions season." |
| 46 | scale back | Reduce scope or ambition | "We scaled the pilot back to two programs." |
| 47 | shut down | Turn off or close permanently | "The old server gets shut down on the 30th." |
| 48 | log in | Access an account | "I can't log in to the BI portal — SSO is rejecting me." |
| 49 | sign up | Register for something | "Forty-two people signed up for the training." |
| 50 | show up | Attend, or appear in the data | "Only half the cohort showed up in the extract." |
| 51 | end up | Finish in a particular state | "We ended up rebuilding the model from scratch." |
| 52 | turn out | Prove to be the case | "It turned out to be a timezone issue." |
| 53 | get through | Complete a volume of work | "I got through half the backlog today." |
| 54 | keep up with | Match the pace of something | "The nightly job can't keep up with the new volume." |
| 55 | carry out | Perform a task, formally | "We carried out a full audit of the source tables." |
| 56 | hang on | Wait a moment | "Hang on — let me share my screen." |
| 57 | go ahead | Proceed, or grant permission | "Go ahead and publish; I've reviewed it." |
| 58 | take over | Assume control of something | "Meera is taking over the reporting workstream." |
| 59 | line up | Arrange in sequence, schedule | "I've lined three vendor demos up for next week." |
| 60 | sync up | Meet briefly to align | "Let's sync up after the stand-up." |

*Separable:* **loop in** (loop her in, never "loop in her"), **run by** (run it by me), **break down** (break it down), **back up** (back it up), **write up** (write it up), **set up** (set it up), **clean up** (clean it up), **hand over** (hand it over), **fill in / fill out** (fill it in), **take on** (take it on), **pick up** (pick it up), **bring up** (bring it up), **point out** (point it out), **lay out** (lay it out), **speed up**, **line up**, **roll out**, **scale back**, **ramp up**, **shut down**, **take over**. With all of these, a pronoun object *must* sit in the middle. **walk through** takes the person in the middle: "walk me through it." Inseparable: follow up, check in, reach out, circle back, look into, go over, go through, come up with, hold off on, keep up with, get through.

### Tier 2 — Weekly (phrases 61–120)

Heard most weeks. These are where you stop sounding merely correct and start sounding like a colleague.

| # | Phrasal verb | Means | Example in use |
|---|---|---|---|
| 61 | flesh out | Add detail to an outline | "Good skeleton — now flesh the methodology section out." |
| 62 | hash out | Argue through to an agreement | "We hashed out the metric definition in a two-hour session." |
| 63 | iron out | Remove the remaining problems | "A few edge cases to iron out, then we ship." |
| 64 | map out | Plan the sequence explicitly | "Let's map out the migration in phases." |
| 65 | carve out | Set aside a portion of time or budget | "I've carved out Thursday morning for the model review." |
| 66 | rule out | Eliminate as a possibility | "We've ruled out a network issue." |
| 67 | narrow down | Reduce to fewer options | "That narrows it down to two source systems." |
| 68 | zero in on | Focus precisely on the cause | "The logs let us zero in on the failing step." |
| 69 | dig into | Examine deeply | "Let me dig into the outliers before I comment." |
| 70 | dive into | Start examining, energetically | "Before we dive into the numbers — one caveat." |
| 71 | break out | Separate into named groups | "Can you break that out by intake year?" |
| 72 | filter out | Exclude by rule | "The query filters out test accounts." |
| 73 | weed out | Remove the poor-quality items | "We weeded out the incomplete applications." |
| 74 | add up | Total correctly, or make sense | "The headcount doesn't add up — we're short by four." |
| 75 | roll back | Revert to a previous state | "We rolled the release back at 3 a.m." |
| 76 | spin up | Create quickly, usually infrastructure | "I'll spin up a test instance this afternoon." |
| 77 | stand up | Get a system running from nothing | "We stood up a staging environment in a day." |
| 78 | tear down | Dismantle | "Tear the sandbox down once the demo's over." |
| 79 | hook up | Connect two things | "I hooked the dashboard up to the live view." |
| 80 | plug in | Connect, or insert a value | "Plug last year's numbers in and see what happens." |
| 81 | dial in | Fine-tune until it's exactly right | "The alert thresholds are finally dialed in." |
| 82 | weigh in | Give your opinion on a decision | "Can you weigh in on the naming convention?" |
| 83 | chime in | Add a comment to a discussion | "Feel free to chime in if I've missed something." |
| 84 | jump in | Interrupt in order to contribute | "Sorry to jump in — one correction on that slide." |
| 85 | step in | Intervene when something is going wrong | "I had to step in when the vendor call went sideways." |
| 86 | step up | Take on more responsibility | "Anil really stepped up while I was on leave." |
| 87 | step back | Take the wider view | "Let's step back — what problem are we actually solving?" |
| 88 | sit in on | Attend as an observer | "Can I sit in on the requirements session?" |
| 89 | drop off | Decline, often sharply | "Engagement drops off after week three." |
| 90 | tail off | Decline gradually | "Applications tail off after the deadline." |
| 91 | level off | Stop rising and stay flat | "Signups leveled off in May." |
| 92 | bottom out | Reach the lowest point and stop falling | "Attrition bottomed out last quarter." |
| 93 | shoot up | Rise very sharply | "Query times shot up after the index was dropped." |
| 94 | call off | Cancel something planned | "They called off the migration window." |
| 95 | put off | Postpone | "Don't put the audit off again." |
| 96 | push out | Move a date later | "We've pushed the launch out by two weeks." |
| 97 | bring forward | Move a date earlier | "Can we bring the review forward to Tuesday?" |
| 98 | move up | Schedule earlier (US) | "They moved the deadline up — it's now the 12th." |
| 99 | hold up | Delay something, or withstand scrutiny | "Legal review is holding the release up." |
| 100 | fall through | Collapse before completion | "The vendor deal fell through last month." |
| 101 | pan out | Turn out well in the end | "If the pilot pans out, we take it to all programs." |
| 102 | play out | Unfold over time | "Let's see how the first cohort plays out." |
| 103 | turn around | Deliver quickly, or reverse a decline | "Can you turn the extract around by Friday?" |
| 104 | take off | Grow very fast | "Usage took off after we added the mobile view." |
| 105 | catch on | Become widely adopted | "The self-serve reports never really caught on." |
| 106 | call out | Highlight explicitly, credit or criticize | "I want to call out Sneha's work on the cleanup." |
| 107 | ping back | Reply on chat | "Ping me back when the job finishes." |
| 108 | get around to | Finally do a delayed task | "I haven't got around to the documentation yet." |
| 109 | come across | Encounter by chance, or be perceived as | "I came across an undocumented view in the schema." |
| 110 | come back to | Return to an earlier point | "Let me come back to your question on sampling." |
| 111 | come up | Arise unexpectedly | "Something's come up — can we push our one-on-one?" |
| 112 | come down to | Reduce to one deciding factor | "It comes down to whether we trust the source." |
| 113 | boil down to | Simplify to the essence | "It boils down to two numbers." |
| 114 | sum up | State the essence briefly | "To sum up: three risks, one blocker." |
| 115 | round up | Raise to a whole number, or gather people | "I rounded the percentages up in the summary." |
| 116 | factor in | Include in the calculation | "Did you factor in the fee waivers?" |
| 117 | account for | Explain, or make up a share of | "International students account for a third of intake." |
| 118 | stand out | Be noticeably different | "One campus stands out — completion is 20 points higher." |
| 119 | cut off | Stop abruptly, or truncate | "The extract cuts off at 10,000 rows." |
| 120 | knock out | Finish something quickly | "I can knock the summary slide out in ten minutes." |

*Separable:* **flesh out**, **hash out**, **iron out**, **map out**, **carve out**, **rule out**, **narrow down**, **break out**, **filter out**, **weed out**, **roll back**, **spin up**, **stand up**, **tear down**, **hook up**, **plug in**, **dial in**, **call off**, **put off**, **push out**, **bring forward**, **move up**, **hold up**, **turn around**, **call out**, **round up**, **factor in**, **cut off**, **knock out**. Say "iron them out," "rule it out," "turn it around" — the pronoun never goes at the end. Inseparable: zero in on, dig into, dive into, add up, sit in on, drop off, tail off, level off, bottom out, shoot up, fall through, pan out, play out, catch on, get around to, come across, come back to, come up, come down to, boil down to, stand out, account for.

### Tier 3 — Situational (phrases 121–170)

You'll need each of these a few times a quarter — during a migration, a hire, a contract, a reorg, a deadline slip.

| # | Phrasal verb | Means | Example in use |
|---|---|---|---|
| 121 | phase out | Retire gradually | "We're phasing the legacy reports out by December." |
| 122 | roll off | Leave a project as planned | "Two contractors roll off at the end of the month." |
| 123 | bring on | Add a person to the team | "We're bringing on a data engineer in September." |
| 124 | bring in | Involve someone, or generate revenue | "Let's bring Legal in before we sign anything." |
| 125 | hand off | Pass work to the next owner | "I'll hand the ETL off to the platform team." |
| 126 | pass on | Decline, or relay a message | "We passed on the premium tier — too expensive." |
| 127 | take up | Raise formally with someone | "I'll take it up with the program office." |
| 128 | take down | Record, or remove from service | "I'll take the action items down as we go." |
| 129 | put forward | Propose formally | "She put forward a phased approach." |
| 130 | put in | Submit, or invest effort | "We've put in a request for two more licenses." |
| 131 | put up with | Tolerate something unsatisfactory | "We've put up with the manual export long enough." |
| 132 | hold back | Withhold, or restrain yourself | "Don't hold back — what's the real risk here?" |
| 133 | hold out for | Wait for a better offer | "They're holding out for a bigger discount." |
| 134 | fall back on | Use as a backup | "If the API fails, we fall back on the flat file." |
| 135 | draw up | Prepare a formal document | "I'll draw up a short scope note." |
| 136 | draw on | Make use of a resource or body of work | "The forecast draws on five years of intake data." |
| 137 | lay off | Make people redundant | "The vendor laid off half their support team." |
| 138 | cut over | Switch production to the new system | "We cut over to the new warehouse on Saturday." |
| 139 | switch over | Change from one thing to another | "We switched over to Power BI last year." |
| 140 | opt out | Choose not to participate | "Students can opt out of the analytics tracking." |
| 141 | buy into | Accept and actively support | "Leadership hasn't bought into the new metric yet." |
| 142 | sign on | Formally join an initiative | "Two more departments signed on to the pilot." |
| 143 | bank on | Rely on confidently, often unwisely | "Don't bank on the file arriving before nine." |
| 144 | count on | Depend on reliably | "You can count on the Monday refresh being complete." |
| 145 | build in | Include from the start | "We built in a two-week buffer." |
| 146 | bake in | Embed so it can't be skipped | "Validation is baked into the pipeline now." |
| 147 | lock in | Fix so it can't change | "Let's lock the definition in before we build." |
| 148 | lock down | Restrict access, or finalize | "Access to the raw tables is locked down." |
| 149 | nail down | Settle the specifics | "We still need to nail the scope down." |
| 150 | pin down | Get a firm commitment from someone | "I can't pin the vendor down on a date." |
| 151 | firm up | Make more definite | "Can you firm the estimate up by Wednesday?" |
| 152 | shore up | Strengthen a weak point | "We shored up the validation checks." |
| 153 | beef up | Add strength or resource | "They beefed up the support SLA after the outage." |
| 154 | trim down | Reduce in size | "Trim the deck down to eight slides." |
| 155 | pare back | Cut to essentials | "We pared the scope back to one program." |
| 156 | wind down | Bring gradually to an end | "The project winds down in March." |
| 157 | gear up | Prepare for something demanding | "We're gearing up for admissions season." |
| 158 | tee up | Set something up for someone else to act on | "I'll tee the decision up for the Dean." |
| 159 | queue up | Line up for processing | "I've queued three jobs up for tonight." |
| 160 | stack up | Compare against a benchmark | "How does our completion rate stack up against peers?" |
| 161 | size up | Assess quickly | "Give me a day to size up the effort." |
| 162 | scope out | Investigate the extent of something | "Let's scope out what the migration actually involves." |
| 163 | skim through | Read quickly for the gist | "I skimmed through the contract — the SLA is weak." |
| 164 | flag up | Raise as a concern (UK/EU) | "I flagged the data-quality issue up in the minutes." |
| 165 | chase up | Pursue someone for a response (UK/EU) | "I'll chase up the vendor for the invoice." |
| 166 | head off | Prevent before it happens | "That caveat heads off the obvious question." |
| 167 | get ahead of | Act before a problem reaches you | "Let's get ahead of the Dean's question on cost." |
| 168 | fall behind | Slip against the schedule | "We're falling behind on documentation." |
| 169 | run over | Exceed the allotted time | "We ran over by ten minutes — apologies." |
| 170 | push through | Force to completion despite friction | "We pushed the change through before the freeze." |

*Separable:* **phase out**, **bring on**, **bring in**, **hand off**, **pass on**, **take up**, **take down**, **put forward**, **put in**, **hold back**, **draw up**, **lay off**, **build in**, **bake in**, **lock in**, **lock down**, **nail down**, **pin down**, **firm up**, **shore up**, **beef up**, **trim down**, **pare back**, **wind down**, **tee up**, **queue up**, **size up**, **scope out**, **flag up**, **head off**, **push through**. Note the three-word ones are all inseparable and take a fixed preposition: **put up with**, **hold out for**, **fall back on**, **get ahead of**. Also inseparable: roll off, buy into, sign on, bank on, count on, stack up, skim through, chase up, fall behind, run over.

### Tier 4 — Rare but high-impact (phrases 171–200)

Low frequency, high signal. Land one of these correctly in a leadership meeting and you sound like you've worked in English your whole career.

| # | Phrasal verb | Means | Example in use |
|---|---|---|---|
| 171 | hammer out | Negotiate the details the hard way | "We hammered out the SLA over three calls." |
| 172 | thrash out | Debate until fully resolved (UK/EU) | "The steering group thrashed out the funding split." |
| 173 | talk through | Discuss something in full, together | "Let's talk through the trade-offs properly." |
| 174 | talk down | Persuade someone to a lower number | "I talked them down to eighteen thousand." |
| 175 | talk up | Present as more impressive than it is | "Let's not talk the pilot up before we have results." |
| 176 | sound out | Test someone's opinion privately | "I'll sound out the program directors first." |
| 177 | bounce off | Test an idea on someone informally | "Can I bounce an idea off you for two minutes?" |
| 178 | kick around | Discuss loosely, without commitment | "We kicked a few options around at lunch." |
| 179 | spell out | State in explicit, unmissable detail | "The report spells out exactly what's missing." |
| 180 | gloss over | Skip lightly past a weak point | "The deck glosses over the sample size." |
| 181 | paper over | Hide a real problem with a cosmetic fix | "That patch papers over a schema issue." |
| 182 | smooth over | Ease a conflict after the fact | "I smoothed things over with the vendor." |
| 183 | brush off | Dismiss too easily | "Don't brush off the reconciliation gap." |
| 184 | shrug off | Ignore without apparent concern | "They shrugged off the latency complaints." |
| 185 | write off | Give up on, or discount entirely | "I wouldn't write the model off after one bad month." |
| 186 | rein in | Bring back under control | "We need to rein in the number of custom reports." |
| 187 | churn out | Produce in high volume, low ceremony | "We churn out forty reports a month." |
| 188 | rattle off | List quickly from memory | "She rattled off the definitions without notes." |
| 189 | shake out | Become clear once things settle | "Let's see how the numbers shake out after cleanup." |
| 190 | shake up | Reorganize dramatically | "The reorg shook up the whole reporting line." |
| 191 | blow up | Fail dramatically, or expand uncontrollably | "The join blew the row count up to nine million." |
| 192 | blow over | Pass without lasting damage | "The complaint blew over once we showed the audit trail." |
| 193 | bear out | Confirm with evidence | "The data bears out what the faculty were saying." |
| 194 | square with | Reconcile with another fact | "That doesn't square with last week's number." |
| 195 | stave off | Postpone something bad | "A patch staved off the outage until the weekend." |
| 196 | see through | Carry to completion | "I'd like to see this migration through before I move roles." |
| 197 | back off | Reduce pressure | "They backed off once we shared the roadmap." |
| 198 | back out of | Withdraw from a commitment | "The partner backed out of the data-sharing agreement." |
| 199 | pull off | Achieve something difficult | "We pulled off the migration with zero downtime." |
| 200 | pull back | Withdraw or reduce deliberately | "We pulled back on scope to hit the date." |

*Separable:* **hammer out**, **thrash out**, **talk through**, **talk down**, **talk up**, **sound out**, **kick around**, **spell out**, **smooth over**, **brush off**, **shrug off**, **write off**, **rein in**, **churn out**, **rattle off**, **shake up**, **stave off**, **pull off**. The tricky one is **see through**: it is *always* split when it means "finish" — "see it through," never "see through it" (that phrase means "detect the deception"). **bounce off** splits around the idea: "bounce it off her." Inseparable: gloss over, paper over, shake out, blow over, bear out, square with, back off, back out of, pull back. Note **blow up** works both ways: "the join blew up" (intransitive) and "it blew the row count up" (separable).

## 17.3 Pattern Breakdown — learn the formula, not the sentence

**Pattern 1:** `Let me + [VERB] + and get back to you (by [TIME])`
- "Let me check the source table and get back to you by lunch."
- "Let me sound out the program office and get back to you tomorrow."
- "Let me dig into the outliers and get back to you before the review."
*Watch out:* Never "I will revert." Also never "get back you" — the preposition *to* is not optional.

**Pattern 2:** `Can I run [THING] by you (before [ACTION])?`
- "Can I run the methodology by you before I publish?"
- "Can I run these two options by you quickly?"
- "Can I run it by you after stand-up?"
*Watch out:* The object always sits between *run* and *by*. "Run by you the numbers" is the single most recognizable non-native word order in corporate English.

**Pattern 3:** `I'll walk you through + [THING]`
- "I'll walk you through the pipeline in five minutes."
- "Let me walk you through how the completion rate is defined."
- "Can someone walk me through why the totals differ?"
*Watch out:* The *person* goes in the middle, not the thing. "I'll walk through you the model" is wrong; "I'll walk through the model" (no listener named) is fine but colder.

**Pattern 4:** `Let's + circle back / touch base / sync up + on [TOPIC] + [TIME]`
- "Let's circle back on the schema question tomorrow."
- "Let's touch base on the vendor contract Friday."
- "Let's sync up on staffing after the board pack goes out."
*Watch out:* The preposition is *on* for the topic and bare for the time. "Circle back about it" is understandable but slightly off; "circle back to it" is fine when *it* is a discussion point.

**Pattern 5:** `Can you break [METRIC] down by [DIMENSION]?`
- "Can you break enrollment down by campus?"
- "Can you break that out by intake year and program?"
- "Break the churn number down by cohort and I'll take a look."
*Watch out:* *Break down* = split into parts; *break out* = pull one group into its own line. Analysts use both, and mixing them up will get you the wrong table.

**Pattern 6:** `I'll loop [PERSON] in on [TOPIC]`
- "I'll loop Priya in on the API change."
- "Looping in Legal for visibility."
- "Can you loop me in on that thread?"
*Watch out:* With a pronoun, splitting is compulsory: "loop her in," never "loop in her." With a full name, both orders are acceptable and "looping in Priya" is the standard email opener.

**Pattern 7:** `We're + ramping up / scaling back / phasing out / winding down + [THING]`
- "We're ramping up reporting ahead of admissions season."
- "We're scaling the pilot back to two programs."
- "We're phasing the legacy reports out by December."
*Watch out:* These describe a *trajectory*, not a one-off change. Don't say "we ramped up the number by 3" — ramping is gradual and ongoing.

**Pattern 8:** `Let's hold off on [ACTION] until [CONDITION]`
- "Let's hold off on the release until QA signs off."
- "I'd hold off on sharing this until the reconciliation is done."
- "Can we hold off on the vendor call until we have the numbers?"
*Watch out:* *Hold off* takes *on*; *put off* takes a direct object. "Let's hold off the release" sounds like you're physically restraining it.

**Pattern 9:** `I'd push back on [THING], because [REASON]`
- "I'd push back on the Friday date — QA needs three days."
- "I pushed back on the scope, and they agreed to drop two reports."
- "Worth pushing back on that assumption before we build."
*Watch out:* *Push back* is intransitive and needs *on*. "I pushed back the deadline" means something different — you moved the date later.

**Pattern 10:** `[PROJECT] + kicks off / wraps up / goes out + [TIME]`
- "The audit kicks off Monday."
- "The migration wraps up on the 14th."
- "We kick off with the program directors, then roll it out more widely."
*Watch out:* *Kick off* is intransitive here but separable when transitive: "let's kick the session off with the headline number."

**Pattern 11:** `Who's signing off on [DELIVERABLE]?`
- "Who's signing off on the numbers before they go to the board?"
- "I can't sign off on this until the reconciliation clears."
- "Finance has signed off; we're clear to publish."
*Watch out:* *Sign off* takes *on* for the thing approved. The noun form is hyphenated: "I'm waiting on sign-off from Finance."

**Pattern 12:** `We still need to + nail down / flesh out / iron out / hash out + [THING]`
- "We still need to nail the scope down."
- "We still need to flesh out the methodology section."
- "There are two definitions to hash out before we build."
*Watch out:* These four are not interchangeable. *Nail down* = make specific. *Flesh out* = add detail. *Iron out* = remove problems. *Hash out* = argue to agreement.

**Pattern 13:** `Can you turn [THING] around by [TIME]?`
- "Can you turn the extract around by Friday?"
- "We turned the fix around in two hours."
- "That's a tight turnaround, but it's doable."
*Watch out:* The noun is one word, *turnaround*; the verb is two words. Getting this wrong in writing is a common tell.

**Pattern 14:** `[PHRASAL VERB → NOUN] the hyphen rule`
- "I'll follow up on Monday." → "Sending a quick follow-up."
- "We roll out next week." → "The rollout starts next week."
- "I'll hand over on Thursday." → "The handover doc is in the repo."
*Watch out:* Verb = two words, never hyphenated. Noun = one word or hyphenated: *follow-up, sign-off, kickoff, rollout, handover, backup, workaround, turnaround, setup, login*. "Please setup a meeting" is wrong; "please set up a meeting" is right.

**Pattern 15:** `I'll + reach out to / follow up with / check in with + [PERSON] + on [TOPIC]`
- "I'll reach out to the vendor on the API limits."
- "I'll follow up with Finance on the sign-off."
- "Let me check in with Priya on where the pipeline stands."
*Watch out:* Three different prepositions for three near-synonyms: *reach out **to*** someone, *follow up **with*** someone, *check in **with*** someone. "Follow up you" and "reach out him" are the two most frequent errors in Indian corporate English — and both are one-word fixes.

## 17.4 Scenarios — see the phrases working

### Scenario 17.1 — Monday stand-up, reporting team

**Setting:** Fifteen-minute call. You lead program analytics; three people report in.

**You:** Morning, everyone. Let's run through the board quickly and then wrap up — I know some of you have the faculty session at ten. [#10, #12]

**Raj:** The nightly load failed twice last week. I've been digging into it and it looks like the volume's grown past what the job can handle. [#69]

**You:** So it's not keeping up with the new intake? [#54]

**Raj:** Right. Partitioning should speed it up, but I'd want to test it in staging first. [#43]

**You:** Go ahead. Can you spin up an instance today and turn a test around by Thursday? [#57, #76, #103]

**Raj:** Should be fine.

**You:** Good. Meera, where are we with the dashboard?

**Meera:** Nearly there. Two edge cases to iron out on the transfers filter, then it's ready to roll out. [#63, #14]

**You:** Who's signing off? [#13]

**Meera:** The program office. I'll follow up with them today. [#1]

**You:** Do that, and loop me in on the reply — I want to get ahead of any question about the definitions before it goes wide. [#7, #167]

**Why this worked:**
- The whole update ran on six phrasal verbs and no Latinate vocabulary. Nobody had to slow down for anybody.
- "Turn a test around by Thursday" put a deliverable and a date in five words. "Complete a test of the aforementioned by Thursday" would have cost you the room.
- "Get ahead of any question" reframed a routine sign-off as risk management, which is how you sound senior without claiming to be.

### Scenario 17.2 — Vendor call about a delayed integration

**Setting:** Thirty minutes with an account manager whose API has been throttling your nightly pull for three weeks.

**You:** Thanks for making time. I want to talk through where we've landed and what happens next. [#173]

**Account manager:** Of course. I know the throttling has been painful.

**You:** It has. Let me lay out the situation as we see it. [#33] Since the 3rd, the pull cuts off at about 40,000 records. [#119] We've ruled out anything on our side — network, credentials, retry logic. [#66]

**Account manager:** That squares with what our engineering team is seeing. [#194]

**You:** Good, that helps. So here's where I'd push back. [#30] Your team told us on the 11th that a fix was going out that week, and it's now the 28th. I don't want to write off the relationship over one bad month — but I do need a date I can hold you to. [#185]

**Account manager:** I understand. I'll chase it up internally today. [#165]

**You:** I'd rather not leave it there. Can we pin down a date on this call, even a provisional one? [#150]

**Account manager:** Let me sound out the platform lead and come back to you by Wednesday. [#176, #110]

**You:** Wednesday works. And if it slips again, I'll need to take it up with your director — I'd rather flag that now than surprise you later. [#127, #164]

**Account manager:** Understood. That's fair.

**Why this worked:**
- "Squares with," "ruled out," and "cuts off" did precise technical work in ordinary words — no jargon, no ambiguity about what's broken.
- "I don't want to write off the relationship" gave something away before taking something. That's what makes "pin down a date" land as reasonable rather than aggressive.
- "I'd rather flag that now than surprise you later" is an escalation threat that costs you nothing relationally, because it's framed as courtesy.

### Scenario 17.3 — Handing over a pipeline before leave

**Setting:** You're out for three weeks. A one-hour session with the colleague picking it up.

**You:** Right — I'll walk you through the whole thing, and then we'll go over the parts that actually break. [#9, #20]

**Colleague:** How much of it is documented?

**You:** Most of it. There's a handover doc in the repo, but let me flesh out two sections while we talk. [#61]

**Colleague:** What's most likely to go wrong?

**You:** Two things. The source file sometimes shows up late — anywhere up to eleven. [#50] If it hasn't landed by then, don't chase it, just fall back on the previous day's snapshot and note it in the log. [#134]

**Colleague:** And the second?

**You:** The transfers filter. If someone changes the campus list upstream without telling us, the numbers drop off and nobody notices for a week. [#89] I've built in a row-count check that fires an alert. [#145] If it goes off, ping me back — I don't mind. [#107]

**Colleague:** I'd rather not bother you on leave.

**You:** Honestly, one message beats me coming back to a mess. But you can hold off on anything cosmetic — none of that has to be sorted out before I'm back. [#29, #24]

**Colleague:** Good. Anything I should not touch?

**You:** Don't roll back the schema change from last month, whatever happens. It'll look like it fixes things and it'll break the archive. [#75]

**Why this worked:**
- Walk through → go over → flesh out is a natural escalation from overview to detail to gap-filling. Native speakers sequence handovers this way instinctively.
- "Fall back on the previous day's snapshot" is a complete contingency instruction in seven words.
- "You can hold off on anything cosmetic" gave explicit permission to deprioritize — the most useful thing you can hand someone along with the work.

### Scenario 17.4 — A rollout goes wrong

**Setting:** Thursday, 9:15 a.m. The new dashboard went live overnight and program directors are seeing wrong numbers.

**Director:** The completion figures are nowhere near what we reported last term.

**You:** I've seen it. We're on it. Let me tell you what we know and what we're doing. [#3 implied]

**Director:** How bad is it?

**You:** The numbers themselves are fine in the warehouse — it's the new view that's wrong. A join blew the row count up, so completions are being double-counted for anyone with a transfer record. [#191]

**Director:** So everything I looked at this morning is wrong?

**You:** For transfer students, yes. For everyone else, correct. I'd rather spell that out than let you find it later. [#179]

**Director:** What now?

**You:** We're rolling it back to the old view within the hour. [#75] Then we iron out the join, test against last term's published figures, and roll out again Monday — with the transfer cases broken out separately so you can see them. [#63, #14, #71]

**Director:** Will that hold?

**You:** It'll hold. The reason we missed it is that our test data didn't include transfers — so we're baking a transfer case into the test suite. That heads off this whole class of problem. [#146, #166]

**Director:** Fine. Keep me posted.

**You:** I'll check in at midday with the rollback confirmed, and again Monday morning before we publish. [#2]

**Why this worked:**
- "Blew the row count up" explained a technical failure in language a non-technical Dean's-office stakeholder understood instantly. No apology theater, no jargon shield.
- Roll back → iron out → roll out gave a three-stage recovery plan without a project plan document.
- "Baking a transfer case into the test suite" turned an incident into a systemic fix, which is the difference between being trusted again and being watched.

### Scenario 17.5 — Leadership review with the Dean's office

**Setting:** Twenty minutes on the quarterly analytics review. Two associate deans, one CFO-side finance lead.

**Associate Dean:** Take us through the headline first.

**You:** Happy to. It boils down to two numbers. [#113] Applications are up nine percent year on year, and completion has leveled off after two years of decline. [#91]

**Finance lead:** Leveled off, or bottomed out?

**You:** Fair question — I'd say leveled off. Bottomed out implies it turns up from here, and the data doesn't bear that out yet. [#92, #193]

**Associate Dean:** What's driving the applications number?

**You:** Mostly one campus. Let me break it down. [#22] Bengaluru accounts for most of the growth; the other three are flat. [#117] If you strip Bengaluru out, we're up one percent.

**Finance lead:** So we shouldn't talk it up externally.

**You:** I'd be careful, yes. If we build the communications around nine percent and someone drills down, it doesn't hold. [#175, #23, #99]

**Associate Dean:** What do you need from us?

**You:** Two things. First, sign-off on the completion definition — we've had three versions floating around, and I'd like to lock one in this quarter. [#13, #147] Second, a decision on the legacy reports. We're carrying forty of them and I'd like to phase most of them out. [#121]

**Associate Dean:** Draw up a short note on both and I'll take it to the committee. [#135]

**You:** I'll have it with you Monday. And I'll flag the ones I think are genuinely load-bearing so we don't cut something people rely on. [#164]

**Why this worked:**
- Refusing "bottomed out" in favor of "leveled off" was a two-word display of analytical precision that made everything else you said more credible.
- "If someone drills down, it doesn't hold" is a risk warning that names the mechanism, not just the worry.
- Ending with a specific ask ("sign-off," "a decision") plus a self-imposed deadline is how you leave a leadership review with authority rather than homework.

## 17.5 Avoid This → Say This

| Instead of (too formal / non-native / vague) | Say | Why it lands better |
|---|---|---|
| I will revert on this. | I'll get back to you on this. | "Revert" means "go back to a previous state" in global English — nobody outside South Asia reads it as "reply." |
| Please intimate me once done. | Please let me know once it's done. | "Intimate" as a verb is archaic and, as an adjective, means something else entirely. |
| Kindly furnish the details. | Could you send the details over? | "Furnish" belongs in a legal filing. "Send over" is what a colleague says. |
| We will commence the project on Monday. | We kick off on Monday. | Commence is ceremonial. Kick off is what appears in every calendar invite. |
| The project has been concluded. | We've wrapped up the project. | Concluded sounds like a treaty. Wrapped up sounds like work. |
| Please do the needful. | Could you take care of this? | "Do the needful" is instantly recognizable as dated Indian-English office idiom. |
| I will apprise you of the developments. | I'll keep you posted. | Four words, zero effort for the listener, same information. |
| Kindly revert at the earliest. | Let me know when you can — ideally by Friday. | "At the earliest" is vague; a named day is actionable and less demanding in tone. |
| We need to ascertain the root cause. | We need to figure out what actually caused it. | Ascertain makes you sound like you're reading from an audit report. |
| I would like to elucidate the methodology. | Let me walk you through the methodology. | Walk through signals you'll go at their pace — elucidate signals a lecture. |
| Please find the attached report. | I've attached the report. / Sending the report over. | "Please find attached" is dead formal-letter English. |
| We shall augment the reporting capacity. | We're ramping up reporting. | Augment is correct and lifeless; ramping up implies a trajectory. |
| The scope has been curtailed. | We've scaled the scope back. | Scaled back is neutral; curtailed sounds like someone was punished. |
| We will discontinue the legacy reports. | We're phasing the legacy reports out. | Phasing out communicates gradual, planned retirement — discontinue sounds abrupt. |
| Let us deliberate on the metric definition. | Let's hash out the metric definition. | Deliberate sounds like a jury. Hash out sounds like a working session. |
| I wish to solicit your opinion. | Can I get your take on this? / Can you weigh in? | Solicit is either legal or unintentionally funny. |
| Please provide clarification on point three. | Can you clear up point three for me? | Clear up is direct without being a formal request for information. |
| We shall implement the solution in stages. | We're rolling it out in stages. | Roll out is the standard word for staged release; implement is fine but generic. |
| The deployment has been reversed. | We rolled it back. | Rolled back is the actual term of art, and it's three syllables shorter. |
| I request an extension of the timeline. | Can we push the date out by a week? | Naming the amount makes it a negotiation rather than a plea. |
| We must expedite the delivery. | Can you turn it around faster? | Expedite is a purchase-order word; turn around is a people word. |
| I shall initiate contact with the vendor. | I'll reach out to the vendor. | Initiate contact sounds like surveillance. |
| Please be informed that the job failed. | Heads up — the job failed. | "Please be informed" adds nothing but distance. |
| We anticipate the numbers will stabilize. | We expect the numbers to level off. | Level off is the exact word for a curve flattening; stabilize is vaguer. |
| Kindly assist me in resolving this issue. | Can you help me sort this out? | Sort out is warm, specific, and doesn't beg. |

## 17.6 Practice Drills

### Drill 1 — The 10× rep

Anchor phrase: **run by**. Say all ten aloud, twice, and pay attention to where the object lands.

1. "Can I run something by you?"
2. "Can I run this by you quickly?"
3. "Can I run the methodology by you before I publish?"
4. "Let me run these two options by you."
5. "I want to run the definition by Finance first."
6. "Mind if I run a draft by you this afternoon?"
7. "I'll run it by Priya and come back to you."
8. "Has anyone run this by the program office?"
9. "Run it by me before it goes to the Dean."
10. "I ran it by Legal last week — they're fine with it."

If any of those came out as "run by you this," stop and do the whole list again. The object goes in the middle, every time, with no exceptions.

### Drill 2 — Slot swap

Pattern: `We still need to [NAIL DOWN / FLESH OUT / IRON OUT / HASH OUT / MAP OUT] + [THING] + before [MILESTONE]`

Speak all eight:

1. "We still need to nail the scope down before we start building."
2. "We still need to flesh out the methodology before it goes to review."
3. "We still need to iron out the transfers filter before we roll out."
4. "We still need to hash out the completion definition before the board pack."
5. "We still need to map out the migration before we commit to a date."
6. "We still need to nail down who signs off before we publish."
7. "We still need to iron out two edge cases before Thursday."
8. "We still need to hash this out before it escalates."

### Drill 3 — Cold open

One sentence each, spoken out loud, using at least one phrasal verb. Don't write them down first.

1. A colleague asks for the enrollment numbers by tomorrow morning. You can do it, but only if you drop something else. What do you say?
2. Your manager suggests launching the dashboard on Friday. You think it needs another week of testing. One sentence.
3. A vendor has missed a fix date twice. You want a firm commitment on this call without escalating yet.
4. A director asks a question in a review that you can't answer from memory.
5. You're joining a project mid-flight and need someone to explain the pipeline to you.

### Drill 4 — AI roleplay prompt

```
You are Bettina Hoffmann, a German-born Head of Institutional Research at a
European business school. You have twenty years in higher-education analytics,
you speak excellent but very formal English, and you are politely allergic to
vague commitments. We are on a 20-minute call about a joint reporting standard
between our two schools.

Roleplay this call with me. Stay in character. Ask concrete questions about
timelines, ownership, and definitions. If I give a vague answer, press once,
politely. Do not break character to coach me during the call.

At the end of 20 turns, stop and give me feedback in exactly this format:

1. PHRASAL VERBS I USED — list each one and mark it correct, wrong preposition,
   or wrong word order.
2. LATINATE SLIPS — every place I used a stiff formal verb (revert, intimate,
   furnish, commence, ascertain, apprise, elucidate) and the phrasal verb a
   native-level speaker would have used instead.
3. MISSED OPPORTUNITIES — three moments where a phrasal verb would have made
   me sound more natural, with the exact sentence I said and the rewrite.
4. SEPARABILITY ERRORS — any time I put a pronoun in the wrong slot.
5. ONE THING TO DRILL — the single highest-value fix, in one sentence.
```

## 17.7 Master these 10 first

1. **follow up (with someone, on something)** — Preposition kills most learners.
2. **get back to you** — Retires "I will revert" permanently.
3. **run [it] by you** — Object in the middle, always.
4. **walk you through [it]** — Person in the middle, always.
5. **loop [her] in** — Pronoun must split the verb.
6. **circle back on [topic]** — Parks a question without dropping it.
7. **sign off on [deliverable]** — The word for institutional approval.
8. **roll out / roll back** — Standard vocabulary for any release.
9. **break [it] down by [dimension]** — The core verb of your job.
10. **push back on [something]** — Disagreement that sounds senior, not difficult.

## 17.8 One phrasal verb, several meanings

The eight highest-traffic phrasal verbs in this chapter each carry three or more common senses. Context does all the disambiguating, which is exactly why they trip people up: the same two words mean different things in two consecutive sentences. Learn the senses as separate items.

| Phrasal verb | Sense | Example in use |
|---|---|---|
| pick up | Take over a task from someone | "Can you pick up the ticket while I'm out?" |
| pick up | Notice or detect something | "The validation check picked up forty bad rows." |
| pick up | Increase or improve | "Applications picked up in the last two weeks." |
| take off | Grow very fast | "Usage took off after we added the mobile view." |
| take off | Remove something from a list or figure | "Take the test accounts off and the number drops by nine." |
| take off | Be absent from work | "I'm taking Friday off." |
| run through | Cover a list quickly | "Let's run through the open tickets." |
| run through | Rehearse | "Can we run through the deck once before the Dean joins?" |
| run through | Use up or burn through | "We ran through the API quota by mid-morning." |
| get through | Complete a volume of work | "I got through half the backlog today." |
| get through | Reach someone by phone or message | "I couldn't get through to the vendor all morning." |
| get through | Survive something difficult | "We got through admissions season with two people." |
| come up | Arise unexpectedly | "Something's come up — can we push our one-on-one?" |
| come up | Be raised in discussion | "Data privacy came up twice in the steering call." |
| come up | Approach in time | "Renewal comes up in March." |
| go over | Review together | "Can we go over the model assumptions?" |
| go over | Exceed a limit | "The report went over the page count." |
| go over | Be received a particular way | "The new dashboard went over well with the directors." |
| fall out | Stop being on good terms | "The two teams fell out over ownership of the metric." |
| fall out | Drop out of a dataset or process | "About sixty records fall out at the dedupe step." |
| fall out | Result or follow from something | "Let's see what falls out of the audit before we plan." |
| work out | Calculate or determine | "I worked out the weighted average by campus." |
| work out | End well, resolve successfully | "It worked out — Finance approved the extra license." |
| work out | Agree on something jointly | "We worked out a fix with the DBA team." |

*How to keep them straight:* the meaning is usually carried by the **object**, not the verb. *Pick up a ticket* (task), *pick up an error* (detect), *pick up in June* (increase — no object at all). If a phrasal verb has no object, you are almost always in the intransitive sense: "usage took off," "applications picked up," "it worked out." When you're unsure which sense a speaker meant, ask with the object in your question — "picked up as in you'll own it, or as in the numbers went up?" — rather than asking what they meant.
