# Appendix A — The 60 phrases to master before anything else

If you do nothing else with this book, install these. They cover roughly 80% of the moments where a non-native professional freezes, over-apologizes, or sounds blunt.

### Buying time and thinking aloud

1. "Let me think about that for a moment."
2. "That's a good question — let me make sure I answer the right one."
3. "Can I come back to you on that by end of day?"
4. "I want to give you an accurate answer, so let me check."
5. "Off the top of my head, roughly X — but let me confirm."

### Not knowing

6. "I'm not sure yet. Let me look into it."
7. "I don't have that number in front of me."
8. "That's outside my area, but I can find out who owns it."
9. "I'd be guessing, and I don't want to guess on this."
10. "I don't know — I'll find out and get back to you today."

### Clarifying

11. "Just to make sure we're aligned..."
12. "So, if I understand correctly..."
13. "Could you walk me through your thinking?"
14. "When you say X, do you mean...?"
15. "Could you say a bit more about that?"
16. "What would success look like here?"

### Agreeing

17. "That makes sense."
18. "That sounds reasonable."
19. "I'm aligned with that."
20. "Agreed — and I'd add one thing."
21. "Fair point."

### Disagreeing

22. "I'm not entirely convinced that's the best approach."
23. "I see it slightly differently."
24. "I'd challenge that assumption."
25. "I see a couple of concerns with this approach."
26. "I agree with you to an extent, but..."
27. "Help me understand the reasoning there."
28. "I'd want to test that before we commit to it."

### Recommending

29. "My recommendation would be to..."
30. "I'd lean toward..."
31. "I'd suggest we start with X and see what it tells us."
32. "There are two options — I'd go with the second, and here's why."
33. "The decision we need to make today is..."

### Meetings

34. "Can I jump in here?"
35. "Before we move on, can I add one thing?"
36. "Could we park that for now and come back to it?"
37. "Let's take that offline."
38. "Just to bring it back to the main point..."
39. "So the actions are — X owns A, I own B, by Friday."
40. "We're at time — shall I stop there?"

### Presenting data

41. "The headline is..."
42. "The key takeaway is..."
43. "We're seeing a significant increase in..."
44. "Directionally, that's right — I'd want to verify the exact figure."
45. "The data doesn't support that conclusion, unfortunately."
46. "The two reports use different definitions — let me explain."

### Explaining technical work

47. "At a high level..."
48. "The intuition behind this is..."
49. "The technical details aren't important here; what matters is..."
50. "In practical terms, that means..."

### Pushing back on scope

51. "That's a reasonable ask — it just moves something else."
52. "If we do X, then Y has to shift. Which would you prefer?"
53. "I can get you a rough version by Friday or an accurate one by Wednesday."
54. "I'd rather give you a right answer tomorrow than a wrong one today."

### Owning problems

55. "That one's on me. Here's what I'm doing about it."
56. "I got that wrong — the corrected number is X."
57. "I should have flagged this earlier."

### Closing

58. "Let me summarize what I heard."
59. "So where we've landed is..."
60. "What I need from you is a decision on X by Thursday."

# Appendix B — Situation index: what to say when

| The moment | Chapter | Section |
|---|---|---|
| You need to interrupt a talkative senior person | 3 | Tier 1 |
| You're asked a question live and don't have the answer | 3, 4, 13 | Tier 1 |
| A stakeholder says "this number looks wrong" | 9 | §9.4 |
| You must retract a number you already published | 9 | Tier 3 |
| Someone asks "can't we just use ChatGPT for this?" | 8 | Tier 2 |
| You need to explain why 92% accuracy isn't good enough | 8 | §8.4 |
| Your manager proposes a method you know is wrong | 10 | §10.4 |
| You're overruled and must support the decision anyway | 10 | Tier 3 |
| You're asked for a two-week job in two days | 11 | §11.4 |
| Two directors both want to be priority one | 11 | §11.4 |
| A salary or promotion conversation | 5, 11 | Tier 2 |
| Giving corrective feedback to someone on your team | 12 | §12.3 |
| Announcing a cancelled project | 12 | §12.4 |
| A two-minute corridor update to a VP or dean | 13 | §13.4 |
| Being cut off mid-slide: "what's the bottom line?" | 13 | Tier 1 |
| Asking for budget | 13 | §13.4 |
| The awkward five minutes before a call starts | 14 | Tier 1 |
| A conference where you know nobody | 14 | §14.4 |
| You've been talked over three times | 15 | §15.4 |
| You keep saying "sorry" and "just" | 15 | §15.3 |
| Admitting a mistake to a senior leader | 15 | §15.4 |
| You don't understand what a US colleague meant | 6 | §6.8 |
| Writing to a German or Nordic counterpart | 7 | §7.8 |
| You sound like a textbook in email | 17 | §17.5 |
| An idiom was used and you missed it | 16 | Tier 1–2 |

# Appendix C — The AI roleplay prompt library

Copy these into a voice conversation with an AI assistant. Do the conversation, then read the feedback. Fifteen minutes each.

**Disagreeing with a senior technical person**

```
Act as my American engineering manager. We're discussing a machine-learning
project and I need to disagree with your proposal professionally.

Rules:
- Stay in character. Push back on me at least twice.
- Do NOT correct my English during the conversation.
- Afterwards, list every unnatural or non-native phrase I used and give me
  5 better corporate alternatives for each.
```

**Executive pressure test**

```
Act as a Vice President who has 3 minutes and no context on my project.
Interrupt me if I don't give you the answer in my first two sentences.
Ask "so what?" whenever I give you detail without implication.
After 10 minutes, tell me exactly where I lost you and rewrite my opening
in 2 sentences.
```

**Hostile Q&A after a presentation**

```
Act as a skeptical finance director. I've just presented an analysis.
Ask me 8 hard questions, including one about methodology, one about data
quality, and one where you assert something my data contradicts.
Don't accept vague answers. Afterwards, rate my answers 1-5 and give me
better phrasing for the three weakest.
```

**Scope negotiation**

```
Act as a demanding internal stakeholder. You want a full analysis in
two days. It genuinely needs two weeks. Do not accept a flat no.
Keep pressing. Afterwards, tell me whether I traded, caved, or refused,
and give me 5 trade phrases I should have used.
```

**Small talk cold start**

```
Act as a colleague from London I've never met. We're both early to a
video call and have 4 minutes before others join. Start the small talk.
Don't let the conversation die — but don't rescue me either.
Afterwards, point out where I gave dead-end answers and show me how to
give the other person something to work with.
```

**One-on-one feedback**

```
Act as an analyst on my team whose work quality has slipped for a month.
You're defensive at first. I need to give corrective feedback without
demotivating you. Afterwards, tell me whether I described behaviour or
character, and rewrite my hardest sentence three ways.
```

**Interview under pressure**

```
Act as a hiring manager for a senior analytics role. Ask me about a project
that failed, then a technical question outside my stack, then push me on
compensation. Don't be hostile, but don't help me either.
Afterwards, list my filler words and hedges with counts.
```

---

# Appendix D — Emergency phrases for the moments that go wrong

| What just happened | Say this |
|---|---|
| You blanked completely | "Give me one second — I want to phrase this properly." |
| You started a sentence badly | "Let me start that again." |
| You realize mid-sentence you're wrong | "Actually, let me correct myself — the right figure is X." |
| You didn't hear the question | "Sorry, I missed the last part — could you repeat that?" |
| You didn't understand the question | "I want to make sure I answer the right question — are you asking about A or B?" |
| Your audio cut out | "You dropped for a few seconds — could you go back to the last point?" |
| Someone talked over you | "I'd like to finish the thought, if that's alright." |
| You need to reclaim the floor | "Coming back to what I was saying — " |
| The meeting is going nowhere | "Can we agree on the next step and take the rest offline?" |
| You were asked something confidential | "I'm not the right person to answer that — let me point you to who is." |
| You disagree strongly but can't say so now | "I have a different view. Can we set up fifteen minutes on this?" |
| You're being blamed unfairly | "Let me lay out what actually happened, and then we can decide what to fix." |
| You made a real mistake | "That one's on me. Here's the impact, and here's what I'm doing about it." |
| You're out of your depth | "I don't have the background to answer that well. Let me bring in someone who does." |
| Time is up and you're not done | "We're at time. The one thing I need from you is X — the rest I'll send in writing." |

*End of book.*
